Exam PProbability

Exam P Study Manual

A syllabus-aligned review of General Probability, Univariate Random Variables, and Multivariate Random Variables, with worked examples and practice problems for the Society of Actuaries Probability Exam.

How to Use This Manual

This manual is organized into three parts that mirror the three official SOA learning-outcome topics for Exam P, weighted the same way the real exam is weighted: General Probability (23–30%), Univariate Random Variables (44–50%), and Multivariate Random Variables (23–30%). Each part is broken into short chapters, each chapter opens with the official learning outcomes it addresses, walks through the theory with worked examples, and closes with a practice problem set with full solutions.

Click any entry in the left-hand outline to jump straight to that section — the outline stays visible as you scroll so you can move between topics without losing your place. On a phone or narrow window, tap the ☰ Contents button in the top-left corner to open the outline.

Suggested order: work through the parts in sequence (I → II → III) since Part III builds on the distributions introduced in Part II, which in turn builds on the counting and conditioning tools from Part I. Attempt every practice problem before revealing its solution, and revisit the Formula & Distribution Reference (Appendix A) throughout — it is designed to be your final week’s cram sheet.

It is aligned to the SOA Exam P syllabus and to the chapter coverage of A First Course in Probability (Ross, 10th ed.) and Mathematical Statistics with Applications (Wackerly, Mendenhall & Scheaffer, 7th ed.). Exam Overview & Study Plan

The Probability Exam (Exam P) is a three-hour, 30-question multiple-choice exam administered as a computer-based test. Each question has five answer choices (A–E) and unanswered questions are scored as incorrect, so plan to answer every question — there is no penalty for a wrong guess beyond losing the credit you would have earned for a right one. An understanding of calculus (series, differentiation, integration) is assumed, along with basic familiarity with insurance terminology from "Risk and Insurance."

Syllabus Weights

General Probability 23–30% Part I
Univariate Random Variables 44–50% Part II
Multivariate Random Variables 23–30% Part III

Because Univariate Random Variables carries the largest weight on the actual exam, Part II of this manual is also the longest and contains the most worked examples — expect roughly half of your study time and half of the real exam's questions to come from that single part.

A Note on Insurance Vocabulary

Several Part II topics (deductibles, coinsurance, policy limits) use insurance-specific language that the syllabus assumes you already know from "Risk and Insurance." Briefly: a deductible d is the amount the policyholder pays out of pocket before the insurer pays anything; coinsurance α is the fraction of the remaining loss the insurer pays (with the policyholder retaining the rest); and a policy limit u caps the insurer's total payment. These combine into a single payment-amount random variable that Chapter II.8 develops in full.

Part I — General Probability (23–30%)

General Probability is the foundation on which every later topic in Exam P is built — random variables, distributions, and even the multivariate topics you'll meet later are all defined in terms of the set-theoretic and axiomatic machinery introduced here. Expect this material to appear both as standalone questions (Venn-diagram set problems, counting problems, Bayes' Theorem problems) and embedded inside harder questions on other topics, so mastering it thoroughly pays dividends across the entire exam.

1. Sets, Sample Spaces & Events

Learning Outcomes Covered: (a) Define set functions, Venn diagrams, sample space, and events. Define probability as a set function on a collection of events and state the basic axioms of probability.

1.1 The Sample Space

Every probability problem begins with an experiment — a roll of a die, a claims year for a policyholder, a blood test result. The sample space, denoted Ω (capital omega), is the set of all possible outcomes of that experiment. An outcome is a single, indivisible result — you cannot break it down further within the context of the problem. For a single fair die roll, Ω = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6}. For two coin flips, Ω = {HH, HT, TH, TT}. The sample space can be finite (as in these examples), countably infinite (e.g., the number of claims filed in a year, Ω = {0, 1, 2, 3, ...}), or uncountably infinite (e.g., the exact time until a claim is filed, Ω = [0, ∞)).

On the exam, the very first step in almost any word problem is to name your sample space and your events precisely, using set notation. Vague verbal reasoning ("well, some of them overlap...") is the single largest source of avoidable errors on General Probability questions. Writing A = {driver is age 16–20} before doing anything else costs you ten seconds and saves you from sign errors later.

1.2 Events

An event is any subset of the sample space — that is, any collection of outcomes we might care about. If A ⊂ Ω (read "A is a subset of Ω"), then A is an event. Events are usually denoted with capital letters: A, B, C, .... The event occurs if the outcome of the experiment is a member of that set. Two special events deserve names: the empty set ∅, which contains no outcomes and therefore never occurs (the "impossible event"), and the sample space Ω itself, which contains every outcome and therefore always occurs (the "certain event").

Events are frequently described in words rather than by listing outcomes ("the claim exceeds $1,000," "the driver is under 25"), and part of the skill tested on Exam P is translating that verbal description into precise set notation before any arithmetic begins. Set-builder notation is a compact way to do this: A = {ω ∈ Ω : condition on ω} is read "A is the set of all outcomes ω in Ω such that the condition holds." For example, if Ω is the set of claim amounts on a policy, we might write A = {x ∈ Ω : x > 1000} for "the claim exceeds $1,000." You will not usually need to write set-builder notation explicitly on the exam, but thinking in these terms helps you avoid the single biggest source of careless errors in word problems: silently assuming an event includes or excludes a boundary case (does "at least 4 claims" include exactly 4? yes — always check the wording for strict versus non-strict inequalities).

It is also worth noting, purely as background context you won't be tested on directly, that in continuous or infinite sample spaces not literally every subset of Ω is required to be an "event" in the fully rigorous measure-theoretic sense — mathematicians restrict attention to a well-behaved collection of subsets called a sigma-algebra. For every sample space you will encounter on this exam, this technicality never matters in practice: any set you can describe in a word problem is a legitimate event.

1.3 Set Operations and Venn Diagrams

Because events are sets, we combine and compare them using the standard operations of set theory. A Venn diagram — a rectangle representing Ω with overlapping circles representing events — is the standard visual tool for organizing these relationships, and you should sketch one on your scratch paper any time three or more events are involved.

The convention is always the same: draw a bounding rectangle first and label it Ω — this represents "everything that could possibly happen" and anchors the complement of any event as "the part of the rectangle outside its circle." Then draw one circle per event, overlapping circles whenever the events could plausibly occur together. With two events you get four regions (only-A, only-B, both, neither); with three events you get eight regions (including the all-three overlap in the very center). The standard technique for filling in a three-circle Venn diagram from percentages, used throughout this Part, is to work from the inside out: fill the center (triple overlap) first, then each pairwise region (pairwise total minus the triple overlap already placed), and finally each single-event region (that event's total minus everything already placed inside its circle). This order avoids the double-subtraction errors that are extremely easy to make if you try to fill the diagram from the outside in.

Table 1.1 — Core Set Operations
Operation Notation Meaning (in words)
Union A ∪ B outcomes in A, or B, or both
Intersection A ∩ B outcomes in both A and B
Complement A′ (also written Ac) outcomes in Ω but not in A
Difference A \ B outcomes in A but not in B; equals A ∩ B′
Subset A ⊂ B every outcome in A is also in B
Do not confuse A \ B with B \ A — set difference is not symmetric. A \ B removes B's outcomes from A; B \ A removes A's outcomes from B. In general these have different sizes and different probabilities. Also, resist the temptation to read "or" as exclusive-or: in probability, A ∪ B always includes the possibility that both A and B occur.

1.4 Mutually Exclusive and Exhaustive Events

Two events A and B are mutually exclusive (equivalently, disjoint) if they share no outcomes: A ∩ B = ∅. They cannot both occur on the same trial of the experiment. A collection of events is exhaustive if their union is the entire sample space — that is, at least one of them must occur. When a collection of events is both pairwise mutually exclusive and exhaustive, it is called a partition of the sample space: every outcome in Ω belongs to exactly one of the events in the collection. Partitions are the backbone of the Law of Total Probability and Bayes' Theorem in Chapter 7, so it is worth internalizing this idea now: a partition slices Ω into non-overlapping puzzle pieces that together reconstruct the whole.

PARTITION (DEFINITION){B1, B2, ..., Bn} partitions Ω if Bi ∩ Bj = ∅ for all i ≠ j, and B1 ∪ B2 ∪ ... ∪ Bn = Ω

Insurance applications are full of natural partitions: policyholders classified into non-overlapping age brackets, claims classified by exactly one cause, drivers classified into exactly one risk tier. Whenever a problem gives you percentages that are described as covering "all" members of a population and breaking them into categories with no double-membership, you should recognize a partition and immediately anticipate that the Law of Total Probability or Bayes' Theorem (Chapter 7) is likely to be the intended tool. Conversely, if you notice that percentages describing supposedly separate categories add up to more than 100%, that is a strong signal the categories are not mutually exclusive, and any subsequent calculation needs to account for overlap (Chapter 2's addition rule) rather than being added directly.

Example 1.5

An insurer classifies every claim it receives as arising from exactly one of three causes: collision, weather, or theft. Over the past year, 45% of claims were collision-related, 35% were weather-related, and the remainder were theft-related. Confirm that these three categories form a partition of the sample space of claims, and find the percentage of claims that were theft-related.

Show Solution

The phrase "exactly one of three causes" is the verbal signal that these categories are mutually exclusive (no claim is double-counted across causes), and since every claim must have some cause and only these three are listed, the categories are also exhaustive. Together, mutual exclusivity and exhaustiveness confirm the three causes partition the claims sample space.

Since the three probabilities must sum to 1 (Axiom 3 applied to a partition): P(theft) = 1 − 0.45 − 0.35 = 0.20, so 20% of claims were theft-related.

1.5 De Morgan's Laws

De Morgan's Laws relate complements to unions and intersections, and they are indispensable for simplifying "at least one" and "none of" statements — extremely common phrasings on the exam.

DE MORGAN'S LAWS(A ∪ B)′ = A′ ∩ B′      (A ∩ B)′ = A′ ∪ B′

In words: "not (A or B)" means "not A and not B," and "not (A and B)" means "not A or not B." This generalizes to any number of events: the complement of "at least one of A1, ..., An occurs" is "none of them occur," i.e., A1′ ∩ A2′ ∩ ... ∩ An′.

1.6 Probability as a Set Function

A set function is simply a rule that assigns a number to each set in some collection. Probability, P, is a set function that assigns to every event A a real number P(A) — interpreted as the long-run relative frequency, or the degree of belief, that A occurs. Not every set function is a legitimate probability measure; to qualify, P must satisfy three defining axioms, due to Kolmogorov, which we introduce here and use as the foundation for everything that follows in Chapter 2.

KOLMOGOROV AXIOMS (PREVIEW)1. P(A) ≥ 0 for every event A
2. P(Ω) = 1
3. If A1, A2, ... are pairwise mutually exclusive, then P(A1 ∪ A2 ∪ ...) = P(A1) + P(A2) + ...
Example 1.1

A fair six-sided die is rolled once, so Ω = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6}. Let A be the event that the roll is even, and B the event that the roll is at least 4. Find (a) A ∪ B, (b) A ∩ B, (c) A′, and (d) A \ B.

Show Solution

First write the events explicitly: A = {2, 4, 6} and B = {4, 5, 6}.

(a) A ∪ B = {2, 4, 5, 6} — every outcome in at least one of the two sets.
(b) A ∩ B = {4, 6} — outcomes common to both.
(c) A′ = {1, 3, 5} — every outcome in Ω not in A.
(d) A \ B = A ∩ B′ = {2} — the outcome 2 is even but not at least 4.

Example 1.2

A gym surveys its 200 members' equipment use. It finds that 120 members use cardio equipment, 90 use weight machines, and 50 use both. Using a Venn diagram, find the number of members who use neither type of equipment.

Show Solution

Let C = "uses cardio" and W = "uses weights," with |C| = 120, |W| = 90, |C ∩ W| = 50 (bars denote the count of members in a set). Sketching a two-circle Venn diagram, the overlap region gets 50, leaving 120 − 50 = 70 in "cardio only" and 90 − 50 = 40 in "weights only."

The number using at least one type of equipment is |C ∪ W| = 70 + 50 + 40 = 160 (equivalently, |C| + |W| − |C ∩ W| = 120 + 90 − 50 = 160). Since the survey covers all 200 members, the number using neither is 200 − 160 = 40 members.

Example 1.3

Let Ω = {1, 2, ..., 10}, A = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5}, and B = {4, 5, 6, 7, 8}. Verify De Morgan's Law (A ∪ B)′ = A′ ∩ B′ directly by listing elements.

Show Solution

Left side: A ∪ B = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8}, so (A ∪ B)′ = {9, 10}.

Right side: A′ = {6, 7, 8, 9, 10} and B′ = {1, 2, 3, 9, 10}, so A′ ∩ B′ = {9, 10}.

Both sides equal {9, 10}, confirming the law for this example. Notice how much faster the right side would be to compute if A and B each excluded only a couple of outcomes — that is exactly the situation ("at least one of several rare events") where De Morgan's Law saves real computation on the exam.

1.7 Probability as a Set Function, Revisited

Return now to the idea introduced in Section 1.6: probability is a rule, P, that eats an event (a set) and outputs a number in [0, 1]. This "function of sets, rather than of numbers" framing can feel unfamiliar at first, since most functions encountered in earlier mathematics coursework take a number as input (e.g., f(x) = x²). It helps to think of P as behaving analogously to a physical measurement — area, mass, length — applied to sets instead of physical regions. Just as the area of a shape assigns a non-negative number to each region and the areas of disjoint regions add together to give the area of their union, probability assigns a non-negative number to each event and the probabilities of disjoint (mutually exclusive) events add together to give the probability of their union. This "probability is like area" analogy is not just a teaching device — it is the same mathematical structure (a measure) that appears throughout advanced probability theory, and keeping the analogy in mind is often enough to correctly guess which set-theoretic manipulations are legitimate even before working through the axioms formally.

1.8 Notation Recap

Different textbooks (and different exam problems) use slightly different symbols for the same ideas. Table 1.2 collects the equivalent notations you may encounter so that a change in symbol on the actual exam doesn't cost you time.

Table 1.2 — Equivalent Notations You May See
Concept Notations you may see
Complement of A A′, Ac, ¬A
Intersection probability P(A ∩ B), P(AB), P(A and B)
Empty set ∅, {}
Subset (not necessarily proper) A ⊂ B, A ⊆ B
Conditional probability P(A | B), P(A given B)

This manual will consistently use A′ for complements and P(A ∩ B) (rather than the shorthand P(AB)) for joint probabilities, purely for clarity — but you should recognize all the alternatives instantly, since the exam's official notation can vary by problem.

Example 1.4

A sample space has exactly 4 outcomes: Ω = {a, b, c, d}. How many distinct events (subsets of Ω, including ∅ and Ω itself) can be formed?

Show Solution

Each of the 4 outcomes is either included in a given subset or excluded from it — an independent binary choice per outcome — so by the multiplication principle (previewed here, and formalized in Chapter 3) there are 2 × 2 × 2 × 2 = 24 = 16 possible events. These range from the empty set (include nothing) to Ω itself (include everything), with 14 proper, non-empty subsets in between — for instance {a}, {a,c}, {b,c,d}, and so on. In general, a sample space with n outcomes has exactly 2n possible events — the same 2n identity you'll meet again as a binomial coefficient sum in Chapter 3.

2. Axioms of Probability

Learning Outcomes Covered: (a) Define probability as a set function on a collection of events and state the basic axioms of probability.

2.1 The Three Axioms

Chapter 1 previewed the three Kolmogorov axioms; here we use them as building blocks to derive every other probability rule you'll need. Restated formally, for a sample space Ω and a collection of events:

KOLMOGOROV AXIOMSAxiom 1: P(A) ≥ 0 for every event A.
Axiom 2: P(Ω) = 1.
Axiom 3 (Countable Additivity): If A1, A2, A3, ... are pairwise mutually exclusive events, then
P(A1 ∪ A2 ∪ A3 ∪ ...) = P(A1) + P(A2) + P(A3) + ...

Everything else — every formula you will use on the exam for computing probabilities — is logically derivable from these three statements. That is what makes them axioms: they are assumed, not proven, and every other true statement about probability follows from them by ordinary logic and algebra. Let's derive the results you'll actually use.

It's worth pausing on why these particular three statements were chosen. Historically, probability was understood informally as either a long-run relative frequency (flip a coin a million times and count the fraction of heads) or as a degree of belief (how confident are you that it will rain tomorrow). Both interpretations agree that a probability should be a non-negative number (Axiom 1 — negative frequencies or negative beliefs make no sense), that the certain event should get the maximum possible value, conventionally 1 (Axiom 2 — this is really just a choice of scale, analogous to choosing to measure temperature in a particular unit), and that probabilities of non-overlapping events should stack by simple addition (Axiom 3 — if 30% of outcomes fall in A and a disjoint 20% fall in B, then 50% of outcomes fall in "A or B," matching how relative frequencies behave). Kolmogorov's insight in 1933 was that these three simple, intuitively obvious requirements are enough to build the entire mathematical theory of probability rigorously — you never need to appeal to "frequency" or "belief" again once the axioms are laid down. For exam purposes, the practical payoff is that any formula you can derive from the axioms is guaranteed valid, and any shortcut that cannot be derived from them is not a legitimate probability rule, no matter how intuitive it seems.

2.2 Derived Property: The Probability of the Empty Set

A useful warm-up derivation: since Ω and ∅ are mutually exclusive (they share no outcomes) and Ω ∪ ∅ = Ω, Axiom 3 gives P(Ω) = P(Ω) + P(∅). Combined with Axiom 2, this forces P(∅) = 0 — the impossible event has probability zero (though, in continuous settings later in the exam, the converse is not always true: probability-zero events are not necessarily impossible).

2.3 The Complement Rule

Since A and A′ are mutually exclusive and their union is all of Ω, Axiom 3 gives P(A) + P(A′) = P(Ω) = 1. Rearranged, this is the single most-used identity on the exam:

COMPLEMENT RULEP(A′) = 1 − P(A)
Any time a problem asks for the probability of "at least one," "at least two more than," or "none of," your fastest path is almost always to compute the probability of the complementary (opposite) event and subtract from 1 — especially in counting and Bayes problems where the complement is a single simple case and the direct event requires summing many cases.

2.4 Monotonicity

If A ⊂ B, then B can be split into the disjoint pieces A and B \ A, so P(B) = P(A) + P(B \ A). Since P(B \ A) ≥ 0 by Axiom 1, we get P(A) ≤ P(B) — probability never decreases as a set grows. A direct consequence: for any event A, 0 ≤ P(A) ≤ 1, since ∅ ⊂ A ⊂ Ω.

2.5 The Addition Rule (Inclusion–Exclusion)

Axiom 3 only applies directly to mutually exclusive events. When A and B overlap, we must correct for double-counting the intersection. This is the general addition rule, sometimes called the inclusion–exclusion principle for two events:

ADDITION RULE (TWO EVENTS)P(A ∪ B) = P(A) + P(B) − P(A ∩ B)

Why it works: writing P(A) and P(B) separately counts every outcome in A ∩ B twice — once as part of A, once as part of B. Subtracting P(A ∩ B) once corrects the double count. When A and B are mutually exclusive, P(A ∩ B) = 0 and the formula collapses to simple addition, matching Axiom 3.

The same idea extends to three events, and this version is heavily tested because it requires careful bookkeeping:

ADDITION RULE (THREE EVENTS)P(A ∪ B ∪ C) = P(A) + P(B) + P(C) − P(A ∩ B) − P(A ∩ C) − P(B ∩ C) + P(A ∩ B ∩ C)

The pattern — add the singles, subtract the pairs, add back the triple — is inclusion–exclusion, and it continues (alternating sign) for any number of events, though the exam rarely goes beyond three.

A Formal Derivation of the Two-Event Addition Rule

It's instructive to see the two-event addition rule proven rigorously from the axioms rather than just argued intuitively, since this is the template for how every "derived property" in this manual is actually justified. The trick is to rewrite A ∪ B as a union of three pairwise mutually exclusive pieces: the part of A outside B, the part of B outside A, and the overlap. Formally, A ∪ B = (A ∩ B′) ∪ (A ∩ B) ∪ (A′ ∩ B), and these three sets are pairwise disjoint by construction. Axiom 3 then gives:

P(A ∪ B) = P(A ∩ B′) + P(A ∩ B) + P(A′ ∩ B)

Now observe that A itself splits into the disjoint pieces A ∩ B′ and A ∩ B, so P(A) = P(A ∩ B′) + P(A ∩ B), i.e., P(A ∩ B′) = P(A) − P(A ∩ B). Similarly P(A′ ∩ B) = P(B) − P(A ∩ B). Substituting both back in:

P(A ∪ B) = [P(A) − P(A∩B)] + P(A∩B) + [P(B) − P(A∩B)] = P(A) + P(B) − P(A∩B) ∎

Every other "rule" in this chapter — monotonicity, the complement rule, the three-event addition formula — can be produced by the same style of argument: split the set of interest into pairwise disjoint pieces, apply Axiom 3, and solve algebraically. You are not expected to reproduce these derivations from scratch on exam day, but understanding why the formulas are true (rather than memorizing them as arbitrary facts) makes them far more resistant to being forgotten or misapplied under time pressure.

A useful bonus fact that occasionally appears as a shortcut: since P(A ∩ B) = P(A)+P(B)-P(A∪B) and P(A∪B) ≤ 1, it follows that P(A ∩ B) ≥ P(A) + P(B) − 1. This is a simple case of what's known more generally as a Bonferroni-type inequality — a quick lower bound on an intersection probability using only the individual probabilities, useful when you don't have enough information to compute P(A∩B) exactly but need to bound it.

Extending Monotonicity: Sandwiching an Unknown Probability

Combining monotonicity (Section 2.4) with the addition rule gives you a general-purpose technique for problems that don't supply quite enough information to pin down a probability exactly, but do let you bound it between two values — a style of question the exam occasionally uses to test conceptual understanding rather than pure calculation. For instance, since A ∩ B ⊂ A and A ∩ B ⊂ B, monotonicity immediately gives P(A ∩ B) ≤ min(P(A), P(B)) — an upper bound obtained with no additional information at all. Paired with the Bonferroni-style lower bound above, you can sandwich an unknown intersection probability between two computable numbers: max(0, P(A)+P(B)−1) ≤ P(A∩B) ≤ min(P(A), P(B)). Similarly, since A ⊂ A ∪ B and B ⊂ A ∪ B, we get P(A∪B) ≥ max(P(A), P(B)), combined with the trivial upper bound P(A∪B) ≤ 1. These bounding techniques rarely replace an exact calculation when enough data is given, but they are a fast way to eliminate implausible answer choices on a multiple-choice question, or to double-check that a computed exact answer at least falls within the range that logic alone guarantees.

2.6 The Equally Likely Outcomes Model

When a sample space has a finite number of outcomes, N, and by the nature of the experiment each outcome is equally likely to occur (a fair die, a well-shuffled deck, a random draw from an urn), Axiom 3 implies each outcome has probability 1/N. Then for any event A consisting of |A| outcomes:

CLASSICAL (EQUALLY LIKELY) PROBABILITYP(A) = |A| / |Ω| = (number of outcomes favorable to A) / (total number of outcomes)

This innocuous-looking formula is precisely why Chapter 3's counting techniques matter so much for the exam: the entire difficulty in an equally-likely-outcomes problem is usually in correctly counting |A| and |Ω|, not in the probability theory itself. In fact, historically, this "classical" definition of probability — proposed well before Kolmogorov's axioms — was the original way probability was defined at all, and it's the reason gambling problems involving dice and cards dominate the early history of the subject. The axiomatic approach in this chapter is more general (it also handles situations, like a biased coin or a skewed claim-size distribution, where outcomes are not equally likely), but whenever the equally-likely assumption legitimately applies, it reduces every probability question to a counting question — which is exactly the bridge to Chapter 3.

The equally likely outcomes model applies only when the problem structure guarantees each outcome has the same probability. "Randomly selected" language is a strong hint, but read carefully — many exam problems describe non-uniform probabilities (e.g., different claim probabilities by age group) precisely to prevent you from applying this shortcut where it doesn't belong.

A useful contrast: consider a loaded (biased) six-sided die versus a fair one. Both have the same sample space, Ω = {1,2,3,4,5,6}, and both are still governed by the same three Kolmogorov axioms — P must still be non-negative, sum to 1 over Ω, and be additive over disjoint events. What differs is only that a loaded die assigns unequal probabilities to the six outcomes (say, P(6) = 0.30 while the other five faces share the remaining 0.70 unequally), so the shortcut P(A) = |A|/|Ω| is simply invalid — you must instead sum the individual outcome probabilities that make up A. This is precisely why the classical (equally likely) model is best thought of as a special case of the general axiomatic theory, not a replacement for it: the axioms always apply, but the convenient counting shortcut only applies when the extra assumption of uniform likelihood is justified by the problem.

Example 2.1

Using only the three axioms (not the complement rule as a given), prove that for any event A, P(A′) = 1 − P(A).

Show Solution

Step 1: Note that A and A′ are mutually exclusive, since an outcome cannot simultaneously be in A and not in A. So A ∩ A′ = ∅.

Step 2: Note that A ∪ A′ = Ω, since every outcome in the sample space either belongs to A or does not.

Step 3: Apply Axiom 3 (countable additivity) to the mutually exclusive pair A, A′: P(A ∪ A′) = P(A) + P(A′).

Step 4: The left side equals P(Ω), which is 1 by Axiom 2. So 1 = P(A) + P(A′), i.e., P(A′) = 1 − P(A). ∎

Example 2.2

Among students in a class, 60% are taking a math course and 50% are taking a science course. 30% are taking both. Find the probability a randomly selected student is taking math or science.

Show Solution

Let M = "taking math," S = "taking science." We're given P(M) = 0.6, P(S) = 0.5, P(M ∩ S) = 0.3. By the addition rule:

P(M ∪ S) = P(M) + P(S) − P(M ∩ S) = 0.6 + 0.5 − 0.3 = 0.8

Example 2.3

A gym surveys its cross-training members and finds: 30% run, 25% cycle, 20% swim, 12% run and cycle, 10% cycle and swim, 8% run and swim, and 5% do all three activities. Find the percentage of members who do none of the three activities.

Show Solution

Let R, C, S denote running, cycling, and swimming. By the three-event addition rule:

P(R ∪ C ∪ S) = P(R) + P(C) + P(S) − P(R∩C) − P(C∩S) − P(R∩S) + P(R∩C∩S)

= 0.30 + 0.25 + 0.20 − 0.12 − 0.10 − 0.08 + 0.05 = 0.50

So 50% do at least one of the three activities, and by the complement rule, the percentage doing none is 1 − 0.50 = 50%. A quick sanity check with a three-circle Venn diagram (fill in the triple-overlap of 5% first, then work outward to the pairwise regions, then the singles) confirms every region is non-negative, which is always worth checking on a three-event problem.

Example 2.4

A single card is drawn from a well-shuffled standard 52-card deck. Find the probability the card is a face card (jack, queen, or king) or a heart.

Show Solution

This is an equally-likely-outcomes problem with |Ω| = 52. Let F = face card, |F| = 12 (3 face cards × 4 suits). Let H = heart, |H| = 13. The overlap F ∩ H = face cards that are hearts = {J♥, Q♥, K♥}, so |F ∩ H| = 3.

P(F ∪ H) = 12/52 + 13/52 − 3/52 = 22/52 = 11/26 ≈ 0.423

2.7 Probability and Long-Run Frequency

Although the axioms are stated abstractly with no reference to repeated trials, one of the most important theorems built on top of them — the Law of Large Numbers, which you will meet formally later in the syllabus once random variables are introduced — guarantees that if an experiment is repeated many times independently, the observed proportion of trials on which event A occurs converges to P(A). This is what ties the abstract axiomatic machinery back to the everyday, intuitive meaning of probability as "long-run relative frequency": it is not assumed as part of the definition, but it is a consequence that can be proven from the axioms (together with the notion of independent repeated trials from Chapter 5). For exam purposes, the practical takeaway is that every probability you compute in this Part can be interpreted concretely: a computed value of P(A) = 0.32 means that, if the underlying experiment were repeated a very large number of times under identical conditions, event A would occur on approximately 32% of those repetitions. This interpretation is a useful final sanity check on any answer — if your computed probability doesn't make sense as a plausible long-run frequency (for instance, a negative number, or a number above 1), you have made an error somewhere upstream.

Example 2.5

Two events A and B satisfy P(A) = 0.65 and P(A ∪ B) = 0.65. What can you conclude about the relationship between A and B, and what is P(A ∩ B) in terms of P(B)?

Show Solution

By monotonicity (Section 2.4), since A ⊂ A ∪ B always, we know P(A) ≤ P(A ∪ B) in general. Here they're equal: P(A) = P(A ∪ B) = 0.65. This forces P(B \ A) = P(A∪B) − P(A) = 0 — meaning B contributes no probability outside of A. In fact this is only possible if B ⊂ A (at least up to a set of probability zero), which in turn forces A ∩ B = B, so P(A ∩ B) = P(B). This problem is a good conceptual check that you understand monotonicity as more than an abstract inequality — an equality case in monotonicity always signals a subset relationship "hiding" in the given probabilities.

3. Counting Methods

Learning Outcomes Covered: (b) Calculate probabilities using combinatorics, such as combinations and permutations.

3.1 The Multiplication Principle

The multiplication principle is the single most fundamental counting tool, and every other formula in this chapter is really just a shortcut derived from applying it repeatedly. It states: if a task consists of a sequence of k choices, and the first choice can be made in n1 ways, the second in n2 ways (regardless of the first choice), ..., and the k-th in nk ways, then the total number of ways to complete the whole task is the product.

MULTIPLICATION PRINCIPLETotal ways = n1 × n2 × ... × nk

3.2 Permutations (Order Matters)

A permutation is an ordered arrangement of objects. If we choose r objects, in order, from a group of n distinguishable objects, without replacement (no repeats), the count follows directly from the multiplication principle: n choices for the first position, n − 1 for the second (one object is used up), and so on down to n − r + 1 for the r-th position.

PERMUTATIONS (WITHOUT REPETITION)nPr = n(n − 1)(n − 2)⋯(n − r + 1) = n! / (n − r)!

The special case r = n gives nPn = n! — the number of ways to arrange all n objects in a row.

If repetition is allowed (each position can reuse any of the n objects, as with digits in a PIN code), the multiplication principle directly gives nr arrangements — no factorial needed, since nothing is "used up."

Permutations with Indistinguishable Objects

If some of the n objects being arranged in a row are identical to each other (e.g., arranging the letters of a word with repeated letters), then swapping two identical objects produces an arrangement that looks the same, so the naive count of n! overcounts. If there are k groups of indistinguishable objects, of sizes n1, n2, ..., nk (with n1 + n2 + ... + nk = n), we divide out the redundant orderings within each group:

DISTINGUISHABLE PERMUTATIONSn! / (n1! n2! ⋯ nk!)

3.3 Combinations (Order Doesn't Matter)

A combination counts the number of ways to choose r objects from n, where order does not matter — we only care which objects are selected, not the sequence in which we picked them. Since each unordered group of r objects corresponds to exactly r! different ordered permutations of that same group, we take the permutation count and divide by r! to eliminate the redundant orderings:

COMBINATIONS (BINOMIAL COEFFICIENT)nCr = (nr) = n! / (r!(n − r)!) = nPr / r!

The quantity nCr is also called the binomial coefficient because it appears as the coefficient of xryn−r in the expansion of (x + y)n — a connection that resurfaces when you study the binomial distribution later in the syllabus. Two identities are worth memorizing because they occasionally shortcut algebra on the exam:

BINOMIAL COEFFICIENT IDENTITIESSymmetry: nCr = nCn−r
Pascal's Rule: nCr = n−1Cr−1 + n−1Cr
Sum over all r: nC0 + nC1 + ... + nCn = 2n

The symmetry identity has an intuitive reading: choosing r objects to include is equivalent to choosing n − r objects to exclude, so the two counts must match. The "sum over all r" identity says that the total number of subsets of an n-element set (including the empty set and the whole set) is 2n, since every subset is determined by choosing some number of elements to include.

3.4 Partitioning into Multiple Groups: The Multinomial Coefficient

Combinations split n objects into exactly two groups: "chosen" (r objects) and "not chosen" (n − r objects). Often we need to split n distinguishable objects into more than two labeled groups of specified sizes — for instance, dividing employees into three named project teams. If the groups have sizes n1, n2, ..., nk summing to n, the count is the multinomial coefficient, which (not coincidentally) has exactly the same formula as the distinguishable-permutations count from Section 3.2 — both problems are really "how many distinguishable ways can I sort n labeled items into slots":

MULTINOMIAL COEFFICIENTn! / (n1! n2! ⋯ nk!)   where   n1 + n2 + ... + nk = n

3.5 Choosing the Right Tool

Most counting mistakes on the exam come from misidentifying which tool applies, not from arithmetic errors. Ask yourself two questions: does order matter? and is repetition (replacement) allowed? Table 3.1 summarizes the decision.

Table 3.1 — Which Counting Tool to Use
Order matters? Repetition allowed? Tool Formula
Yes No Permutation nPr = n!/(n−r)!
Yes Yes Multiplication principle nr
No No Combination nCr = n!/(r!(n−r)!)
No (2+ labeled groups) No Multinomial coefficient n!/(n1!⋯nk!)
N/A (arranging with repeats) Objects repeat Distinguishable permutations n!/(n1!⋯nk!)
A fast diagnostic: if swapping two selected items or two chosen people would create a "new" outcome the problem cares about (1st place vs. 2nd place, president vs. secretary), order matters — use permutations. If swapping them changes nothing about the outcome (both are just "on the committee"), order doesn't matter — use combinations.

Side-by-Side Worked Comparison

To make the permutation-versus-combination distinction completely concrete, consider the identical scenario — choosing 2 objects from a group of 5 labeled objects {P, Q, R, S, T} — worked both ways.

Table 3.2 — Same n, r; Different Question, Different Count
Question Tool Listed outcomes (partial) Count
How many ordered pairs (1st choice, 2nd choice)? Permutation, 5P2 PQ, QP, PR, RP, PS, SP, ... (PQ and QP both counted, as different outcomes) 5×4 = 20
How many unordered pairs (just "which two")? Combination, 5C2 PQ, PR, PS, PT, QR, ... (PQ and QP counted as the same outcome) 20/2! = 10

Every permutation count is related to the corresponding combination count by exactly this factor of r! — the number of ways to order the r chosen objects among themselves — which is precisely the algebraic relationship nCr = nPr/r! stated in Section 3.3. Keeping this simple 5-choose-2 example in mind is often enough to break a mental block when a harder word problem leaves you unsure which formula applies: mentally substitute a tiny, listable case like this one, check whether order matters by asking whether swapping the roles of two chosen elements changes the outcome you listed, and then scale the same logic up to the problem's actual numbers.

A Note on "At Least" Counting Problems

A very common exam pattern asks for the number of arrangements or the probability of an event described with "at least" language — at least one defective item, at least two women on a committee, at least one matching pair. Counting these cases directly usually means summing several separate combinatorial terms (exactly one, exactly two, exactly three, ...), which is slow and error-prone. It is almost always faster to count the complementary event ("none," which is usually a single clean case) and subtract from the total, exactly as recommended for probability in the Section 2.3 callout. This complement trick is arguably used more often in counting problems than anywhere else on the exam, precisely because "at least" scenarios are so common in insurance contexts (at least one claim, at least one policyholder in a group having some characteristic).

Bonus: Circular Arrangements

A related counting variant worth recognizing (occasionally tested, and useful for building comfort with the "why does this formula look the way it does" style of reasoning) is arranging n distinguishable objects around a circle, where arrangements that are rotations of one another are considered the same. Lining n objects up in a row gives n! arrangements, but on a circle, each distinct circular arrangement can be "cut open" at any of its n positions to produce n different linear arrangements that are really rotations of the same circular one. Dividing out this redundancy gives:

CIRCULAR PERMUTATIONS(n − 1)!

For example, seating 5 distinguishable guests around a circular table, where only relative order matters (rotating everyone one seat to the left produces "the same" seating arrangement), gives (5−1)! = 4! = 24 distinct arrangements rather than the 5! = 120 you'd get for seating them in a row. This kind of formula rarely appears directly on Exam P, but it is a good test of whether you truly understand why the permutation formula divides out redundant orderings, rather than having simply memorized n!/(n−r)! as a rule.

3.6 Bonus: Combinations with Repetition

All of the combination and permutation formulas above assume you are selecting from distinguishable objects without reusing any of them (aside from the explicit "with repetition" multiplication-principle case, where positions — not objects — are being filled independently). A different, less commonly tested but occasionally useful counting problem asks: in how many ways can you choose r items from n types of object, where repetition of a type is allowed and order does not matter (e.g., choosing a dozen donuts from 5 available flavors, where you may pick multiple of the same flavor)? This is solved by a technique known as "stars and bars," and it produces a combination formula with repetition baked in:

COMBINATIONS WITH REPETITION (STARS AND BARS)n+r−1Cr = (n + r − 1)! / (r!(n − 1)!)

This formula appears only occasionally on Exam P (it is far more central to later exams covering compound distributions), but recognizing the setup — unordered selection with repetition allowed — will keep you from incorrectly forcing such a problem into the ordinary combination or permutation formula, which assume no repeats.

Example 3.1

A license plate consists of 3 letters followed by 4 digits. Letters and digits may repeat. How many distinct license plates are possible?

Show Solution

This is a straightforward multiplication-principle problem: 7 independent positions, each with a fixed number of choices (26 for each letter position, 10 for each digit position), and repetition is allowed.

26 × 26 × 26 × 10 × 10 × 10 × 10 = 263 × 104 = 17,576 × 10,000 = 175,760,000

Example 3.2

From a board of 10 members, a president, a secretary, and a treasurer are to be chosen (one person per role, no one holding two roles). How many ways can this be done?

Show Solution

Order matters here — the same three people arranged into different roles are different outcomes — and no repetition is allowed (each person fills at most one role). This is a permutation of 3 objects chosen from 10:

10P3 = 10 × 9 × 8 = 720

Example 3.3

How many distinct arrangements are there of the letters in the word BANANA?

Show Solution

BANANA has 6 letters total: B appears once, A appears three times, and N appears twice. Because the repeated letters are indistinguishable from one another, we use the distinguishable-permutations formula:

6! / (1! 3! 2!) = 720 / (1 × 6 × 2) = 720/12 = 60

Example 3.4

A committee of 4 people is to be selected from a group of 12 people, with no distinction of role among committee members. How many different committees are possible?

Show Solution

Order doesn't matter (all 4 committee members have the same status), and there's no repetition (each person can serve at most once). This is a combination:

12C4 = 12! / (4! 8!) = (12 × 11 × 10 × 9) / (4 × 3 × 2 × 1) = 11,880/24 = 495

Example 3.5

A committee of 5 people is to be formed from a pool of 7 men and 5 women, with the requirement that exactly 2 women serve on the committee. How many such committees are possible?

Show Solution

Break the task into two independent combination sub-choices linked by the multiplication principle: choose 2 women from 5, and choose the remaining 5 − 2 = 3 members (all men) from 7.

5C2 × 7C3 = 10 × 35 = 350

Example 3.6

A company must split 12 employees into three project teams — Team A, Team B, and Team C — with exactly 4 employees on each team. How many ways can this assignment be made?

Show Solution

The teams are labeled (distinguishable), and each employee ends up on exactly one team, so this is a direct multinomial-coefficient computation with n = 12 and group sizes n1 = n2 = n3 = 4:

12! / (4! 4! 4!) = 479,001,600 / (24 × 24 × 24) = 479,001,600 / 13,824 = 34,650

Equivalently, you can build this up with sequential combinations: choose 4 of 12 for Team A (12C4 = 495), then 4 of the remaining 8 for Team B (8C4 = 70), then the last 4 automatically form Team C. 495 × 70 = 34,650 — the same answer, since the multinomial coefficient is exactly this chain of combinations collapsed into one formula.

Example 3.7

A 5-card poker hand is dealt from a standard 52-card deck. Find the probability that the hand contains exactly 2 aces.

Show Solution

This combines counting with the equally-likely-outcomes model from Chapter 2. The sample space is all possible 5-card hands: |Ω| = 52C5 = 2,598,960.

To count hands with exactly 2 aces: choose 2 of the 4 aces (4C2 = 6), and choose the remaining 3 cards from the 48 non-aces (48C3 = 17,296). By the multiplication principle, the number of favorable hands is 6 × 17,296 = 103,776.

P(exactly 2 aces) = 103,776 / 2,598,960 ≈ 0.0399 (about 4%).

Example 3.8

A quality-control inspector samples 5 items without replacement from a shipment of 40, of which 6 are defective. Find the probability the sample contains at least one defective item.

Show Solution

Directly counting "exactly 1 defective," "exactly 2 defective," ..., "exactly 5 defective" and adding them would require five separate combination calculations. Instead, apply the complement trick from Section 3.5: the only way to have zero defectives is if all 5 sampled items come from the 40 − 6 = 34 non-defective items.

Sample space: 40C5 = 658,008. Favorable outcomes for "zero defective": 34C5 = 278,256.

P(zero defective) = 278,256 / 658,008 ≈ 0.4229

P(at least one defective) = 1 − 0.4229 ≈ 0.5771

Computing this directly (summing the probabilities of exactly 1 through exactly 5 defectives) would require five separate numerator calculations and give the identical answer — the complement approach here is roughly five times less arithmetic for the same result.

4. Conditional Probability

Learning Outcomes Covered: (f) Define and calculate conditional probabilities.

4.1 Definition

Conditional probability answers the question: "given that I already know event B occurred, how does that change the probability I assign to event A?" Learning that B occurred is new information, and it should update our probabilities — sometimes dramatically. The formal definition:

CONDITIONAL PROBABILITYP(A | B) = P(A ∩ B) / P(B),   provided P(B) > 0

Read P(A | B) as "the probability of A given B." Note carefully that P(A | B) and P(B | A) are generally not equal — they answer different questions and are computed by dividing the same numerator, P(A ∩ B), by two different denominators.

Confusing P(A|B) with P(B|A) is one of the most common conceptual errors on this exam (it even has a name outside actuarial contexts: the "prosecutor's fallacy"). The probability that a random disease-carrier tests positive is not the same number as the probability that a random person who tests positive actually carries the disease — these can differ by orders of magnitude, as you'll see in the Bayes' Theorem examples in Chapter 7.

It also helps to notice what conditional probability is not doing: it is not changing the underlying experiment or somehow altering event A itself. P(A|B) is still an ordinary probability (it satisfies all three Kolmogorov axioms, treated as a set function of A with B held fixed) — it simply measures A's likelihood under the extra piece of information that B is known to have occurred. This is why, for a fixed conditioning event B, conditional probabilities still obey every rule from Chapters 1–2: P(A′|B) = 1 − P(A|B), P(A1∪A2|B) = P(A1|B)+P(A2|B) when A1, A2 are mutually exclusive, and so on — you are simply doing ordinary probability inside the smaller, "conditioned-on-B" universe.

4.2 The Reduced Sample Space Interpretation

There is a very useful way to picture conditional probability: once we're told that B has occurred, we effectively shrink the sample space from Ω down to B itself — B becomes the new "universe," since we know for certain we're somewhere inside it. Within this reduced sample space, the only way for A to also occur is via the outcomes in A ∩ B. So P(A|B) is just the (renormalized) fraction of the new, smaller universe B that also belongs to A — which is exactly the ratio P(A ∩ B)/P(B) in the definition. This picture is especially powerful for equally-likely-outcomes problems: P(A|B) = |A ∩ B| / |B|, a simple count of outcomes within the reduced space.

4.3 The Multiplication Rule (Derived)

Multiplying both sides of the definition by P(B) gives an equally important identity, used constantly for computing the probability that two events happen in sequence:

MULTIPLICATION RULEP(A ∩ B) = P(B) · P(A | B) = P(A) · P(B | A)

This generalizes to chains of more than two events by repeated conditioning — condition each new event on everything that came before it:

Look ahead briefly to Chapter 5: independence is really just the special case of the multiplication rule where conditioning turns out not to matter, i.e., where P(B|A) = P(B). Every independent-events multiplication problem is secretly a conditional-probability problem in which the condition happens to have no effect — which is exactly why independence is defined the way it is, and why it is introduced immediately after conditional probability in this manual's sequence rather than earlier.
CHAIN RULE (GENERAL MULTIPLICATION RULE)P(A ∩ B ∩ C) = P(A) · P(B | A) · P(C | A ∩ B)

This is exactly how you compute probabilities for sequential draws without replacement: the probability of drawing three specific-colored balls in a row from an urn is the product of a chain of conditional probabilities, each computed on the "reduced" urn remaining after the previous draws.

Example 4.1

Two fair six-sided dice are rolled. Given that the first die shows a 5, find the probability that the sum of the two dice is 8.

Show Solution

Let A = "sum is 8" and B = "first die shows 5." We want P(A|B). Using the reduced sample space idea: given B, the only possible outcomes are (5,1), (5,2), (5,3), (5,4), (5,5), (5,6) — six equally likely outcomes. Among these, only (5,3) gives a sum of 8.

P(A | B) = 1/6 (You can verify this with the formula directly: P(A∩B) = 1/36, P(B) = 6/36, so P(A|B) = (1/36)/(6/36) = 1/6.)

Example 4.2

A researcher reviews the medical records of 800 men and finds that 150 died of causes related to heart disease. Of the 800 men, 250 had at least one parent who suffered from heart disease, and of those 250 men, 80 died of causes related to heart disease. Find the probability that a randomly selected man from this group died of heart-disease-related causes, given that neither of his parents suffered from heart disease.

Show Solution

Let D = "died of heart-disease-related causes" and N = "neither parent had heart disease." We're told |N′| = 250 (at least one parent affected) and |D ∩ N′| = 80. So |N| = 800 − 250 = 550, and by subtraction, |D ∩ N| = 150 − 80 = 70 (the total who died, minus those who died and had an affected parent).

Using the reduced sample space interpretation with N as the new universe:

P(D | N) = |D ∩ N| / |N| = 70/550 = 7/55 ≈ 0.127

Example 4.3

An urn contains 8 red balls and 5 blue balls. Three balls are drawn without replacement. Find the probability that all three balls drawn are red.

Show Solution

Apply the chain rule, conditioning each draw on the outcomes of the previous draws (the urn composition shrinks each time a ball is removed):

P(R1 ∩ R2 ∩ R3) = P(R1) · P(R2 | R1) · P(R3 | R1∩R2) = (8/13)(7/12)(6/11)

= 336/1716 = 28/143 ≈ 0.196

Notice each successive fraction has both numerator and denominator one smaller than the previous — one fewer red ball and one fewer total ball, since the previous draw is known to have removed a red one.

A frequent variation on this style of problem asks for the probability of drawing a specific color at a particular position in the sequence without specifying the colors of the other draws — for instance, "the probability the third ball drawn is red," with no constraint on the first two. A common mistake is to assume the third draw is somehow different from the first because of when it happens. In fact, for draws without replacement from a well-mixed urn, every position in the sequence has the identical marginal probability of being red, namely (number of red)/(total) — the same as the very first draw. This can be proven with the Law of Total Probability from Chapter 7 (averaging over all the possible colors of the earlier draws), but it is worth remembering as a standalone fact: symmetry across draw position is a legitimate and exam-tested shortcut, not an approximation.
Example 4.4

Of 500 shoppers at a store, 200 bought a shirt, 150 bought pants, and 60 bought both. Given that a randomly selected shopper bought a shirt, find the probability that shopper also bought pants.

Show Solution

Let T = "bought pants (trousers)" and S = "bought a shirt." We want P(T | S). Using counts directly in the reduced sample space (S, with 200 shoppers):

P(T | S) = |T ∩ S| / |S| = 60/200 = 0.30

Example 4.5

A hand of 2 cards is dealt from a standard 52-card deck. Given that both cards are face cards (jack, queen, or king), find the probability that both cards are the same suit.

Show Solution

This combines the reduced-sample-space idea with the counting tools from Chapter 3. There are 12 face cards total (3 ranks × 4 suits), so the reduced sample space B = "both cards are face cards" has |B| = 12C2 = 66 equally likely 2-card hands.

Within that reduced space, "both the same suit" (event A) requires choosing 1 suit (4 ways) and then 2 of the 3 face cards in that suit (3C2 = 3 ways): |A ∩ B| = 4 × 3 = 12.

P(A | B) = |A∩B| / |B| = 12/66 = 2/11 ≈ 0.182

Example 4.6

A batch of 15 components contains 4 that are defective. Three components are selected at random, one at a time, without replacement. Find the probability that the first is defective, the second is not defective, and the third is defective.

Show Solution

This is a direct chain-rule (general multiplication rule) application, with the composition of the batch updating after each draw. Let D1, G2, D3 denote "1st defective," "2nd good," "3rd defective."

P(D1) = 4/15 (4 defective out of 15 total).

P(G2|D1) = 11/14 (after removing one defective unit, 11 good units remain out of 14 total).

P(D3|D1∩G2) = 3/13 (3 defective units remain out of 13 total, since one defective and one good unit have already been removed).

P(D1 ∩ G2 ∩ D3) = (4/15)(11/14)(3/13) = 132/2730 = 22/455 ≈ 0.0484

Notice that at each stage, both the numerator (count remaining of the type of interest) and the denominator (total units remaining) shrink by exactly the amount removed in the previous step — this bookkeeping discipline, tracking exactly what has been removed so far, is the entire difficulty in chain-rule problems of this type.

5. Independence of Events

Learning Outcomes Covered: (c) Define independence and calculate probabilities of independent events.

5.1 Definition

Two events A and B are independent if learning that one occurred provides no information about — does not change the probability of — the other. Formally, using the conditional probability definition, independence means P(A | B) = P(A) (equivalently P(B | A) = P(B)). Substituting into the multiplication rule from Chapter 4 gives the standard working definition, which has the advantage of not requiring P(B) > 0:

INDEPENDENCE (TWO EVENTS)A and B are independent  ⟺  P(A ∩ B) = P(A) · P(B)

This is both a definition and a test: given any two events with known probabilities, you can check independence by comparing P(A ∩ B) to the product P(A)P(B). If they match, the events are independent; if not, they are dependent.

5.2 Mutual Independence of Three or More Events

For three (or more) events, independence is more subtle than checking pairs. Events A, B, C are mutually independent if every pair is independent and the joint probability factors as well:

MUTUAL INDEPENDENCE (THREE EVENTS)P(A∩B) = P(A)P(B),   P(A∩C) = P(A)P(C),   P(B∩C) = P(B)P(C),   and   P(A∩B∩C) = P(A)P(B)P(C)

All four conditions are required. It is entirely possible for three events to be pairwise independent (every pair satisfies the two-event condition) without being mutually independent (the triple product condition fails) — Example 5.3 below constructs exactly this scenario. When events are mutually independent, the multiplication rule extends cleanly to any subset of them, which is what makes independence assumptions so powerful for computation (e.g., in reliability problems with many components).

In practice, most exam problems don't ask you to verify mutual independence from first principles — instead, the problem statement simply tells you to assume a collection of events (e.g., claims from different, unrelated policyholders, or successive independent trials of an experiment) are mutually independent. When that assumption is given, the multiplication rule extends to any number of events with no correction terms at all:

MULTIPLICATION RULE — n MUTUALLY INDEPENDENT EVENTSP(A1 ∩ A2 ∩ ... ∩ An) = P(A1) · P(A2) ⋯ P(An)

This is the formula underlying, for instance, the assumption in many introductory insurance models that different policyholders' claim experiences are independent of one another — it lets an actuary multiply probabilities across an entire block of policies without needing to track complicated joint dependencies.

5.3 Independence vs. Mutual Exclusivity

This is one of the most heavily tested points of confusion on the entire exam. Independence and mutual exclusivity sound similar but are nearly opposite ideas. Mutually exclusive events (with both probabilities positive) can never be independent: if A ∩ B = ∅, then P(A ∩ B) = 0, but P(A) · P(B) > 0 whenever both events have positive probability — so the independence condition P(A∩B) = P(A)P(B) fails. Intuitively: if A and B are mutually exclusive, then knowing A occurred tells you for certain that B did not — that is about as far from "no information" as two events can get. Independence is about probabilistic irrelevance; mutual exclusivity is about physical (or logical) impossibility of co-occurrence. Do not use these terms interchangeably, and do not assume one implies the other — a problem that says "assume the events are mutually exclusive" is telling you they are dependent, not that you may multiply their probabilities.

5.4 Independence of Complements

A convenient fact: if A and B are independent, then so are A and B′, and likewise A′ and B, and A′ and B′. This makes sense intuitively — if knowing whether B happened tells you nothing about A, then knowing whether B failed to happen shouldn't tell you anything about A either.

INDEPENDENCE OF COMPLEMENTSIf A, B independent, then P(A′ ∩ B′) = P(A′) · P(B′) = (1 − P(A))(1 − P(B))
Example 5.1

Given P(A) = 0.4, P(B) = 0.5, and P(A ∩ B) = 0.2, determine whether A and B are independent.

Show Solution

Check whether P(A ∩ B) = P(A) · P(B): P(A) · P(B) = 0.4 × 0.5 = 0.2, which matches the given P(A∩B) = 0.2. The events are independent.

Example 5.2

A machine has two identical, independently operating backup components, each with a 0.9 probability of working when needed. (a) If the machine needs only one of the two components to work (a parallel/backup arrangement), find the probability the machine operates. (b) If the machine instead needs both components to work (a series arrangement), find that probability.

Show Solution

(a) Parallel (at least one works): Use the complement — the machine fails only if both components fail. By independence, P(both fail) = (0.1)(0.1) = 0.01. So P(machine works) = 1 − 0.01 = 0.99.

(b) Series (both must work): By independence, P(both work) = (0.9)(0.9) = 0.81.

This pair of results is the standard reliability-engineering illustration of why redundancy (parallel components) dramatically improves system reliability compared to a single point of failure (series components), even when the improvement per component seems modest.

Example 5.3

Two fair coins are flipped independently. Let A = "first flip is heads," B = "second flip is heads," and C = "both flips match (both heads or both tails)." Show that A, B, C are pairwise independent but not mutually independent.

Show Solution

The sample space is Ω = {HH, HT, TH, TT}, each outcome with probability 1/4. Then A = {HH, HT}, B = {HH, TH}, C = {HH, TT}, each with probability 1/2.

Pairwise check: A ∩ B = {HH}, so P(A∩B) = 1/4 = P(A)P(B) = (1/2)(1/2). ✓
A ∩ C = {HH}, so P(A∩C) = 1/4 = P(A)P(C). ✓
B ∩ C = {HH}, so P(B∩C) = 1/4 = P(B)P(C). ✓
All three pairs are independent.

Triple check: A ∩ B ∩ C = {HH}, so P(A∩B∩C) = 1/4. But P(A)P(B)P(C) = (1/2)(1/2)(1/2) = 1/8. Since 1/4 ≠ 1/8, A, B, C are not mutually independent — even though every pair is. Intuitively, knowing any two of {A, B, C} completely determines the third (e.g., knowing A and B tells you exactly whether C occurred), so the three events carry redundant joint information even though no single pair does.

Example 5.4

Suppose P(A) = 0.3 and P(B) = 0.6, and A and B are independent. Find P(A′ ∩ B′).

Show Solution

Since A and B are independent, so are A′ and B′:

P(A′ ∩ B′) = P(A′) · P(B′) = (1 − 0.3)(1 − 0.6) = (0.7)(0.4) = 0.28

Example 5.5

A satellite relies on 4 mutually independent backup transmitters, each with a 0.80 probability of functioning correctly. The satellite can still communicate as long as at least one transmitter functions. Find the probability the satellite can communicate.

Show Solution

With 4 mutually independent components, use the complement together with the n-event multiplication rule: the satellite fails to communicate only if all four transmitters fail simultaneously.

P(all 4 fail) = (0.20)(0.20)(0.20)(0.20) = 0.204 = 0.0016

P(at least one works) = 1 − 0.0016 = 0.9984

This example generalizes Example 5.2's two-component parallel system to n components, and illustrates why engineered systems requiring high reliability are built with several independent redundant components rather than relying on a single high-quality one: each additional independent backup multiplies the (already small) failure probability by another factor less than 1, driving the overall failure probability down geometrically fast.

Example 5.5 is also a first glimpse of an idea you will formalize soon after finishing this Part: when an experiment consisting of n independent, identical trials (here, n independent transmitters, each either "success" or "failure" with the same probability) is repeated, the number of successes follows what is called a binomial distribution. You are not expected to know that name or its formulas yet — that arrives later in the syllabus — but it is worth noticing now that the raw computational tool making the binomial distribution work is exactly the n-event independent multiplication rule from this chapter, combined with counting the number of ways successes and failures can be arranged (Chapter 3). Every major discrete distribution you will study later builds on this Part's tools in some such combination.

5.5 Independence as a Modeling Assumption

It is worth being explicit about something implicit throughout this chapter: independence is very often an assumption imposed by the person building a model, not a fact that is directly observed in the raw data. When an actuary assumes that the claim experience of different policyholders is mutually independent, that assumption is a simplification that makes the mathematics tractable (it lets you multiply probabilities freely across an entire block of business, as in Section 5.2), but it can fail to hold in reality — a single catastrophic event, such as a hurricane or a widespread economic downturn, can cause many policyholders' claims to become correlated all at once, precisely because a common underlying cause (the storm, the recession) affects everyone simultaneously. Recognizing when an independence assumption is reasonable, and when it is likely to break down, is a core professional skill for actuaries beyond this exam, and it is also directly testable: a well-constructed exam problem will sometimes explicitly state "assume independence" specifically because, without that explicit statement, you should not assume it, and the calculation would be impossible to complete without it (since P(A∩B) cannot be recovered from P(A) and P(B) alone unless independence, or some other explicit joint information, is given).

If a problem gives you only the marginal probabilities P(A) and P(B) and asks for P(A∩B) or P(A∪B) with no further information, check the wording carefully for "independent," "independently," or an equivalent phrase. Without it, the problem is under-determined and you are missing a piece of given information — go back and re-read the problem statement rather than guessing.

Finally, a terminology note that becomes more important later in the syllabus once you study joint distributions of random variables: the notion of independence for events defined here is the direct ancestor of the notion of independence for random variables you will meet soon after. A more general (and, for continuous random variables, more natural) equivalent characterization of independent events is that knowing whether B occurred does not shift the conditional probability of A away from its unconditional value for any event A built from the same experiment — not just the two specific events named in a problem. You won't need this more general phrasing for Exam P computations, but it explains why independence, once established between two events, tends to propagate cleanly to their complements, unions, and intersections with other independent events, as used throughout this chapter.

6. Mutually Exclusive Events; Addition & Multiplication Rules

Learning Outcomes Covered: (d) Calculate probabilities of mutually exclusive events. (e) Calculate probabilities using addition and multiplication rules.

6.1 Consolidating the Addition Rules

Chapters 2 through 5 introduced the addition rule, conditional probability, and independence as separate topics, each with its own motivating questions. This chapter is deliberately a synthesis chapter rather than a source of brand-new theory: its job is to make sure you can move fluidly between the addition-rule material (Chapter 2) and the multiplication-rule material (Chapters 4 and 5) within a single problem, since real exam questions rarely test a single idea in isolation. If Chapters 2–5 taught you the individual tools, this chapter is about building the muscle memory to select the right tool quickly, under time pressure, from a mixed toolbox.

Chapter 2 introduced the general addition rule; here we tie it together with mutual exclusivity as a special case worth recognizing instantly, since it saves you a step of arithmetic every time it applies.

GENERAL ADDITION RULEP(A ∪ B) = P(A) + P(B) − P(A ∩ B)
ADDITION RULE — MUTUALLY EXCLUSIVE EVENTSIf A ∩ B = ∅, then   P(A ∪ B) = P(A) + P(B)

The mutually-exclusive version is simply the general rule with P(A∩B) = 0. The reflex to look for should be: whenever a problem's events are structured as disjoint categories (a person is in exactly one age bracket, a claim came from exactly one of several distinct causes), you can add probabilities directly with no correction term.

6.2 Consolidating the Multiplication Rules

Similarly, Chapter 4's general multiplication rule (based on conditional probability) specializes neatly when events are independent, since P(A|B) reduces to the unconditional P(A).

GENERAL MULTIPLICATION RULE (DEPENDENT EVENTS)P(A ∩ B) = P(A) · P(B | A) = P(B) · P(A | B)
MULTIPLICATION RULE — INDEPENDENT EVENTSIf A, B independent, then   P(A ∩ B) = P(A) · P(B)

6.3 Which Rule to Reach For

Because these four formulas look superficially similar (all involve P(A), P(B), and either a sum or product), it helps to organize the decision as two separate yes/no questions, summarized in Table 6.1.

Table 6.1 — Decision Summary: Addition vs. Multiplication, Dependent vs. Independent
Question being asked Rule family Extra condition? Formula
P(A or B) — "at least one occurs" Addition Are A, B mutually exclusive? Yes: P(A)+P(B)  |  No: P(A)+P(B)−P(A∩B)
P(A and B) — "both occur" Multiplication Are A, B independent? Yes: P(A)P(B)  |  No: P(A)P(B|A)

Read the question first: "or"/"at least one"/"union" language signals addition; "and"/"both"/"joint" language signals multiplication. Then ask the follow-up question (mutual exclusivity for addition, independence for multiplication) to decide whether you need the correction term or can use the simplified shortcut.

Written out as a decision path, the full logic of this chapter (and much of Chapters 2, 4, and 5) collapses into a short sequence of questions you can run through on any General Probability word problem:

  1. Does the question ask for P(A or B) or P(A and B)? If neither — if it asks for P(A given B) instead — you're in Chapter 4/conditional-probability territory, not this decision path.
  2. If "or": are A and B mutually exclusive (can they physically/logically occur together)? If yes, add the probabilities directly. If no, or if you're unsure, use the general addition rule and subtract P(A∩B).
  3. If "and": are A and B independent (does the problem state or imply that one event's occurrence has no bearing on the other)? If yes, multiply the unconditional probabilities directly. If no, or if you're unsure, use the general multiplication rule with a conditional probability.
  4. Three or more events? Extend the same two-event logic: sum pairwise/triple-overlap corrections for "or" questions (inclusion–exclusion), or chain conditional probabilities for "and" questions (the chain rule from Chapter 4).

Internalizing this four-step path is, in a real sense, a compact summary of the computational core of the entire General Probability topic — nearly every numeric word problem in Chapters 2 through 7 is some combination of these steps applied once or twice.

The single most common arithmetic error in this chapter is adding P(A) + P(B) for events that are not actually mutually exclusive, which double-counts the overlap and produces an inflated (sometimes even impossible, greater-than-1) answer. If a problem doesn't explicitly state the events are disjoint, check whether they logically could overlap before dropping the −P(A∩B) term.

A helpful habit, especially under exam time pressure, is to sanity-check your final numeric answer against the axioms themselves before moving on: any legitimate probability must fall between 0 and 1 inclusive (Axiom 1 combined with monotonicity against Ω). If an addition-rule calculation produces something like 1.15, you have almost certainly either mistakenly treated overlapping events as mutually exclusive, or made an arithmetic slip in the correction term — either way, that is a free, nearly instantaneous error check that costs only a glance at your final number.

Example 6.1

A single card is drawn from a standard 52-card deck. Find the probability the card is a king or a queen.

Show Solution

No card is both a king and a queen, so these events are mutually exclusive: P(K ∩ Q) = 0.

P(K ∪ Q) = P(K) + P(Q) = 4/52 + 4/52 = 8/52 = 2/13 ≈ 0.154

Example 6.2

A streaming platform's records show that 60% of subscribers have a video plan, 40% have a music plan, and 20% have both. The platform estimates that 25% of subscribers with only a video plan will upgrade to premium next year, 45% of those with only a music plan will upgrade, and 75% of those with both plans will upgrade. Find the overall percentage of subscribers expected to upgrade to premium.

Show Solution

The key move is to partition the subscribers holding at least one plan into three mutually exclusive groups so the addition rule applies with no overlap correction: video-only, music-only, and both.

P(video only) = P(video) − P(both) = 0.60 − 0.20 = 0.40
P(music only) = P(music) − P(both) = 0.40 − 0.20 = 0.20
P(both) = 0.20

These three groups are mutually exclusive, and together they account for 80% of subscribers — the remaining 20% hold neither plan and, having nothing to upgrade, contribute zero to the total. Weight each group's upgrade rate by its size and add:

P(upgrades) = (0.40)(0.25) + (0.20)(0.45) + (0.20)(0.75) = 0.10 + 0.09 + 0.15 = 0.34

About 34% of subscribers are expected to upgrade to premium.

Example 6.3

A fair die is rolled and a fair coin is flipped, independently of each other. Find the probability the die shows a 6 and the coin shows heads.

Show Solution

The die roll and coin flip are physically independent events, so:

P(die=6 ∩ heads) = P(die=6) · P(heads) = (1/6)(1/2) = 1/12

Example 6.4

A box contains 20 light bulbs, of which 5 are defective. Two bulbs are selected at random without replacement. Find the probability both are defective.

Show Solution

These draws are dependent (sampling without replacement changes the composition of the box), so we use the general multiplication rule with conditioning:

P(D1 ∩ D2) = P(D1) · P(D2 | D1) = (5/20)(4/19) = 20/380 = 1/19 ≈ 0.0526

Example 6.5

A pair of independent, fair four-sided dice (faces 1–4) is rolled once. Find the probability that the sum of the two dice is 5 or both dice show the same number.

Show Solution

This problem requires both an addition step and a multiplication step, so it's a good test of the full decision path in Table 6.1. There are 4 × 4 = 16 equally likely outcomes.

Let A = "sum is 5": the pairs (1,4),(2,3),(3,2),(4,1), so P(A) = 4/16.

Let B = "both dice match": the pairs (1,1),(2,2),(3,3),(4,4), so P(B) = 4/16.

These two events are mutually exclusive — if the dice match, their sum is even (2, 4, 6, or 8), never 5 — so no correction term is needed:

P(A ∪ B) = P(A) + P(B) = 4/16 + 4/16 = 8/16 = 0.5

(Had the problem instead asked for the probability that both dice individually show an even number and the sum is 5 — an "and" question about independent-seeming but actually dependent conditions — you would instead reach for the multiplication step of the decision path, conditioning as needed. Recognizing which step the wording calls for is the real skill being tested here.)

Example 6.6

A component is tested in two independent stages. It passes Stage 1 with probability 0.85. If it passes Stage 1, it moves to Stage 2, which it passes with probability 0.90; if it fails Stage 1, it is retested once at Stage 1 only, passing the retest with probability 0.85 (independent of the first attempt), and if it passes the retest it still must go on to (and pass) Stage 2. Find the probability the component ultimately passes both stages.

Show Solution

This problem has two mutually exclusive paths to ultimate success — "pass Stage 1 immediately, then pass Stage 2" or "fail Stage 1, pass the retest, then pass Stage 2" — so the addition rule combines them, while each individual path is itself a chain of independent multiplications.

Path 1 (pass immediately): P = (0.85)(0.90) = 0.765

Path 2 (fail, then pass retest, then pass Stage 2): P = (1−0.85)(0.85)(0.90) = (0.15)(0.85)(0.90) = 0.114750

These two paths cannot both occur for the same component (it either passes Stage 1 on the first try or it doesn't), so they are mutually exclusive, and we add:

P(ultimately passes both stages) = 0.765 + 0.11475 = 0.87975 ≈ 0.880

This "sum of mutually exclusive paths, each a product of independent stage probabilities" structure is exactly the tree-diagram logic that will reappear, formalized, as the Law of Total Probability in Chapter 7 — every path through a probability tree ending in the same outcome gets added, while probabilities multiply along any single path.

7. Bayes' Theorem & the Law of Total Probability

Learning Outcomes Covered: (g) State Bayes Theorem and the law of total probability and use them to calculate conditional probabilities.

This final chapter of Part I is, for many candidates, the single most consequential section of the entire General Probability topic: Bayes' Theorem problems are a perennial exam favorite precisely because they require synthesizing everything from Chapters 1 through 6 — partitions, the addition and multiplication rules, and conditional probability — into one multi-step calculation. The good news is that, once the tree-diagram or table organization from Section 7.3 becomes second nature, the calculation itself is entirely mechanical: multiply along each path, sum for the denominator, and divide for whichever posterior probability the question asks for.

7.1 The Law of Total Probability

Suppose the sample space is sliced into a partition B1, B2, ..., Bn (recall from Chapter 1: mutually exclusive and exhaustive). Any event A can then be decomposed into the pieces of itself that fall inside each partition block: A = (A∩B1) ∪ (A∩B2) ∪ ... ∪ (A∩Bn), and these pieces are themselves mutually exclusive (since the Bi are). Applying Axiom 3 and then the multiplication rule to each piece yields the Law of Total Probability:

LAW OF TOTAL PROBABILITYP(A) = P(A|B1)P(B1) + P(A|B2)P(B2) + ... + P(A|Bn)P(Bn) = Σi P(A|Bi) P(Bi)

In words: to find the overall (unconditional) probability of A, break the world into scenarios Bi, find the probability of A within each scenario, weight by how likely each scenario is, and add. This is the natural tool whenever a population is divided into distinct sub-groups (age brackets, policy types, machines on a factory floor) each with its own rate of some outcome.

7.2 Bayes' Theorem

Bayes' Theorem reverses the direction of conditioning: given that A has occurred, it tells us how to update our probability that we're in scenario Bk. The derivation is short and worth knowing, because it shows Bayes' Theorem is not a new axiom — it's just the definition of conditional probability applied twice, once forward and once via the Law of Total Probability:

Start from the definition of conditional probability applied in the "reverse" direction: P(Bk|A) = P(Bk ∩ A)/P(A). Rewrite the numerator using the multiplication rule, P(Bk∩A) = P(A|Bk)P(Bk), and rewrite the denominator using the Law of Total Probability. The result:

BAYES' THEOREMP(Bk | A) = [P(A|Bk) · P(Bk)] / [Σi P(A|Bi) P(Bi)]

The P(Bk) terms are called prior probabilities — what we believed about each scenario before observing A. The P(Bk|A) terms are posterior probabilities — the updated beliefs after observing A. The P(A|Bk) terms are likelihoods — how probable the observed evidence A is under each scenario.

It's worth being precise about how the Law of Total Probability and Bayes' Theorem relate, since students sometimes treat them as two unrelated formulas to memorize separately rather than as two views of the same calculation. Table 7.0 makes the relationship explicit.

Table 7.0 — Law of Total Probability vs. Bayes' Theorem
Law of Total Probability Bayes' Theorem
Question answered "Overall, how likely is A?" "Given A occurred, which scenario were we probably in?"
Direction of conditioning used as input P(A|Bi) (scenario → evidence) P(A|Bi) (same inputs)
Direction of conditioning produced as output None — output is unconditional, P(A) P(Bk|A) (evidence → scenario)
Relationship Bayes' Theorem's denominator is the Law of Total Probability computation; Bayes' Theorem is the Law of Total Probability result combined with one extra numerator term.
Do not mix up priors and posteriors. P(Bk) is given directly in the problem, before you know anything about A; P(Bk|A) is what you compute at the very end, after folding in the evidence.
