A shockingly common exam mistake is to report the likelihood P(A|Bk) as if it were the answer to a question that actually asked for the posterior P(Bk|A) — always re-read the question to confirm which direction of conditioning it wants.

7.3 The Tree Diagram Approach

The most reliable way to organize a Bayes problem — and the technique actuarial students are strongly encouraged to use on scratch paper — is a probability tree. Draw a first layer of branches for each scenario Bi, labeled with the prior probabilities P(Bi). From the end of each of those branches, draw a second layer of branches for whether A occurs, labeled with the conditional (likelihood) probabilities P(A|Bi). Multiplying along any full path from the root to a leaf gives the joint probability of that scenario-and-outcome combination — exactly the terms that appear in the Law of Total Probability. Bayes' Theorem then just asks: of all the paths ending in "A occurred," what fraction of their combined probability belongs to the one path through Bk?

Table 7.1 — Anatomy of a Bayes Tree (n scenarios)
Layer Branch labels Represents
1st (root) P(B1), P(B2), ..., P(Bn) Prior probability of each scenario
2nd (leaves) P(A|Bi) for each i Likelihood of the evidence within scenario i
Path product P(Bi) · P(A|Bi) Joint probability of scenario i and evidence A
Bayes answer [path through Bk] / [sum of all paths ending in A] Posterior P(Bk|A)
On the actual exam you will rarely have time to draw a polished tree — a quick two-column scratch table (scenarios down the left, prior/likelihood/joint-product across the top) accomplishes the same organization in far less time and is what most successful candidates actually do under time pressure.

7.4 The Odds Form of Bayes' Theorem (Two Hypotheses)

When a Bayes problem involves exactly two competing scenarios — a disease is present or not, a claim is fraudulent or not, a part came from Supplier A or Supplier B — there is an equivalent, often faster, way to organize the calculation using odds instead of probabilities. The odds of an event A are defined as P(A) / P(A′) — for instance, "3 to 1 odds" means P(A) = 3 P(A′), i.e., P(A) = 3/4. Taking the ratio of Bayes' Theorem applied to B1 versus B1′ causes the shared denominator (the Law of Total Probability term) to cancel entirely:

ODDS FORM OF BAYES' THEOREMPosterior odds of B1 given A  =  [Prior odds of B1]  ×  [Likelihood ratio]
P(B1|A) / P(B1′|A)  =  [P(B1)/P(B1′)]  ×  [P(A|B1)/P(A|B1′)]

This restatement makes explicit something that is easy to lose sight of inside the full Bayes' Theorem fraction: observing evidence A updates your prior odds by multiplying them by a single number, the likelihood ratio. A likelihood ratio greater than 1 means the evidence favors B1 (increases its odds); a likelihood ratio less than 1 means the evidence favors B1′. Once you have the posterior odds, converting back to a posterior probability is a short final step: if the odds are x to 1, then P(B1|A) = x / (x+1).

Example 7.5

A shipment of components is known to come from one of two factories with equal prior probability (a coin-flip as to origin). Factory X's components pass a stress test 90% of the time; Factory Y's components pass only 60% of the time. A randomly selected component is tested and passes. Use the odds form of Bayes' Theorem to find the probability it came from Factory X.

Show Solution

Prior odds of Factory X versus Factory Y: since both are equally likely, P(X)/P(Y) = 0.5/0.5 = 1 (i.e., "1 to 1" or "even odds").

Likelihood ratio: P(Pass|X)/P(Pass|Y) = 0.90/0.60 = 1.5.

Posterior odds: 1 × 1.5 = 1.5 — i.e., "1.5 to 1" in favor of Factory X.

Convert back to a probability: P(X | Pass) = 1.5 / (1.5 + 1) = 1.5/2.5 = 0.6.

Check against the standard Bayes' Theorem formula directly: P(Pass) = (0.5)(0.90)+(0.5)(0.60) = 0.75, and P(X|Pass) = (0.5)(0.90)/0.75 = 0.45/0.75 = 0.6 — the same answer, confirming the odds-form shortcut. The odds form is particularly convenient whenever the prior is 50/50 (prior odds of exactly 1), since the posterior odds then equal the likelihood ratio directly, skipping the Law of Total Probability computation entirely.

Example 7.6

A claims department routes incoming auto claims to one of four adjusters based on region: Adjuster 1 handles 40% of claims and flags 5% of the claims they review for potential fraud; Adjuster 2 handles 25% of claims and flags 8%; Adjuster 3 handles 20% of claims and flags 3%; Adjuster 4 handles 15% of claims and flags 10%. A claim selected at random from the department's records is found to be flagged for potential fraud. Find the probability it was reviewed by Adjuster 4.

Show Solution

This four-branch problem is solved with exactly the same two-step mechanical process as Example 7.4's three-branch case — the number of branches doesn't change the method, only the length of the sum. Organize the four path products in a table:

Adjuster (prior) P(flagged | adjuster) Path product
1: 0.40 0.05 0.40 × 0.05 = 0.0200
2: 0.25 0.08 0.25 × 0.08 = 0.0200
3: 0.20 0.03 0.20 × 0.03 = 0.0060
4: 0.15 0.10 0.15 × 0.10 = 0.0150

Denominator (Law of Total Probability): P(Flagged) = 0.0200+0.0200+0.0060+0.0150 = 0.0610.

Bayes' Theorem for Adjuster 4: P(Adjuster 4 | Flagged) = 0.0150 / 0.0610 ≈ 0.246.

Even though Adjuster 4 reviews only 15% of all claims, they account for roughly 24.6% of flagged claims, reflecting their comparatively high flag rate. As a quick check, the four posterior probabilities across all adjusters should themselves sum to 1 — here (0.0200+0.0200+0.0060+0.0150)/0.0610 = 0.0610/0.0610 = 1, confirming the four path products correctly exhaust every way a flagged claim could have arisen.

7.5 Sequential Updating

A subtle but powerful feature of Bayes' Theorem is that it can be applied repeatedly as new evidence arrives, with each round's posterior probability becoming the next round's prior probability. Suppose, in a Bayes problem with scenarios B1, ..., Bn, you first observe evidence A1 and compute the posterior P(Bk|A1) exactly as in this chapter's examples. If a second, independent piece of evidence A2 then becomes available, you can fold it in by treating P(Bk|A1) as the new prior and re-applying Bayes' Theorem with A2's likelihoods — there is no need to redo the calculation from scratch incorporating both pieces of evidence simultaneously, provided the evidence is conditionally independent given each scenario. This is the same idea underlying the odds form of Bayes' Theorem in Section 7.4: each new, conditionally independent piece of evidence simply multiplies the current odds by its own likelihood ratio, one factor at a time. While multi-stage sequential Bayes problems of this kind are more common on later, more advanced actuarial exams than on Exam P, recognizing the concept — that a posterior can serve as the next prior — deepens your understanding of what Bayes' Theorem is really doing: continuously updating a belief as information accumulates, rather than computing a single static answer from a fixed, one-time data set.

Example 7.1

A factory has two machines producing the same part. Machine 1 produces 60% of all parts and has a 2% defect rate; Machine 2 produces the remaining 40% and has a 5% defect rate. Find the overall probability a randomly selected part is defective.

Show Solution

This is a direct application of the Law of Total Probability with the partition {Machine 1, Machine 2}. Let D = "defective."

P(D) = P(D|M1)P(M1) + P(D|M2)P(M2) = (0.02)(0.60) + (0.05)(0.40) = 0.012 + 0.020 = 0.032

Example 7.2

A screening test for a disease has 92% sensitivity (it correctly returns positive for 92% of people who actually have the disease) and produces a false positive in 3% of people who do not have the disease. The disease has a 2% prevalence in the population being screened. Given that a randomly selected person tests positive, find the probability that person actually has the disease.

Show Solution

Let Dis = "has the disease" and Pos = "tests positive." We're given the priors P(Dis) = 0.02, P(Dis′) = 0.98, and the likelihoods P(Pos|Dis) = 0.92, P(Pos|Dis′) = 0.03. We want the posterior P(Dis|Pos) — note this is the reverse direction from what's given, which is exactly the signal to use Bayes' Theorem.

Step 1 — Law of Total Probability (the denominator):

P(Pos) = P(Pos|Dis)P(Dis) + P(Pos|Dis′)P(Dis′) = (0.92)(0.02) + (0.03)(0.98) = 0.0184 + 0.0294 = 0.0478

Step 2 — Bayes' Theorem:

P(Dis | Pos) = 0.0184 / 0.0478 ≈ 0.385

Despite the test seeming quite accurate (92% sensitivity, only 3% false-positive rate), a person who tests positive has under a 40% chance of actually having the disease — because the disease is rare (2% prevalence), the much larger pool of disease-free people still generates a substantial number of false positives. This "surprising" result is a classic and very testable Bayes' Theorem phenomenon: always weigh the base rate (prior), not just the apparent accuracy of the test.

Example 7.3

An insurer's policyholders fall into three risk tiers: 55% standard, 35% preferred, and 10% elite. The probability of death within the year is 0.012 for standard policyholders, 0.006 for preferred, and 0.002 for elite. A policyholder is known to have died during the year. Find the probability that policyholder was in the elite tier.

Show Solution

This is a three-branch Bayes problem. Let S, P, E denote the three tiers and Death the event of dying within the year. Priors: P(S)=0.55, P(P)=0.35, P(E)=0.10. Likelihoods: P(Death|S)=0.012, P(Death|P)=0.006, P(Death|E)=0.002.

Step 1 — total probability of death:

P(Death) = (0.55)(0.012) + (0.35)(0.006) + (0.10)(0.002) = 0.0066 + 0.0021 + 0.0002 = 0.0089

Step 2 — Bayes' Theorem for the elite tier:

P(E | Death) = 0.0002 / 0.0089 ≈ 0.0225

Even though elite policyholders make up 10% of the book of business, they account for only about 2.25% of deaths — consistent with their much lower mortality rate — illustrating how the posterior can shift dramatically away from the prior when likelihoods differ sharply across scenarios.

Example 7.4

A retailer sources phone batteries from three suppliers: Supplier A provides 50% of stock with a 3% defect rate, Supplier B provides 30% with a 5% defect rate, and Supplier C provides 20% with an 8% defect rate. A battery is selected at random and found to be defective. Using a tree-diagram approach, find the probability it came from Supplier C.

Show Solution

Set up the tree with three first-layer branches (the suppliers) and a defective/not-defective second layer under each. Only the "defective" leaves matter for this problem, so we compute just those three path products:

Supplier (prior) P(defective | supplier) Path product
A: 0.50 0.03 0.50 × 0.03 = 0.0150
B: 0.30 0.05 0.30 × 0.05 = 0.0150
C: 0.20 0.08 0.20 × 0.08 = 0.0160

Denominator (Law of Total Probability — sum of all three path products, i.e., the overall probability a random battery is defective):

P(Defective) = 0.0150 + 0.0150 + 0.0160 = 0.0460

Bayes' Theorem — the posterior for Supplier C is its path product divided by the sum of all paths:

P(C | Defective) = 0.0160 / 0.0460 ≈ 0.348

Notice how the tree/table structure makes this a mechanical two-step process regardless of how many suppliers (branches) are involved — this scales cleanly to problems with four, five, or more scenarios, which is exactly the multi-branch style favored on the actual exam.

Part I Practice Problems

The following 22 problems span all seven chapters of Part I, in roughly increasing order of difficulty within each cluster. Attempt each problem fully before revealing the solution. As you work through them, notice how frequently a problem draws on more than one chapter at once — this cumulative, cross-chapter blending is exactly how the real exam tests General Probability, so treat this set as much as a diagnostic of your overall fluency as a check on any single topic.

1. Let Ω = {1, 2, ..., 12}. Let A be the event that a number is a multiple of 2, and B the event that a number is a multiple of 3. Find A ∩ B, A ∪ B, and (A ∪ B)′.
Show Answer

A = {2,4,6,8,10,12}, B = {3,6,9,12}.

A ∩ B = {6, 12}.

A ∪ B = {2,3,4,6,8,9,10,12}.

(A ∪ B)′ = {1, 5, 7, 11} — the numbers from 1 to 12 that are multiples of neither 2 nor 3.

Answer: A∩B = {6,12}; A∪B = {2,3,4,6,8,9,10,12}; (A∪B)′ = {1,5,7,11}

As a check, count elements: |A∪B| = 8 and |(A∪B)′| = 4, and 8 + 4 = 12 = |Ω|, confirming the complement was computed correctly — a quick arithmetic check worth running any time you compute a complement by listing elements.

2. A fair die is rolled. Let A = {1,2}, B = {3,4}, C = {5,6}. (a) Verify that A, B, C form a partition of the sample space. (b) Verify that P(A)+P(B)+P(C)=1, consistent with Axiom 3.
Show Answer

(a) The three sets are pairwise disjoint (no shared outcomes) and their union {1,2,3,4,5,6} = Ω, so they form a partition.

(b) Each event has 2 of the 6 equally likely outcomes, so P(A)=P(B)=P(C)=1/3. Sum: 1/3+1/3+1/3 = 1, matching Axiom 3 applied to a partition of Ω.

Answer: Yes, it's a valid partition; probabilities sum to 1.

This tiny example is a useful mental template: any time a die, spinner, or similar equally-likely device is split into equal-sized, non-overlapping groups of outcomes, the resulting event probabilities automatically form a partition summing to 1 — you never need to separately verify Axiom 3 numerically once you've confirmed the sets are disjoint and exhaustive, since the axiom guarantees it.

3. Given P(A) = 0.7, P(B) = 0.4, and P(A ∪ B) = 0.9, find P(A ∩ B).
Show Answer

By the general addition rule, P(A∩B) = P(A)+P(B)−P(A∪B) = 0.7+0.4−0.9 = 0.2.

This problem simply rearranges the addition rule to solve for the intersection instead of the union — a common variation where the exam gives you three of the four quantities in P(A), P(B), P(A∩B), P(A∪B) and asks for the fourth. Always identify which of the four quantities is missing before choosing which way to rearrange the formula.

4. Two fair six-sided dice are rolled. Find the probability that the sum is 7 or the sum is 11.
Show Answer

These sums cannot occur simultaneously, so the events are mutually exclusive. P(sum=7) = 6/36 (the pairs (1,6),(2,5),(3,4),(4,3),(5,2),(6,1)); P(sum=11) = 2/36 (the pairs (5,6),(6,5)).

P(7 or 11) = 6/36 + 2/36 = 8/36 = 2/9 ≈ 0.222

Note that no correction term was needed here precisely because a single roll of two dice cannot simultaneously sum to both 7 and 11 — a good habit is to explicitly note why two events are mutually exclusive (rather than assuming it) before dropping the intersection term from the addition rule.

5. Given P(A) = 0.6, P(A ∪ B) = 0.8, and P(A ∩ B) = 0.2, find P(B).
Show Answer

From the addition rule: P(B) = P(A∪B) − P(A) + P(A∩B) = 0.8 − 0.6 + 0.2 = 0.4

Sanity check: with P(A)=0.6 and P(B)=0.4, the largest P(A∩B) could possibly be is min(P(A),P(B)) = 0.4 (since A∩B ⊂ B forces P(A∩B) ≤ P(B) by monotonicity), and the given value 0.2 respects that bound, so the answer is internally consistent.

6. A restaurant's fixed-price menu offers 5 appetizers, 8 entrées, and 4 desserts. A meal consists of exactly one item from each course. How many distinct meals are possible?
Show Answer

By the multiplication principle: 5 × 8 × 4 = 160 distinct meals.

Note there is no division by any factorial here, unlike the combination and permutation formulas elsewhere in this set — that's because the three choices (appetizer, entrée, dessert) come from three different categories, so there is no possibility of "double counting" an outcome by choosing the same two items in a different order. Dividing by a factorial is only ever appropriate when you are removing redundant orderings within a single selection from one category.

7. A track team has 9 runners. In how many ways can 4 of them be assigned to finish in 1st, 2nd, 3rd, and 4th place (i.e., how many distinguishable orderings of the top 4 are possible)?
Show Answer

Order matters and there's no repetition — this is a permutation: 9P4 = 9×8×7×6 = 3,024

Contrast this with simply choosing (with no regard to finishing order) which 4 of the 9 runners make up a relay team: that would be the combination 9C4 = 126, exactly 4! = 24 times smaller — the factor by which the permutation count over-counts relative to the combination count, since each unordered group of 4 corresponds to 24 different finishing-order arrangements.

8. A lottery requires choosing 6 numbers from 1 to 49, with no regard to the order chosen. How many distinct lottery tickets are possible?
Show Answer

Order doesn't matter, no repetition — a combination: 49C6 = 49!/(6!43!) = 13,983,816

This is the classic real-world illustration of just how explosively combination counts grow: increasing the pool from, say, 6 numbers to 49 numbers while still choosing 6 takes the count from a trivially small number (6C6=1) to nearly 14 million, which is exactly why lottery jackpot odds are so extreme even though the underlying rule ("pick 6 numbers") sounds simple.

9. How many distinct arrangements are there of the letters in the word MISSISSIPPI?
Show Answer

MISSISSIPPI has 11 letters: M (1), I (4), S (4), P (2). Using the distinguishable-permutations formula:

11! / (1! 4! 4! 2!) = 39,916,800 / (1×24×24×2) = 39,916,800/1,152 = 34,650

Interesting aside: this is the identical numeric answer as Example 3.6's employee-team-assignment problem, and for the same underlying reason — both are "partition n labeled slots into groups of specified sizes" problems, just dressed up in different language (letter positions vs. employees). Recognizing this structural equivalence across superficially different word problems is a hallmark of strong combinatorics fluency.

10. A manager must assign 9 new hires to three departments: Sales needs 4 people, Marketing needs 3, and Support needs 2. (Assume employees are distinguishable but positions within a department are not.) How many ways can this assignment be made?
Show Answer

This is a multinomial-coefficient problem with group sizes 4, 3, 2 (summing to 9):

9! / (4! 3! 2!) = 362,880 / (24×6×2) = 362,880/288 = 1,260

You can also build this up sequentially, exactly as in Example 3.6: choose 4 of 9 for Sales (9C4=126), then 3 of the remaining 5 for Marketing (5C3=10), and the last 2 automatically form Support. 126 × 10 = 1,260, matching the direct multinomial computation and confirming the two approaches are algebraically identical.

11. A jar contains 6 red marbles and 4 green marbles. Three marbles are drawn at once (as a single handful, so order is irrelevant). Find the probability that exactly 2 of the 3 marbles drawn are red.
Show Answer

Sample space: 10C3 = 120 equally likely handfuls. Favorable outcomes: choose 2 of 6 red and 1 of 4 green: 6C2 × 4C1 = 15 × 4 = 60.

P(exactly 2 red) = 60/120 = 0.5

As a check, the four mutually exclusive cases for the number of red marbles drawn (0, 1, 2, or 3) should account for all 10C3=120 equally likely handfuls: zero red is 4C3=4, one red is 6C14C2=36, two red is the 60 found above, and three red is 6C3=20. Summing, 4+36+60+20=120 ✓, confirming no case was miscounted — running this kind of exhaustiveness check is cheap insurance whenever a problem naturally breaks into a small number of mutually exclusive cases.

12. Given P(A) = 0.5, P(B) = 0.3, and P(A ∩ B) = 0.1, find both P(A|B) and P(B|A), and confirm they are not equal.
Show Answer

P(A|B) = P(A∩B)/P(B) = 0.1/0.3 = 1/3 ≈ 0.333

P(B|A) = P(A∩B)/P(A) = 0.1/0.5 = 0.2

P(A|B) ≈ 0.333 while P(B|A) = 0.2 — confirmed unequal, illustrating that conditioning is not symmetric.

As a further check, verify that A and B are indeed dependent (not independent) here: P(A)P(B) = (0.5)(0.3) = 0.15 ≠ P(A∩B) = 0.1, so the two events are dependent, which is exactly why P(A|B) ≠ P(A) and P(B|A) ≠ P(B) in this problem — a useful bridge to the independence test formalized in Chapter 5.

13. Of 300 employees at a company, commute mode and department are cross-tabulated below. Find the probability an employee works in tech, given that they commute by transit.
Tech Non-Tech Total
Car 80 100 180
Transit 70 50 120
Total 150 150 300
Show Answer

Using the reduced sample space of transit commuters (120 employees), 70 of them work in tech:

P(Tech | Transit) = 70/120 = 7/12 ≈ 0.583

Compare this to the unconditional (overall) probability of working in tech, P(Tech) = 150/300 = 0.5. Since P(Tech|Transit) ≈ 0.583 ≠ P(Tech) = 0.5, commute mode and department are not independent in this data — transit commuters are somewhat more likely to work in tech than the overall population, a useful cross-check technique (comparing a conditional probability to its corresponding unconditional one) for spotting dependence in a table.

14. At an airline's frequent-flyer program, all members hold at least basic status. 70% hold elite status, and 25% have purchased travel insurance. Of members with elite status, 30% have also purchased travel insurance. Find the probability a randomly selected member does not have elite status and has not purchased travel insurance.
Show Answer

Let E = "holds elite status," so P(E) = 0.70 and P(E′) = 0.30. Let I = "purchased travel insurance," P(I) = 0.25. We're given P(I | E) = 0.30, so P(I ∩ E) = (0.70)(0.30) = 0.21.

Since every member is either in E or E′, we can isolate the insurance holders without elite status by subtraction:

P(I ∩ E′) = P(I) − P(I ∩ E) = 0.25 − 0.21 = 0.04

Finally, subtract this from the "no elite status" total to remove those who also have insurance:

P(E′ ∩ I′) = P(E′) − P(I ∩ E′) = 0.30 − 0.04 = 0.26

15. A monitoring system has two independent sensors, each with a 95% chance of correctly detecting a fault when one is present. Given a fault is present, find the probability at least one sensor detects it.
Show Answer

Use the complement: the system fails to detect only if both sensors fail, which by independence has probability (0.05)(0.05) = 0.0025.

P(at least one detects) = 1 − 0.0025 = 0.9975

This mirrors the reliability logic of Example 5.2(a) and Example 5.5 exactly: "at least one of several independent components succeeds" is always fastest to compute via the complement "all of them fail," since the individual failure probabilities (here, just 0.05 each) multiply cleanly under independence, whereas directly summing "exactly one detects," "exactly two detect" would require extra casework for no benefit.

16. A shopper is four times as likely to use a store's loyalty app as to use a paper coupon. Use of the two is independent, and the probability a shopper uses both is 0.09. Find the probability the shopper uses neither.
Show Answer

Let P(paper) = x and P(app) = 4x. By independence, P(both) = 4x² = 0.09, so x² = 0.0225 and x = 0.15. Then P(paper) = 0.15 and P(app) = 0.6.

Independence of the events implies independence of their complements:

P(neither) = (1 − 0.15)(1 − 0.6) = (0.85)(0.4) = 0.34

17. A tile is drawn at random from 52 tiles numbered 1 through 52. Find the probability the number is a multiple of 5 or a multiple of 13.
Show Answer

Multiples of 5 from 1–52: {5,10,...,50}, 10 numbers. Multiples of 13: {13,26,39,52}, 4 numbers. A number can only be a multiple of both 5 and 13 if it's a multiple of 65, but 65 > 52, so these events are mutually exclusive.

P(mult. of 5 or 13) = 10/52 + 4/52 = 14/52 = 7/26 ≈ 0.269

The key step easy to overlook here is checking mutual exclusivity numerically rather than just assuming it: two events "multiple of 5" and "multiple of 13" would overlap at any multiple of their least common multiple, 65 — since the tiles only run from 1 to 52, no such overlap can occur, and only for that reason is dropping the intersection term valid. Had the tiles instead run from 1 to 100, the number 65 would appear and an overlap correction of −1/100 would be required.

18. At a furniture store, the probability a customer requests neither delivery service nor assembly service is 0.30. The probability a customer requests delivery is 0.55, and the probability a customer requests assembly is 0.35. Find the probability a customer requests both services.
Show Answer

By De Morgan's Law, P(neither) = P((D∪A)′) = 1 − P(D∪A), so P(D∪A) = 1 − 0.30 = 0.70.

By the addition rule: P(D∩A) = P(D)+P(A)−P(D∪A) = 0.55+0.35−0.70 = 0.20

The two-step structure here — first use the complement/De Morgan step to recover P(D∪A) from the "neither" percentage, then use the ordinary addition rule to recover the intersection — is one of the most frequently recurring patterns on the actual exam for this topic, since "probability of neither" is a very natural way for a word problem to describe data.

19. Two cards are drawn without replacement from a standard 52-card deck. Find the probability the first is a spade and the second is a heart.
Show Answer

These draws are dependent, but note the second draw's condition (heart) doesn't overlap with the first draw's outcome (spade), so the heart count is unaffected by the first draw:

P(1st spade ∩ 2nd heart) = (13/52)(13/51) = 169/2652 = 13/204 ≈ 0.0637

It's worth noticing why the second fraction's numerator (13) is unchanged from a full suit's count even though a card has already been removed from the deck: the first card removed was a spade, which doesn't affect the count of hearts remaining. Only the denominator shrinks (52 to 51), reflecting one fewer card overall in the deck. Had the problem instead asked for two spades in a row, both the numerator and denominator would shrink between draws, exactly as in Example 4.3's urn problem.

20. A factory's output comes from three production lines contributing 50%, 30%, and 20% of total output, with defect rates of 1%, 2%, and 4% respectively. Find the overall probability a randomly selected unit is defective.
Show Answer

By the Law of Total Probability:

P(Defective) = (0.50)(0.01) + (0.30)(0.02) + (0.20)(0.04) = 0.005 + 0.006 + 0.008 = 0.019

Note that this overall defect rate (1.9%) is a weighted average of the three individual line defect rates (1%, 2%, 4%), weighted by each line's share of total output — the Law of Total Probability is, in this sense, nothing more than a probability-weighted average, a useful intuition to carry into every Bayes-adjacent problem in this chapter.

21. A tech retailer's laptop sales break down by tier: 50% are budget models (2% one-year warranty claim rate), 30% are mid-range models (6% claim rate), and 20% are premium models (10% claim rate). A randomly selected laptop is returned under warranty within the first year. Find the probability that laptop was a premium model.
Show Answer

Step 1 — total probability of a claim:

P(Claim) = (0.50)(0.02) + (0.30)(0.06) + (0.20)(0.10) = 0.010 + 0.018 + 0.020 = 0.048

Step 2 — Bayes' Theorem:

P(Premium | Claim) = 0.020/0.048 = 5/12 ≈ 0.417

Premium models make up only 20% of sales overall but account for roughly 42% of warranty claims — the posterior probability shifted upward from the 20% prior precisely because premium models carry the highest claim rate (10%) of the three tiers. As always in a Bayes problem, double-check that the three posterior probabilities (for budget, mid-range, and premium) sum to 1 as a final consistency check.

22. A commuter survey finds that P(uses public transit) = 0.40. Public-transit commuters are 1.5 times as likely to report being satisfied with their daily commute as commuters who drive. Find the probability a randomly selected commuter who reports being satisfied with their commute uses public transit.
Show Answer

Let T = public transit, D = drives, with P(T)=0.40, P(D)=0.60. Let P(satisfied | D) = p, so P(satisfied | T) = 1.5p (the exact value of p will cancel out).

Law of Total Probability: P(Satisfied) = (1.5p)(0.40) + (p)(0.60) = 0.6p + 0.6p = 1.2p

Bayes' Theorem:

P(T | Satisfied) = (1.5p)(0.40) / (1.2p) = 0.6p / 1.2p = 0.6/1.2 = 0.5

Notice p cancels entirely — only the ratio of the two conditional rates matters, not their absolute values.

Part II — Univariate Random Variables (44–50%)

Univariate random variables are the single largest topic on Exam P, accounting for nearly half of the exam. Everything here builds on Part I's probability rules but shifts the focus from events to numerically-valued outcomes. You will learn how to describe a random variable's behavior with a probability mass function (pmf) or probability density function (pdf), summarize it with a cumulative distribution function (CDF), and characterize its "typical value" and "spread" using expected value, variance, and related moments. You will then meet the eleven named distributions the SOA syllabus singles out by name — binomial, Bernoulli, geometric, negative binomial, hypergeometric, Poisson, and discrete uniform on the discrete side; continuous uniform, exponential, gamma, normal, and beta on the continuous side — and learn to recognize which real-world situation each one models. The part closes with the applied engine that turns this theory into exam points on almost every sitting: modeling an insurance loss random variable and the payment random variable an insurer actually disburses once deductibles, coinsurance, and policy limits are applied, including how to compute the mean and variance of that payment. Master this part and you have mastered the exam.

Textbook Cross-Reference for Part II
This Manual Ross, A First Course in Probability (10th ed.) Wackerly/Mendenhall/Scheaffer, Mathematical Statistics with Applications (7th ed.)
Ch. 1 — Random Variables, PMF, PDF & CDF Ch. 4 (§4.1–4.2), Ch. 5 (§5.1) Ch. 3 (§3.1–3.3), Ch. 4 (§4.1–4.2)
Ch. 2 — Expected Value, Moments, Mode, Median & Percentiles Ch. 4 (§4.3–4.5), Ch. 5 (§5.2) Ch. 3 (§3.4–3.5), Ch. 4 (§4.3–4.4)
Ch. 3 — Variance, SD & Coefficient of Variation Ch. 4 (§4.5) Ch. 3 (§3.4), Ch. 4 (§4.4)
Ch. 4 — Binomial, Bernoulli, Geometric & Negative Binomial Ch. 4 (§4.6–4.8, excl. 4.8.4) Ch. 3 (§3.2, 3.5, 3.7)
Ch. 5 — Hypergeometric, Poisson & Discrete Uniform Ch. 4 (§4.6, 4.7) Ch. 3 (§3.6, 3.8)
Ch. 6 — Uniform, Exponential & Gamma Ch. 5 (§5.3–5.5) Ch. 4 (§4.5–4.6)
Ch. 7 — Normal & Beta Ch. 5 (§5.4, 5.6.1) Ch. 4 (§4.6)
Ch. 8–9 — Insurance Applications & Moments of Loss/Payment Ch. 4–5 (applied problems) Ch. 3–4 (applied problems)

The table above maps each chapter of this part to the corresponding sections of the two texts referenced throughout this manual. Note that Ross §4.8.4 and §5.6.2, 5.6.3, 5.6.5, 5.7 are intentionally excluded (topics outside the current SOA syllabus), as is the moment generating function material in Wackerly §3.9 — this manual's treatment of moments (Chapter 2) sticks to raw and central moments computed directly via LOTUS, consistent with the syllabus's exclusion of MGF-based techniques from this topic.

The nine chapters are sequenced deliberately. Chapters 1–3 build the universal toolkit (pmf/pdf/CDF, expected value and moments, variance and its relatives) that applies to every random variable regardless of its name. Chapters 4–5 cover the discrete named distributions, and Chapters 6–7 cover the continuous named distributions — in each pair, master the mean/variance formulas cold, and spend at least as much effort learning to recognize which distribution a word problem describes as you spend on the algebra itself, since recognition is usually the harder half of the problem on exam day. Chapters 8–9 then take every tool from the first seven chapters and apply it to the single most exam-relevant real-world scenario on the syllabus: an insurance company deciding how much to pay a policyholder. Because this topic carries such heavy weight, budget your study time accordingly — a candidate who has only partially mastered Part I but has fully mastered Part II is in a much stronger position than the reverse.

1. Random Variables, PMF, PDF & CDF

Learning Outcomes Covered: (a) Explain and apply the concepts of probability, random variables, probability density functions, and cumulative distribution functions. (b) Calculate conditional probabilities involving random variables.

This chapter lays the vocabulary and notation for the rest of Part II. Every one of the eleven named distributions in Chapters 4 through 7 is nothing more than a specific choice of pmf or pdf, and every insurance-payment calculation in Chapters 8 and 9 is nothing more than a probability or expectation computed from a CDF or density built out of this chapter's tools. Time invested here pays off across the entire remainder of the topic: a candidate who is fluent in translating between pmf/pdf and CDF, and comfortable with the discrete-vs.-continuous distinction, will move through the distribution chapters far faster than one who has to re-derive these mechanics each time.

1.1 What Is a Random Variable?

A random variable (r.v.) is a function that assigns a real number to every outcome in a sample space. Formally, if S is the sample space of a random experiment, a random variable is a function X: S → ℝ. The randomness comes from the underlying experiment; once an outcome s ∈ S occurs, the value X(s) is a fixed number. What makes random variables useful is that they let us stop tracking messy, unstructured outcomes (a die showing "three dots," a policyholder "having an accident") and instead track a number we can add, average, and compare.

Random variables come in two flavors that dominate Exam P:

  • Discrete random variables take on a finite or countably infinite set of values (e.g., 0, 1, 2, 3, …). Counts — number of claims, number of successes, number of defective items — are discrete.
  • Continuous random variables take on any value in an interval (or union of intervals) of real numbers. Measurements — a loss amount, a waiting time, a policy's payout — are typically continuous.
Exam tip: The first thing to do when you read a word problem is decide whether the random variable in question is discrete or continuous. This single decision tells you whether to sum or integrate for the rest of the problem.

1.2 The Probability Mass Function (pmf)

For a discrete random variable X, the probability mass function p(x) = P(X = x) gives the probability that X takes the exact value x.

PMF PROPERTIESp(x) ≥ 0 for all x, and Σx p(x) = 1

The sum is taken over all values x in the support of X (the set of values with positive probability). Any probability question about a discrete r.v. — "what is the chance of at most 2 claims," "what is the chance of an odd number of successes" — reduces to summing p(x) over the relevant values.

Example 1.1

A discrete random variable X has pmf p(x) = c·x for x = 1, 2, 3, 4, and p(x) = 0 otherwise. Find c and compute P(X ≥ 3).

Show Solution

The pmf must sum to 1: c(1) + c(2) + c(3) + c(4) = 10c = 1, so c = 1/10. Then P(X ≥ 3) = p(3) + p(4) = 3/10 + 4/10 = 7/10 = 0.70.

1.3 The Probability Density Function (pdf)

For a continuous random variable, there is no useful analogue of "the probability of an exact value" — indeed, for any continuous X and any single number a, P(X = a) = 0. This is one of the most important and most frequently misunderstood facts on the exam: it means that for continuous random variables, P(X ≤ a) = P(X < a) and P(X ≥ a) = P(X > a) — strict vs. non-strict inequalities never matter for continuous distributions (they very much matter for discrete ones).

Instead, a continuous random variable is described by a probability density function f(x), which gives probability only after being integrated over an interval:

PROBABILITY FROM A PDFP(a ≤ X ≤ b) = ∫ab f(x) dx
PDF PROPERTIESf(x) ≥ 0 for all x, and ∫−∞∞ f(x) dx = 1

Intuitively, f(x) is not a probability itself (it can exceed 1) — it is a probability density: f(x) dx approximates the probability that X falls in a tiny interval of width dx around x.

Common mistake: Students sometimes try to interpret f(x) itself as "the probability of x." It is not — probabilities for continuous variables always come from integrating f over a range, never from evaluating f at a single point.
Example 1.2

A continuous random variable X has pdf f(x) = kx2 for 0 ≤ x ≤ 3, and f(x) = 0 elsewhere. Find k and compute P(1 ≤ X ≤ 2).

Show Solution

Require ∫03 kx2 dx = k·(27/3) = 9k = 1, so k = 1/9. Then P(1 ≤ X ≤ 2) = ∫12 (x2/9) dx = (1/9)[x3/3]12 = (1/9)(8/3 − 1/3) = 7/27 ≈ 0.2593.

1.4 The Cumulative Distribution Function (CDF)

The cumulative distribution function is defined for any random variable, discrete or continuous, as

DEFINITION OF THE CDFF(x) = P(X ≤ x)

For a discrete r.v., F(x) = Σt ≤ x p(t) — a step function that jumps by p(t) at each value t in the support and is flat in between. For a continuous r.v., F(x) = ∫−∞x f(t) dt — a smooth (or at least continuous), non-decreasing curve.

Properties every CDF must satisfy

CDF PROPERTIESF is non-decreasing; F is right-continuous; limx→−∞ F(x) = 0; limx→∞ F(x) = 1

Non-decreasing makes intuitive sense: as you move the threshold x to the right, you can only accumulate more probability, never less. Right-continuity means that at any jump point of a discrete CDF, the function takes the value after the jump (i.e., F(x) already includes P(X = x)). The two limit conditions simply say that eventually all probability has been accounted for (limit 1) and that no probability exists below the lowest possible outcome (limit 0).

The link between F and f (continuous case)

When X is continuous and F is differentiable, the pdf is recovered by differentiating the CDF:

CDF–PDF RELATIONSHIPf(x) = F′(x)

This relationship runs both ways and is tested constantly: given a pdf, integrate to get the CDF; given a CDF, differentiate to get the pdf. Many exam problems describe a distribution by giving F(x) directly (especially for the exponential and Pareto-type families) precisely because differentiating is often easier than the reverse.

Computing interval probabilities from the CDF

INTERVAL PROBABILITYP(a < X ≤ b) = F(b) − F(a)

For continuous X, because single points carry no probability, this also equals P(a ≤ X ≤ b), P(a ≤ X < b), and P(a < X < b) — all four give the identical number. For discrete X you must track the endpoints carefully, since P(a < X ≤ b) ≠ P(a ≤ X ≤ b) in general (they differ by p(a)).

Example 1.3

A loss random variable X has CDF F(x) = 1 − e−x²/50 for x ≥ 0. (a) Verify this is a valid CDF on [0, ∞). (b) Find the pdf. (c) Compute P(5 < X ≤ 10).

Show Solution

(a) F(0) = 1 − e0 = 0, F(x) → 1 as x → ∞, and F is increasing since x²/50 is increasing in x for x ≥ 0 — all CDF conditions hold.

(b) Differentiate: f(x) = F′(x) = e−x²/50·(2x/50) = (x/25)e−x²/50, for x ≥ 0.

(c) P(5 < X ≤ 10) = F(10) − F(5) = (1 − e−2) − (1 − e−0.5) = e−0.5 − e−2 ≈ 0.6065 − 0.1353 = 0.4712.

1.5 Conditional Probabilities Involving Random Variables

Conditional probability rules from Part I carry over directly when the events are described in terms of a random variable. If A = {X ∈ B} and C = {X ∈ D} for sets B, D, then

CONDITIONAL PROBABILITY WITH AN R.V.P(X ∈ B | X ∈ D) = P(X ∈ B ∩ D) / P(X ∈ D)

A very common exam pattern conditions on "the loss exceeds some threshold" and asks for the probability or expected value of some further event — this is central to insurance applications in Chapters 8–9, where quantities like "given that a loss exceeded the deductible" appear repeatedly.

Example 1.4

Losses X have pdf f(x) = 2x for 0 < x < 1 (and 0 elsewhere). Given that a loss exceeds 0.5, find the probability that it also exceeds 0.8.

Show Solution

We need P(X > 0.8 | X > 0.5) = P(X > 0.8)/P(X > 0.5) since {X>0.8} ⊂ {X>0.5}. Using the CDF F(x) = x² (found by integrating f): P(X>0.8) = 1 − 0.64 = 0.36 and P(X>0.5) = 1 − 0.25 = 0.75. So the answer is 0.36/0.75 = 0.48.

Exam tip: Whenever you see "given that a loss exceeds a threshold," first write the survival function S(x) = 1 − F(x) = P(X>x). Conditional survival probabilities beyond a threshold are simply ratios of survival function values, and for the exponential distribution this ratio simplifies dramatically because of the memoryless property (Chapter 6).

1.5b Right-Continuity of a Discrete CDF, Illustrated

The abstract property "F is right-continuous" is easiest to internalize with a concrete discrete example. Suppose X takes values 1, 2, 3 with probabilities 0.2, 0.5, 0.3. Its CDF is the step function

F(x) = 0 for x<1; F(x)=0.2 for 1≤x<2; F(x)=0.7 for 2≤x<3; F(x)=1 for x≥3.

At the jump point x=2, the function takes the value after the jump, F(2)=0.7, not the value approaching from the left (0.2). This is precisely what "right-continuous" means graphically: at each jump, the function's plotted value belongs with the flat piece to its right, and the CDF is drawn with filled-in dots on the right end of each step and open circles on the left end. Whenever you need P(X=2) from this CDF, you compute it as the size of the jump at x=2: F(2) − F(2⁻) = 0.7 − 0.2 = 0.5, matching p(2) directly.

1.6 The Survival Function

It is worth formally naming the complement of the CDF, since it recurs constantly in loss-model and insurance calculations: the survival function (or reliability function) is

SURVIVAL FUNCTIONS(x) = 1 − F(x) = P(X > x)

S(x) answers "what is the probability the variable exceeds x?" — for a loss variable, this is literally the probability that a claim is large enough to pierce a given threshold, which is exactly the calculation needed whenever a deductible or attachment point is involved. S inherits mirror-image properties from F: it is non-increasing, S(−∞)=1, S(∞)=0, and for a continuous r.v., S′(x) = −f(x).

1.7 Mixed and Piecewise-Defined Random Variables

Not every random variable relevant to Exam P is purely discrete or purely continuous. A very common construction in the insurance-applications chapters (8 and 9) is a mixed random variable: continuous over part of its range but with a discrete "spike" of probability at one or more specific points — most often at zero. For example, if a policy has a deductible d and the underlying loss X is continuous, the payment Y = max(X−d, 0) is a mixed random variable: it has a point mass P(Y=0) = P(X≤d) = F(d), and a continuous density over y > 0 inherited (with a shift) from the density of X.

For a mixed random variable, the CDF is still well defined and still satisfies all the usual CDF properties, but it will have (a) a jump exactly at the point(s) of positive probability mass and (b) a smooth, continuously increasing piece elsewhere. Expected values of mixed variables are computed by summing the discrete piece(s) and integrating the continuous piece(s) separately, then adding the results — this is exactly the technique used throughout Chapters 8 and 9 whenever a deductible creates a point mass at a zero payment.

Example 1.5

A loss X has pdf f(x) = 0.1e−0.1x for x>0 (Exponential, mean 10). A policy has a deductible of 5, so the payment is Y = max(X−5,0). Find P(Y=0) and write the CDF of Y for y > 0.

Show Solution

P(Y=0) = P(X≤5) = F_X(5) = 1−e−0.5 ≈ 0.3935 — this is the discrete "spike" of the mixed distribution of Y.

For y>0: P(Y≤y) = P(X≤y+5) = 1−e−0.1(y+5). This is the continuous piece; note that F_Y jumps from 0 at y=0⁻ to 0.3935 at y=0, then rises continuously afterward — a textbook mixed-distribution CDF.

1.9 Finding the Distribution of a Function of X

A skill that recurs throughout this manual (it is exactly how the exponential-to-gamma and uniform-sum relationships of later chapters are justified) is finding the CDF of Y=g(X) given the distribution of X, when g is monotonic (strictly increasing or strictly decreasing). The technique is always the same: write F_Y(y) = P(Y≤y) = P(g(X)≤y), then solve the inequality inside for X (flipping the inequality if g is decreasing), and evaluate using F_X.

CDF OF Y=g(X), g INCREASINGF_Y(y) = F_X(g−1(y))
CDF OF Y=g(X), g DECREASINGF_Y(y) = 1 − F_X(g−1(y))

Once F_Y is known, f_Y(y) = F_Y′(y) recovers the density if needed (this is the basis of the classical "change of variables" density formula, which multiplies by the Jacobian |dx/dy| — a shortcut for exactly this two-step process, but the two-step process itself always works and requires no separate formula to memorize).

Example 1.6

Let X ~ Uniform(0,1), and let Y = −ln(X) (an increasing transformation of −X, i.e. a strictly decreasing function of X itself, since larger X gives smaller Y). Find the distribution of Y.

Show Solution

Y ranges over (0,∞) as X ranges over (0,1). For y>0: F_Y(y) = P(−\ln X ≤ y) = P(\ln X ≥ −y) = P(X ≥ e^{-y}) = 1−F_X(e^{-y}) = 1 − e^{-y} (using F_X(x)=x for Uniform(0,1), and noting g is decreasing so we use the second boxed formula).

This is exactly the CDF of an Exponential(rate 1) distribution! This transformation — −\ln(\text{Uniform}(0,1)) is Exponential(1) — is in fact the standard method computers use internally to simulate exponential random variables from uniform ones, and it is a clean illustration of the monotonic-transformation technique.

1.9b A Second Change-of-Variables Example

Example 1.7

Let X be Exponential(rate λ), and let Y=X² (an increasing function of X on X≥0, since squaring is increasing for non-negative inputs). Find the pdf of Y.

Show Solution

F_Y(y) = P(Y≤y) = P(X²≤y) = P(X≤√y) = F_X(√y) = 1−e^{-λ√y}, for y≥0.

Differentiate to get the density: f_Y(y) = F_Y′(y) = λe^{-λ√y}·\frac{1}{2\sqrt{y}} = \frac{λ}{2\sqrt{y}}e^{-λ\sqrt{y}}, for y>0.

Notice this is not one of the eleven named distributions in this manual — it is a legitimate, if unfamiliar, continuous distribution in its own right (related to the Weibull family). The two-step "find F_Y, then differentiate" technique works regardless of whether the resulting distribution has a name, which is precisely its value: it never requires you to recognize the answer in advance.

1.11 Chapter Summary

Key Definitions: Chapter 1
Object Discrete X Continuous X
Describes probabilities via pmf p(x)=P(X=x) pdf f(x), with P(X=x)=0
Normalization Σp(x)=1 ∫f(x)dx=1
CDF step function, right-continuous continuous, non-decreasing
CDF ↔ density link p(x)=F(x)−F(x⁻) (jump size) f(x)=F′(x)
Survival function S(x)=1−F(x) S(x)=1−F(x)

2. Expected Value, Moments, Mode, Median & Percentiles

Learning Outcomes Covered: (c) Explain and calculate expected values, including moments, mode, median, and percentiles.

Expected value is the single most heavily used number on this exam — it is the ingredient in the mean/variance formulas of every named distribution in Chapters 4–7, and it is the entire engine behind every "expected claim payment" calculation in Chapters 8 and 9. The law of the unconscious statistician (Section 2.2) in particular deserves special attention: it is the technical fact that makes it possible to compute the expected value of an insurance payment — a complicated, piecewise function of the loss — without ever needing to find the distribution of the payment itself. Every subsequent chapter leans on this chapter's tools constantly, so build fluency with LOTUS, mode, median, and percentiles before moving on.

2.1 Expected Value

The expected value (mean) of a random variable is its probability-weighted average — the long-run average value you'd observe if the experiment were repeated indefinitely.

EXPECTED VALUE — DISCRETEE[X] = Σx x·p(x)
EXPECTED VALUE — CONTINUOUSE[X] = ∫−∞∞ x·f(x) dx

Both formulas say the same thing: multiply each possible value by its probability (mass or density) and add (or integrate) over all values.

2.2 The Law of the Unconscious Statistician (LOTUS)

Often you need the expected value not of X itself but of some function of it, such as X², etX, or a payout function like min(X, u). The remarkable fact — usually called the law of the unconscious statistician because it is used so automatically — is that you do not need to find the distribution of g(X) first. You can compute its expectation directly from the distribution of X:

LOTUSE[g(X)] = Σx g(x)·p(x) (discrete) or E[g(X)] = ∫ g(x)·f(x) dx (continuous)

This single tool is the backbone of Chapters 8 and 9: computing the expected insurance payment E[g(X)] where g encodes the deductible/coinsurance/limit structure, without ever finding the distribution of the payment itself.

2.3 Linearity of Expectation

LINEARITYE[aX + b] = a·E[X] + b, for any constants a, b

This holds regardless of whether X is discrete or continuous, and regardless of the shape of its distribution — no independence or special structure is required, because expectation is fundamentally a weighted sum/integral, which is linear. Linearity extends to sums of several (possibly dependent) random variables, E[X + Y] = E[X] + E[Y], a fact used heavily in Part III but worth remembering here as well.

2.4 Raw Moments and Central Moments

The kth raw moment of X is E[Xk]; the ordinary mean is the first raw moment. The kth central moment is E[(X − E[X])k] — a moment taken about the mean rather than about zero. The most important central moment is the second one, the variance, covered fully in Chapter 3.

RAW MOMENTE[Xk] = Σ xkp(x) or ∫ xkf(x) dx
CENTRAL MOMENTE[(X−μ)k] = Σ (x−μ)kp(x) or ∫ (x−μ)kf(x) dx, μ = E[X]
Example 2.1

A discrete random variable has pmf p(1) = 0.2, p(2) = 0.5, p(3) = 0.3. Find E[X], E[X²], and E[3X − 4].

Show Solution

E[X] = 1(0.2) + 2(0.5) + 3(0.3) = 0.2 + 1.0 + 0.9 = 2.1.

E[X²] = 1(0.2) + 4(0.5) + 9(0.3) = 0.2 + 2.0 + 2.7 = 4.9 (by LOTUS with g(x) = x²).

E[3X − 4] = 3(2.1) − 4 = 2.3 by linearity.

Example 2.2

A continuous loss variable has pdf f(x) = 3x² on (0, 1). An insurer pays Y = min(X, 0.5). Find E[Y] using LOTUS.

Show Solution

Split the integral at the cap: E[Y] = ∫00.5 x·3x² dx + ∫0.51 0.5·3x² dx

= 3∫00.5 x³ dx + 1.5∫0.51 x² dx = 3[x⁴/4]00.5 + 1.5[x³/3]0.51

= 3(0.0625/4) + 1.5[(1/3) − (0.125/3)] = 0.046875 + 1.5(0.291667) = 0.046875 + 0.4375 = 0.484375.

This "min-with-a-cap" trick is exactly the limited expected value used throughout Chapter 9.

2.4b Skewness: Using the Third Central Moment

The third central moment, E[(X−μ)³], measures the asymmetry of a distribution. Because odd powers of negative deviations stay negative, a distribution with a long right tail (more extreme values above the mean than below it) has a positive third central moment, and a distribution with a long left tail has a negative one; a perfectly symmetric distribution (normal, uniform) has a third central moment of exactly zero. To make this measure comparable across distributions of different scales, it is standardized by dividing by σ³, giving the (unitless) skewness coefficient:

SKEWNESS COEFFICIENTγ = E[(X−μ)³] / σ³

Loss severity distributions on this exam — exponential, gamma, and lognormal-type models in general — are essentially always right-skewed (γ>0): most losses are small to moderate, but a long right tail of rare, very large losses pulls the mean above the median, exactly as observed numerically in Example 2.4 and Section 2.6. Recognizing this pattern is a useful sanity check: if your calculated mean for a loss distribution comes out below the median, revisit your work, since right skew — mean above median — is the overwhelmingly typical case for the severity models tested here (the normal is the notable symmetric exception, and even it is sometimes a poor real-world fit for losses precisely because real losses are skewed).

2.5 Mode

The mode is the value (or values) at which the pmf/pdf attains its maximum — the "most likely" outcome for discrete variables, or the peak of the density for continuous ones. A distribution can be unimodal (one mode), bimodal, or have no unique mode (e.g., a uniform distribution, where every value in the support is equally likely, so the "mode" is not unique in the usual sense).

Exam tip: To find the mode of a discrete distribution like Poisson or Binomial, look at the ratio p(k)/p(k−1); the pmf is increasing while this ratio exceeds 1 and decreasing once it drops below 1, so the mode sits at the crossover point. For Poisson(λ), the mode is ⌊λ⌋ (and also λ−1 when λ is an integer, giving two modes).
Example 2.3

Find the mode of a Poisson distribution with mean 2.5.

Show Solution

For Poisson(λ), p(k)/p(k−1) = λ/k. This ratio exceeds 1 (pmf still rising) when k < λ = 2.5, i.e., for k = 1, 2, and drops below 1 for k ≥ 3. So p(0) < p(1) < p(2) > p(3) > ⋯, and the mode is k = 2 = ⌊2.5⌋. (This matches SOA sample question #358 — see the Part II practice set.)

2.6 Median and Percentiles

The 100pth percentile (or p-quantile) of X, denoted πp, is a value satisfying F(πp) = p (for continuous distributions where F is strictly increasing, this is unique; for discrete distributions percentiles require care — see below). The median is simply the 50th percentile, π0.5, satisfying F(π0.5) = 0.5.

PERCENTILE (CONTINUOUS X)Solve F(πp) = p for πp, i.e., πp = F−1(p)

Percentiles when the CDF has no closed-form inverse

Some distributions (most notably the normal and gamma) do not have an elementary closed-form CDF, so you cannot algebraically invert F. For the normal distribution, this is handled by standardizing and reading percentiles off the standard normal table (Chapter 7). For other distributions without closed forms, exam problems will either supply enough numerical information to avoid needing the true inverse, or will ask for a mode/median that happens to have a nicer closed form than the general percentile.

Percentiles for discrete distributions

Because a discrete CDF is a step function, there may be a whole interval of values x with F(x) = p exactly, or no value at all with F(x) exactly equal to p. The standard convention (and the one Exam P uses) is: the 100pth percentile is the smallest value x such that F(x) ≥ p.

Example 2.4

Losses are exponentially distributed with mean 4, so F(x) = 1 − e−x/4. Find the median loss and the 90th percentile.

Show Solution

Median: solve 1 − e−m/4 = 0.5 ⟹ e−m/4 = 0.5 ⟹ m = −4 ln(0.5) = 4 ln 2 ≈ 2.773.

90th percentile: solve 1 − e−x/4 = 0.9 ⟹ e−x/4 = 0.1 ⟹ x = −4 ln(0.1) = 4 ln 10 ≈ 9.210.

Notice the median (2.773) is well below the mean (4) — this right-skew, where mean > median, is typical of loss distributions and a hallmark of the exponential and related severity distributions.

Common mistake: Do not confuse the median with the mean. For skewed distributions (essentially all loss severity distributions on this exam) they are different, sometimes very different. Only for symmetric distributions (normal, uniform) does mean = median.

2.7 Existence of Moments

Not every distribution has a finite mean or variance — heavy-tailed distributions can have infinite moments. On Exam P, all the named distributions in the syllabus (binomial, Poisson, exponential, gamma, normal, beta, etc.) have finite moments of all orders, so this is mostly a conceptual point: always check that the defining sum/integral actually converges before trusting a mean/variance formula, especially for distributions defined only by a pdf/pmf formula in a word problem rather than by name.

2.8 Percentiles for Non-Invertible or Piecewise CDFs

When a distribution's CDF is piecewise (for example, a mixed loss/payment variable as in Section 1.7, or a distribution defined by different formulas on different intervals), finding a percentile requires first identifying which piece of the CDF the target probability falls into, and only then inverting that piece. This two-step process — locate the interval, then invert locally — is the standard technique for percentile problems that look intimidating at first glance.

Example 2.5

A random variable has CDF F(x) = x/4 for 0≤x≤2, and F(x) = x²/16 for 2 < x ≤ 4 (you may verify this is continuous and non-decreasing, with F(4)=1). Find the median.

Show Solution

First check which piece contains F=0.5. At x=2, the first piece gives F(2)=2/4=0.5 exactly — so the median is precisely x=2, right at the boundary between the two pieces. (Had the target probability been, say, 0.7, we would have needed to invert the second piece instead: x²/16=0.7 ⟹ x=√11.2≈3.347.)

2.9 Mode for Continuous Distributions

For a continuous random variable, the mode is found using ordinary calculus: differentiate f(x), set the derivative to zero, and confirm a maximum (or, for pdfs that are monotonic across their whole support, the mode is simply the relevant endpoint). This mirrors the ratio-test technique used for discrete pmfs in Section 2.5, but uses derivatives instead of ratios of consecutive terms.

Example 2.6

A loss has pdf f(x) = 6x(1−x) for 0<x<1 (a Beta(2,2) shape). Find the mode.

Show Solution

f′(x) = 6(1−2x). Setting f′(x)=0 gives x=1/2. Since f′>0 for x<1/2 and f′<0 for x>1/2, this is indeed a maximum. Mode = 0.5 (as expected by the symmetry of a Beta(2,2) density about 1/2).

2.10 Worked Example Combining Several Concepts

Example 2.7

A claim size X (in thousands) has pdf f(x) = (3/64)x² for 0≤x≤4. Find (a) E[X], (b) E[X²], (c) the median, and (d) E[X³] (the third raw moment).

Show Solution

First find the CDF for later use: F(x) = ∫₀ˣ(3/64)t²dt = x³/64 for 0≤x≤4 (check: F(4)=64/64=1 ✓).

(a) E[X] = ∫₀⁴ x(3/64)x²dx = (3/64)∫₀⁴x³dx = (3/64)(256/4) = (3/64)(64) = 3.

(b) E[X²] = (3/64)∫₀⁴x⁴dx = (3/64)(1024/5) = 3072/320 = 9.6.

(c) Median: solve m³/64 = 0.5 ⟹ m³=32 ⟹ m = 32^{1/3} ≈ 3.1748.

(d) E[X³] = (3/64)∫₀⁴x⁵dx = (3/64)(4096/6) = 12288/384 = 32.

Notice again that the mean (3) exceeds the median (≈3.175)... actually here the median slightly exceeds the mean, illustrating that skewness direction is not automatic — always compute both rather than assuming loss distributions are always right-skewed with mean above median (that pattern is typical, e.g., for the exponential, but is not a universal law).

2.10b Expectation of a Mixed Random Variable

Building on the mixed-variable idea from Section 1.7, the expected value of a mixed random variable is computed by adding a discrete-style sum over the point mass(es) to a continuous-style integral over the continuous part. If Y has a point mass P(Y=0)=q and a continuous density g(y) for y>0, then

EXPECTATION OF A MIXED VARIABLEE[Y] = 0·q + ∫0∞ y·g(y) dy

Because the point mass sits at zero in almost every insurance application (a policy that pays nothing when the loss is below the deductible), the discrete term usually contributes nothing to the mean — but it still must be accounted for when computing E[Y²] for a variance, since squaring does not eliminate a term that is already zero, and — more importantly — when computing probabilities like P(Y=0) directly, or percentiles of Y, where the point mass absolutely matters (see Chapter 9).

Example 2.8

A device either works perfectly (probability 0.3, contributing a repair cost of exactly 0) or fails, in which case the repair cost is Uniform on (0, 500) (probability 0.7 of failing). Let Y be the repair cost. Find E[Y].

Show Solution

E[Y] = 0(0.3) + 0.7·E[\text{Uniform}(0,500)] = 0.7(250) = 175.

2.10c Expectation via the Survival Function (a Preview of Chapter 9)

For a non-negative random variable X (as every loss variable is), there is an elegant alternative formula for the mean that avoids ever writing down x·f(x) at all — it uses only the survival function S(x)=1−F(x) introduced conceptually in Section 1.6:

EXPECTATION FROM THE SURVIVAL FUNCTION (X ≥ 0)E[X] = ∫0∞ S(x) dx = ∫0∞ [1−F(x)] dx

Derivation

Start from E[X] = ∫₀^∞ x f(x)dx and write x = ∫₀ˣ 1\, dt (i.e., x is the length of the interval from 0 to x). Substituting and switching the order of integration (a standard Fubini-type swap):

E[X] = ∫₀^∞ ∫₀ˣ 1\,dt\, f(x)\,dx = ∫₀^∞ ∫_t^∞ f(x)\,dx\,dt = ∫₀^∞ S(t)\,dt

(the inner region "0≤t≤x" is re-described as "for each t, integrate over all x≥t," which is exactly S(t)=P(X>t)). This identity is the exact continuous-variable parent of the limited-expected-value formula E[X∧u]=∫₀ᵘ S(x)dx used throughout Chapter 9 — the limited expected value is simply this same integral truncated at u instead of run out to infinity.

Example 2.9

Use the survival-function formula to find E[X] for X ~ Exponential(θ), confirming the known result E[X]=θ.

Show Solution

S(x) = e^{-x/θ}. E[X] = ∫₀^∞ e^{-x/θ}dx = [−θe^{-x/θ}]₀^∞ = 0−(−θ) = θ. ✓ Matches the standard exponential mean, confirmed via a completely different route (no need to ever multiply by x).

2.10d A Percentile Calculation for a Mixed Random Variable

Example 2.10

A device either works with no repair cost (probability 0.4) or fails and requires a repair cost C that is Exponential with mean 200 (probability 0.6 of failing). Let Y denote the (possibly zero) repair cost. Find the median of Y, and find its 75th percentile.

Show Solution

First identify where the point mass sits relative to the target percentiles. P(Y=0)=0.4, so F_Y(0)=0.4 — the CDF jumps immediately to 0.4 at y=0 and only then begins rising continuously.

Median (50th percentile): since F_Y(0)=0.4 < 0.5, the median lies in the continuous piece, at a value y>0 solving F_Y(y) = 0.4 + 0.6·F_C(y) = 0.5, i.e. F_C(y) = 0.1/0.6 = 1/6. For Exponential(200): 1−e^{-y/200}=1/6 ⟹ y = −200\ln(5/6) ≈ −200(−0.1823) ≈ 36.5.

75th percentile: similarly, solve 0.4+0.6F_C(y)=0.75 ⟹ F_C(y)=0.35/0.6≈0.5833. 1−e^{-y/200}=0.5833 ⟹ y=−200\ln(0.4167)≈−200(−0.8755)≈175.1.

The general technique: whenever a target percentile p exceeds the point-mass probability at zero, rescale the "remaining" probability (p−P(Y=0))/(1−P(Y=0)) into the correct percentile of the continuous piece alone, then solve that piece's CDF as usual — exactly the same rescaling idea used for percentiles of insurance payments in Chapter 9.

2.10e A Combined Discrete Expectation Example

Example 2.11

The number of claims N filed by a policyholder in a year has pmf P(N=0)=0.6, P(N=1)=0.3, P(N=2)=0.1. Each claim (independent of the others and of N) costs the insurer exactly 500 to process, regardless of the claim's dollar size. Find the expected total processing cost for a policyholder in a year, and find the variance of the number of claims.

Show Solution

Total processing cost C = 500N, so by linearity E[C] = 500·E[N]. E[N] = 0(0.6)+1(0.3)+2(0.1) = 0.3+0.2=0.5. E[C] = 500(0.5)=250.

For the variance of N: E[N²] = 0(0.6)+1(0.3)+4(0.1)=0.3+0.4=0.7. Var(N) = 0.7−0.25=0.45.

(By the Chapter 3 scaling rule, Var(C)=500²(0.45)=112,500, so SD(C)≈335.4 — a preview of exactly the kind of calculation that appears throughout Chapters 8–9 whenever a fixed cost per event is combined with a random event count.)

2.13 Chapter Summary

E[g(X)] (LOTUS) Σg(x)·p(x) ∫g(x)·f(x)dx

3. Variance, Standard Deviation & Coefficient of Variation

Learning Outcomes Covered: (d) Explain and calculate variance, standard deviation, and coefficient of variation.

If expected value answers "what is the typical value?", variance answers "how confident should we be in that typical value, and how much could reality deviate from it?" — a question of central importance to an insurer setting premiums and holding capital against unexpectedly large claims. This chapter is short relative to Chapters 2 and 8–9, but its two central formulas (the variance shortcut and the linear-transformation rule) are used, without exception, in essentially every remaining chapter of Part II — whenever a mean and variance of a named distribution are quoted, or a payment variable's variance is computed under a deductible or coinsurance arrangement, these are the rules doing the work behind the scenes.

3.1 Definition of Variance

Variance measures how spread out a distribution is around its mean — the average squared distance from the mean.

VARIANCE — DEFINITIONVar(X) = E[(X − μ)²], where μ = E[X]

3.2 The Shortcut (Computational) Formula

Expanding the square inside the expectation gives the formula almost always used in practice:

VARIANCE — SHORTCUT FORMULAVar(X) = E[X²] − (E[X])²

Derivation

Starting from the definition and expanding (X − μ)² = X² − 2μX + μ², then applying linearity of expectation (noting μ is a constant):

Var(X) = E[X² − 2μX + μ²] = E[X²] − 2μE[X] + μ² = E[X²] − 2μ·μ + μ² = E[X²] − μ²

Since μ = E[X], this is exactly E[X²] − (E[X])². This formula is used far more often on the exam than the raw definition, because it only requires the first two raw moments — both directly computable from the pmf/pdf via LOTUS — rather than requiring you to first find μ and then integrate/sum a squared deviation.

Example 3.1

Using the distribution of Example 2.1 (p(1)=0.2, p(2)=0.5, p(3)=0.3, so E[X]=2.1 and E[X²]=4.9), find Var(X) and the standard deviation.

Show Solution

Var(X) = E[X²] − (E[X])² = 4.9 − (2.1)² = 4.9 − 4.41 = 0.49.

Standard deviation: σ = √0.49 = 0.7.

3.3 Standard Deviation

STANDARD DEVIATIONσ = SD(X) = √Var(X)

Standard deviation is preferred over variance for communicating spread because it is in the same units as X itself (variance is in squared units). If X is a loss in dollars, Var(X) is in dollars², which is not directly interpretable, while σ is in dollars and can be compared directly to the mean.

3.4 Variance Under Linear Transformations

VARIANCE OF aX + bVar(aX + b) = a²·Var(X)

Derivation

Let Y = aX + b. Then E[Y] = aE[X] + b by linearity. So

Var(Y) = E[(Y − E[Y])²] = E[(aX + b − aE[X] − b)²] = E[(a(X − E[X]))²] = a²E[(X−E[X])²] = a²Var(X)

Two consequences worth memorizing: adding a constant b never changes variance (shifting a distribution doesn't stretch or compress it), and multiplying by a constant a scales the variance by a² — and hence scales the standard deviation by |a| (not a²).

Common mistake: Do not scale standard deviation by a² — that error comes from confusing SD(aX) with Var(aX). Correctly, SD(aX+b) = |a|·SD(X), while Var(aX+b) = a²Var(X).
Example 3.2

A loss X has Var(X) = 500. Due to inflation, next year's loss is modeled as X′ = 1.06X (a 6% trend factor). Find Var(X′) and SD(X′).

Show Solution

Var(X′) = (1.06)²(500) = 1.1236(500) = 561.8. SD(X′) = 1.06·√500 = 1.06(22.36) ≈ 23.70. This inflation-trending pattern reappears in Chapter 8.

3.5 Coefficient of Variation

The coefficient of variation (CV) is a unitless, relative measure of spread — the standard deviation expressed as a fraction (or percentage) of the mean:

COEFFICIENT OF VARIATIONCV(X) = σ / μ = SD(X) / E[X] (requires μ > 0)

The CV is useful precisely because raw standard deviations of two variables measured on different scales cannot be compared meaningfully. A standard deviation of $10,000 sounds enormous for a policy with mean loss $500, but modest for one with mean loss $1,000,000. The CV strips out the scale and lets you compare relative riskiness directly.

Example 3.3

Policy A has mean loss 500 and standard deviation 300. Policy B has mean loss 50,000 and standard deviation 20,000. Which policy is relatively riskier, as measured by CV?

Show Solution

CVA = 300/500 = 0.60. CVB = 20,000/50,000 = 0.40. Even though Policy B has a vastly larger absolute standard deviation, Policy A is relatively riskier — its losses are more variable relative to their own size.

Exam tip: CV is invariant under scaling: CV(aX) = CV(X) for a > 0, since both σ and μ scale by a. But CV is not invariant under adding a constant, since CV(X+b) = σ/(μ+b) ≠ σ/μ in general. This is exactly why CV is meaningful for comparing pure multiplicative scale (e.g., currency conversion, inflation) but not for shifted variables.

3.6 Variance of a Sum of Independent Random Variables

Although full coverage of joint distributions belongs to Part III, one fact about variance is used so often within univariate problems (sums of iid claims, aggregate counts, totals of independent policy payments) that it belongs here as well:

VARIANCE OF A SUM OF INDEPENDENT VARIABLESIf X₁,…,Xₙ are independent, then Var(X₁+⋯+Xₙ) = Var(X₁)+⋯+Var(Xₙ)

Unlike expectation, which is additive whether or not the variables are independent, variance is additive across a sum only when the summands are independent (or, more generally, uncorrelated) — with dependent variables, covariance terms appear (Part III). This fact is exactly why, e.g., the variance of a Binomial(n,p) is np(1−p): a binomial is literally a sum of n iid Bernoulli(p) variables, each contributing variance p(1−p), and the totals add.

Example 3.4

A book of business has 50 independent, identical policies, each with mean annual loss 800 and standard deviation 300. Find the mean and standard deviation of the total annual loss across all 50 policies.

Show Solution

Let T = X₁+⋯+X₅₀. E[T] = 50(800) = 40,000 (linearity, no independence needed).

Var(T) = 50(300²) = 50(90,000) = 4,500,000 (variance addition, independence needed here). SD(T) = √4,500,000 ≈ 2121.3.

Notice SD(T) ≈ 2121.3 is far less than 50 × 300 = 15,000 — pooling many independent risks grows the standard deviation only like √n, not like n, which is the mathematical basis of insurance pooling/diversification.

3.7 Chebyshev's Inequality (a Variance-Based Probability Bound)

Variance also gives a distribution-free bound on how much probability can lie far from the mean, useful when only the mean and variance are known (not the full distribution):

CHEBYSHEV'S INEQUALITYP(|X − μ| ≥ kσ) ≤ 1/k², for any k > 0

Chebyshev's inequality holds for any distribution with finite variance — no assumption of normality or symmetry required — which makes it much weaker (looser) than the empirical rule of Chapter 7, but universally valid. It is a useful conceptual bridge between "variance is large" and "probability is spread far from the mean," and occasionally appears directly on the exam when a problem gives only a mean and variance and asks for a bound rather than an exact probability.

Example 3.5

A random variable has mean 100 and standard deviation 10, but its distribution is otherwise unspecified. Give an upper bound on P(|X−100| ≥ 25).

Show Solution

Here kσ = 25 with σ=10, so k=2.5. Chebyshev gives P(|X−100|≥25) ≤ 1/(2.5)² = 1/6.25 = 0.16.

3.7b Coefficient of Variation of an Insurance Payment

A preview of the Chapter 8–9 material: because a deductible removes small losses entirely (setting the payment to exactly 0 for those losses) while leaving larger losses only partially reduced, imposing a deductible typically increases the coefficient of variation of the payment relative to the coefficient of variation of the original loss — the remaining payments are, relatively speaking, more spread out around their (now smaller) mean, because the policy has selectively filtered out the low-variance, near-certain small claims and left mostly the more unpredictable larger ones.

Example 3.6

A loss X has mean 500 and standard deviation 500 (so CV(X)=1). After a deductible is applied, the resulting payment Y has mean 200 and standard deviation 260. Compare CV(Y) to CV(X) and interpret.

Show Solution

CV(Y) = 260/200 = 1.30, compared to CV(X)=500/500=1.00. As expected, the payment is relatively more variable than the original loss — the deductible trimmed away the more predictable small losses, leaving a payment distribution that is proportionally more volatile around its own (smaller) mean.

3.7c A Chebyshev Bound Applied to an Insurance Context

Example 3.7

An insurer's aggregate annual claims have mean 2,000,000 and standard deviation 250,000, but the exact distribution is unknown (perhaps too new a line of business to have a well-fitted severity/frequency model). Using only Chebyshev's inequality, bound the probability that aggregate claims exceed 2,750,000.

Show Solution

Note 2,750,000 = 2,000,000 + 3(250,000), exactly 3 standard deviations above the mean. Chebyshev bounds the two-sided probability: P(|X−μ|≥3σ) ≤ 1/9 ≈ 0.1111. Since we only need the upper tail (aggregate claims exceeding, not just deviating from, the mean by 3σ) and Chebyshev gives no guarantee of symmetry, the most we can say without further assumptions is P(X>2,750,000) ≤ 1/9 ≈ 0.1111 (the full two-sided bound, since the one-sided bound is generally no tighter without extra structure).

Contrast this with what the empirical rule (Chapter 7) would suggest if claims happened to be exactly normal: P(X>μ+3σ) ≈ 0.0013, dramatically smaller than the Chebyshev bound of 0.111. This gap illustrates exactly why Chebyshev is called "distribution-free" — it must remain valid for every possible distribution with the given mean and variance, including pathological ones far more spread out in the tails than the normal, so it necessarily sacrifices a great deal of precision for that universality.

3.8b Comparing Variance Across Two Candidate Severity Models

Example 3.8

An actuary is choosing between two candidate models for a new liability line, both calibrated to the same mean of 5,000: Model 1 is Exponential(mean 5,000); Model 2 is Gamma(α=4, θ=1,250) (note αθ=4(1250)=5000, matching means). Compare the two models' variances and standard deviations, and discuss which is "riskier" by the coefficient of variation.

Show Solution

Model 1: Var = θ² = 5000² = 25,000,000, SD=5000, CV = 5000/5000 = 1.

Model 2: Var = αθ² = 4(1250²) = 4(1,562,500) = 6,250,000, SD = 2500, CV = 2500/5000 = 0.5.

Even with identical means, Model 1 (the exponential) has exactly double the standard deviation and double the coefficient of variation of Model 2 (the gamma with shape 4) — a direct illustration of the general gamma fact from Section 6.7b that increasing the shape parameter α (while proportionally shrinking θ to hold the mean fixed) systematically reduces relative variability. The exponential, as the α=1 boundary case, is the most "relatively risky" member of the gamma family for any given mean.

3.8c A Quick Diagnostic for Recognizing Poisson-Like Data

Because Var(X) = E[X] is a defining property of the Poisson distribution (Section 5.2), a quick and commonly tested diagnostic works in reverse: if a word problem states or implies that a count variable's variance equals its mean, that is a strong verbal signal the intended model is Poisson, even if the word "Poisson" never appears explicitly in the problem. Conversely, if a stated variance is noticeably larger than the stated mean for a count variable, this signals "overdispersion" relative to a Poisson model — a pattern more consistent with a negative binomial count (Chapter 4), whose variance r(1−p)/p² always exceeds its mean r(1−p)/p by a factor of 1/p > 1. Keep this mean-versus-variance comparison in your toolkit as a fast way to narrow down which discrete distribution a problem is describing before committing to a formula.

3.11 Chapter Summary

Key Formulas: Chapter 3
Quantity Formula
Variance (definition) E[(X−μ)²]
Variance (shortcut) E[X²] − (E[X])²
Standard deviation √Var(X)
Var(aX+b) a²Var(X)
SD(aX+b) |a|·SD(X)
Coefficient of variation σ/μ

4. Discrete Distributions I: Binomial, Bernoulli, Geometric & Negative Binomial

Learning Outcomes Covered: Understand discrete univariate distributions including binomial and geometric (and negative binomial) and their applications; explain and calculate expected values and variance for these families.

With Chapters 1–3's tools in hand, Part II now turns to the named distributions the SOA syllabus lists explicitly. This chapter and the next carry substantial exam weight on their own: a very large share of Part II questions simply describe a scenario in words and expect you to recognize which named distribution applies, quote its mean/variance (or a specific probability) from memory or a quick formula application, and move on. Binomial and its relatives (Bernoulli, geometric, negative binomial) form a tightly connected family, all built from repeated independent trials — understanding how they relate to one another (Bernoulli as the atomic building block, geometric and negative binomial as "waiting for successes" variants, binomial as "counting successes in a fixed number of trials") is more valuable than memorizing four disconnected formula sets.

4.1 Bernoulli Distribution

The Bernoulli distribution models a single trial with two outcomes: "success" (coded 1) with probability p, and "failure" (coded 0) with probability 1 − p. It is the atomic building block for the binomial, geometric, and negative binomial families below.

BERNOULLI(p)P(X=1) = p, P(X=0) = 1−p; E[X] = p; Var(X) = p(1−p)

4.2 Binomial Distribution

The binomial distribution counts the number of successes in n independent, identical Bernoulli(p) trials.

BINOMIAL(n, p) PMFP(X=k) = C(n,k)·pk(1−p)n−k, k = 0, 1, …, n
BINOMIAL MEAN & VARIANCEE[X] = np, Var(X) = np(1−p)

Here C(n,k) = n!/(k!(n−k)!) counts the number of ways to choose which k of the n trials were successes; pk(1−p)n−k is the probability of any one particular arrangement of k successes and n−k failures (by independence, probabilities multiply).

Sum of independent binomials with a common p

SUM OF BINOMIALSIf X~Binomial(n₁,p) and Y~Binomial(n₂,p) are independent, then X+Y ~ Binomial(n₁+n₂, p)

This makes intuitive sense: n₁+n₂ total independent trials at the same success probability p, and it does not matter that they were originally grouped into two batches. This fails if the two binomials have different values of p — the sum is then not binomial.

Example 4.1

An insurer holds 20 independent auto policies, each with probability 0.15 of generating at least one claim this year. Find the probability that exactly 3 policies generate a claim, and find the mean and standard deviation of the number of policies with a claim.

Show Solution

Let X ~ Binomial(20, 0.15). P(X=3) = C(20,3)(0.15)³(0.85)17 = 1140(0.003375)(0.05953) ≈ 0.2290.

E[X] = 20(0.15) = 3. Var(X) = 20(0.15)(0.85) = 2.55, so SD(X) = √2.55 ≈ 1.597.

4.3 Geometric Distribution — Two Parameterizations (Watch Out!)

The geometric distribution models the number of independent Bernoulli(p) trials needed to observe the first success. There are two different but closely related versions of this distribution in circulation, and confusing them is one of the single most common errors on this exam.

Common mistake — the #1 geometric trap: Always identify which version a problem is using before writing down a formula. Read the support ("number of trials," which starts at 1, vs. "number of failures before the first success," which starts at 0) — do not assume.

Version 1 — "number of trials until the first success" (support 1, 2, 3, …)

GEOMETRIC (TRIALS), VERSION 1P(X=k) = (1−p)k−1p, k = 1, 2, 3, …
MEAN & VARIANCE, VERSION 1E[X] = 1/p, Var(X) = (1−p)/p²

Version 2 — "number of failures before the first success" (support 0, 1, 2, …)

GEOMETRIC (FAILURES), VERSION 2P(Y=k) = (1−p)kp, k = 0, 1, 2, …
MEAN & VARIANCE, VERSION 2E[Y] = (1−p)/p, Var(Y) = (1−p)/p²

Note that Y = X − 1: the number of failures before the first success is always exactly one less than the number of trials needed to get that success. The variance is identical in both versions (subtracting a constant doesn't change variance — see Chapter 3), but the mean differs by exactly 1. Ross's textbook (Ch. 4) primarily uses Version 1 ("number of trials"); many applied/actuarial texts, including Wackerly et al., also emphasize Version 1, but Exam P problems freely use both, so read carefully.

Memoryless Property

The geometric distribution (either version) is memoryless: given that the first m trials were all failures, the number of additional trials needed for the first success has the same distribution as the original problem, unconditionally. Formally, for Version 1,

MEMORYLESS PROPERTYP(X > m+n | X > m) = P(X > n)

Intuitively: past failures give no information about how much longer you'll wait, because each trial is an independent fresh start. This is the discrete analogue of the exponential distribution's memoryless property (Chapter 6) and the two are deeply linked — the geometric is essentially a "discretized exponential."

Common mistake: Memorylessness does NOT mean "the process forgets how many trials happened" in a way that changes probabilities of the past — it specifically refers to the conditional distribution of future trials given that some fixed number of past trials all failed.
