Do not apply it to condition on partial information about a single trial's outcome distribution, or to any distribution other than geometric/exponential.
Example 4.2

An actuary tests policies for fraud one at a time; each is independently fraudulent with probability 0.05. Let X = number of policies tested until the first fraudulent one is found (Version 1). Find E[X], Var(X), and P(X > 20).

Show Solution

E[X] = 1/0.05 = 20. Var(X) = (0.95)/(0.05)² = 0.95/0.0025 = 380.

P(X > 20) = probability the first 20 trials were all non-fraudulent = (0.95)20 ≈ 0.3585. (Directly: P(X>n) = (1−p)ⁿ for the "trials" version.)

4.4 Negative Binomial Distribution

The negative binomial distribution generalizes the geometric: instead of stopping at the first success, we count the number of failures before the rth success (the geometric is the special case r = 1).

NEGATIVE BINOMIAL(r, p) — FAILURES BEFORE THE rth SUCCESSP(X=k) = C(k+r−1, r−1)·(1−p)kpr, k = 0, 1, 2, …
NEGATIVE BINOMIAL MEAN & VARIANCEE[X] = r(1−p)/p, Var(X) = r(1−p)/p²

As with the geometric, there is an alternative "trials" parameterization counting the total number of trials T = X + r needed to reach the rth success, with E[T] = r/p and the same variance. Always check which quantity ("number of failures" vs. "number of trials") the problem defines.

Exam tip: The negative binomial can be viewed as a sum of r independent geometric random variables (each geometric counting failures before one success, using Version 2): X = Y₁ + Y₂ + ⋯ + Yᵣ. This immediately explains why the mean and variance are exactly r times the single-geometric mean and variance.
Example 4.3

A salesperson closes independent sales calls with probability 0.25 each. Find the probability that the salesperson's 4th sale occurs on exactly the 10th call.

Show Solution

We need r=4 successes with the 4th success landing on trial 10, i.e., exactly 3 successes among the first 9 calls, then a success on call 10. Using the "trials" form: P(T=10) = C(9,3)(0.25)³(0.75)⁶(0.25) = C(9,3)(0.25)⁴(0.75)⁶.

C(9,3) = 84. (0.25)⁴ = 0.00390625. (0.75)⁶ ≈ 0.177979. So P(T=10) ≈ 84(0.00390625)(0.177979) ≈ 0.05833.

Example 4.4

The number of warranty claims filed on a particular appliance model over its service life satisfies q(n+1) = 0.25·q(n) for all integers n ≥ 0. Find P(more than one claim).

Show Solution

The recursion q(n+1) = 0.25 q(n) means q(n) = q(0)·(0.25)ⁿ — a geometric-shaped pmf (Version 2, "failures" form, with 1−p = 0.25, i.e. p = 0.75). Requiring the pmf to sum to 1: q(0)·Σn=0∞(0.25)ⁿ = q(0)/(1−0.25) = q(0)/0.75 = 1, so q(0) = 0.75. Then q(1) = 0.75(0.25) = 0.1875.

P(N > 1) = 1 − q(0) − q(1) = 1 − 0.75 − 0.1875 = 0.0625.

Boxed answer: 0.0625.

4.5 Deriving the Binomial Mean and Variance

It is worth seeing why E[X]=np and Var(X)=np(1−p) for the binomial, since the underlying technique — decomposing a complicated random variable into a sum of simple indicator (Bernoulli) variables — is one of the most powerful tools on the entire exam. Write X = I₁+I₂+⋯+Iₙ, where Iⱼ is the indicator (1 or 0) of success on trial j; each Iⱼ is Bernoulli(p). By linearity, E[X] = ΣE[Iⱼ] = np. Because the trials are independent, the indicators are independent, so variances add: Var(X) = ΣVar(Iⱼ) = n·p(1−p). This "sum-of-indicators" trick reduces a seemingly complicated combinatorial object (the binomial) to n trivial pieces, and it generalizes readily — the same technique derives the mean of a hypergeometric distribution (Chapter 5) even though the hypergeometric's indicators are not independent (variance requires more care there, hence the correction factor).

4.6 More on the Geometric Distribution

Example 4.5

Two claims adjusters inspect vehicles independently: Adjuster Elena inspects fleet-A vehicles (each with 15% chance of damage) until finding one that is damaged; Adjuster Marcus inspects fleet-B vehicles (each with 25% chance of damage) the same way. Find P(Elena inspects fewer vehicles than Marcus).

Show Solution

Let E ~ Geometric(trials, p=0.15) and M ~ Geometric(trials, p=0.25), independent. We want P(E<M) = Σ_{n=1}^∞ P(E=n)P(M>n).

P(E=n)=(0.85)^{n-1}(0.15), and P(M>n)=(0.75)ⁿ (probability the first n fleet-B trials are all damage-free).

P(E<M) = Σ_{n=1}^∞ (0.85)^{n-1}(0.15)(0.75)ⁿ = 0.15(0.75)Σ_{n=1}^∞(0.85)^{n-1}(0.75)^{n-1} = 0.1125 Σ_{n=1}^∞(0.6375)^{n-1} = 0.1125/(1−0.6375) = 0.1125/0.3625 ≈ 0.3103.

This geometric-series summation technique — recognizing Σ(0.85)^{n-1}(0.75)^{n-1}=Σ(0.6375)^{n-1} as another geometric series — is the standard route whenever two independent geometric race-type variables are compared.

4.7 Negative Binomial: the Trials Parameterization

Recall from Section 4.4 that if X counts failures before the rth success, the total number of trials is T = X+r. Some textbooks (and some exam problems) define the negative binomial directly in terms of T:

NEGATIVE BINOMIAL — TRIALS PARAMETERIZATIONP(T=t) = C(t−1, r−1)·pr(1−p)t−r, t = r, r+1, r+2, …
MEAN & VARIANCE — TRIALS PARAMETERIZATIONE[T] = r/p, Var(T) = r(1−p)/p²

The variance is identical to the "failures" version (again, because T and X differ only by the constant r), but the mean differs by exactly r. As with the plain geometric, always determine from context whether a problem is counting failures or total trials before applying a formula.

Example 4.6

An underwriter reviews policy applications one at a time; each is independently approved with probability 0.6. Find the probability that the 5th approval occurs on exactly the 8th application reviewed.

Show Solution

Trials parameterization with r=5, p=0.6, t=8: P(T=8) = C(7,4)(0.6)⁵(0.4)³ = 35(0.07776)(0.064) ≈ 35(0.0049766) ≈ 0.1742.

4.9 Sum of Independent Binomials: a Worked Check

Example 4.7

A reinsurer covers two independent blocks of policies: block A has 30 policies each with claim probability 0.1, and block B has 20 policies each with the same claim probability 0.1. Let T be the total number of policies (across both blocks) that generate a claim. Verify that T is binomial and find P(T=6).

Show Solution

Since both blocks share p=0.1 and are independent, T ~ Binomial(30+20, 0.1) = Binomial(50, 0.1).

P(T=6) = C(50,6)(0.1)⁶(0.9)⁴⁴. Using C(50,6)=15,890,700: P(T=6) ≈ 15,890,700(10⁻⁶)(0.9)⁴⁴. Since (0.9)⁴⁴ ≈ 0.009718, P(T=6) ≈ 15,890,700(0.000001)(0.009718) ≈ 0.1544.

Had the two blocks had different claim probabilities, this shortcut would fail entirely, and T would not be binomial at all — its exact distribution would need to be built up by convolving the two different binomial pmfs term by term.

4.11 The Minimum of Independent Geometrics Is Geometric

A useful closure property, parallel to the "sum of independent binomials is binomial" and "sum of independent Poissons is Poisson" results already seen: if X₁,…,Xn are independent Geometric (failures-before-first-success form) random variables with respective success probabilities p₁,…,pn, and we ask "how many rounds pass before any of the n processes records its first success" (i.e., M = min(X₁,…,Xn), thinking of each Xᵢ as failures-before-success in an independent parallel process running in lockstep), then M is itself geometric, with success probability

MINIMUM OF INDEPENDENT GEOMETRICSP(\text{success on a given round}) = 1 − (1−p₁)(1−p₂)⋯(1−pn)

Intuitively, "at least one of n independent processes succeeds on a given round" has probability equal to one minus the probability that all of them fail that round, and because each process is memoryless individually, the combined process is also memoryless — hence itself geometric.

Example 4.8

Two independent underwriting reviewers each flag a policy for audit with probability 0.05 per policy reviewed (reviewer A) and 0.08 per policy reviewed (reviewer B), reviewing policies in parallel, one at a time. Find the probability that neither reviewer has flagged a policy within the first 10 policies reviewed.

Show Solution

Combined "success" (at least one flag) probability per round: 1−(0.95)(0.92) = 1−0.874 = 0.126. The number of rounds until the first combined flag is Geometric(trials) with p=0.126. P(\text{no flag in first 10}) = (1−0.126)^{10} = (0.874)^{10} ≈ 0.2593.

4.13 A Combined Binomial/Geometric Word Problem

Example 4.9

An inspector examines light bulbs from a production line one at a time. Each bulb is independently defective with probability 0.02. (a) Find the probability that among the next 50 bulbs examined, at least 2 are defective. (b) Find the expected number of bulbs examined until the first defective one is found.

Show Solution

(a) Let X ~ Binomial(50, 0.02). P(X≥2) = 1−P(X=0)−P(X=1).

P(X=0) = (0.98)^{50} ≈ 0.3642. P(X=1) = 50(0.02)(0.98)^{49} ≈ 50(0.02)(0.3716) ≈ 0.3716.

P(X≥2) ≈ 1−0.3642−0.3716 = 0.2642.

(b) The number of bulbs until the first defective is Geometric (trials form), p=0.02: E[X]=1/0.02=50.

Notice how the same underlying "0.02 probability per independent trial" setup supports two entirely different distributions depending on what is being counted — a fixed number of trials with a random count of successes (binomial) versus a random number of trials needed for a fixed count of successes (geometric). Always identify which quantity is fixed and which is random before choosing a formula.

4.15 A Negative Binomial Insurance-Flavored Example

Example 4.10

A reinsurer will terminate a quota-share treaty with a cedent as soon as the cedent's 4th large loss (exceeding a stated threshold) occurs. Each loss reported to the cedent independently qualifies as "large" with probability 0.15. Find the expected number of losses reported before the treaty terminates (i.e., before, and not counting, the 4th large loss), and find the probability the treaty terminates on exactly the 20th reported loss.

Show Solution

Let N = number of non-large ("small") losses reported before the 4th large loss — Negative Binomial, failures form, with r=4, p=0.15. E[N] = r(1−p)/p = 4(0.85)/0.15 ≈ 22.67.

For the second part, use the trials parameterization: the treaty terminates on trial T=20 if exactly 3 of the first 19 losses are large, and the 20th is large. P(T=20) = C(19,3)(0.15)⁴(0.85)^{16}.

C(19,3) = 969. (0.15)⁴ = 0.00050625. (0.85)^{16} ≈ 0.07425. So P(T=20) ≈ 969(0.00050625)(0.07425) ≈ 0.03643.

4.17 A Note on Independence Assumptions in This Chapter

Every distribution in this chapter — Bernoulli, binomial, geometric, and negative binomial — rests on an assumption of independent, identically distributed trials with a constant success probability p. In a word problem, watch for language that quietly violates this assumption: "the probability of a claim increases after each renewal," or "trials are drawn without replacement from a finite pool," both signal that a straightforward binomial or geometric model no longer applies exactly (the latter case pushes you toward the hypergeometric distribution of Chapter 5 instead). When trials are independent but not identically distributed (e.g., n policies each with a different claim probability pᵢ), the count of successes is still well-defined and its mean is still the sum of the individual probabilities by linearity of expectation, but it is no longer binomial in distribution — its exact pmf would need to be built up by convolving the individual, non-identical Bernoulli pmfs, a construction sometimes called a Poisson-binomial distribution (not itself separately tested by name on this syllabus, but worth recognizing as the reason a plain binomial formula would give the wrong answer in that scenario).

4.18 Chapter Summary Table (partial — completed with Ch. 5 families)

Discrete Distributions I
Distribution PMF Support E[X] Var(X)
Bernoulli(p) px(1−p)1−x {0,1} p p(1−p)
Binomial(n,p) C(n,k)pk(1−p)n−k {0,…,n} np np(1−p)
Geometric(p), trials (1−p)k−1p {1,2,…} 1/p (1−p)/p²
Geometric(p), failures (1−p)kp {0,1,…} (1−p)/p (1−p)/p²
Neg. Binomial(r,p), failures C(k+r−1,r−1)(1−p)kpr {0,1,…} r(1−p)/p r(1−p)/p²

5. Discrete Distributions II: Hypergeometric, Poisson & Discrete Uniform

Learning Outcomes Covered: Understand discrete univariate distributions including hypergeometric, Poisson, and uniform, and their applications; calculate expected values and variance for these families.

This chapter completes the discrete distribution roster with three families that, unlike Chapter 4's trial-based distributions, arise from three structurally distinct settings: sampling without replacement from a finite population (hypergeometric), counting rare events over a continuous exposure (Poisson), and simple equally-likely outcomes (discrete uniform). Of the three, Poisson is by far the most heavily tested — it reappears throughout the exam as the standard model for claim counts, event counts, and defect counts, and its deep relationship with the exponential distribution (Chapter 6) is one of the most important structural connections in the entire syllabus.

5.1 Hypergeometric Distribution

The hypergeometric distribution counts successes when sampling without replacement from a finite population — this is the key structural contrast with the binomial, which requires independent trials (equivalently, sampling with replacement, or sampling from an infinite population).

Setup: a population of size N contains K "successes" and N−K "failures." A sample of size n is drawn without replacement. X = number of successes in the sample.

HYPERGEOMETRIC PMFP(X=k) = C(K,k)·C(N−K, n−k) / C(N,n)
HYPERGEOMETRIC MEAN & VARIANCEE[X] = n·K/N, Var(X) = n·(K/N)·(1−K/N)·[(N−n)/(N−1)]

The support of k runs over integers with max(0, n−(N−K)) ≤ k ≤ min(n, K) — you cannot have more successes than either the sample size or the total number of successes available, and if the sample is large enough relative to the number of failures, you're forced to include at least some successes.

Deriving the Hypergeometric Mean via Indicators

Exactly as in Section 4.5's binomial derivation, write X = I₁+I₂+⋯+In, where Iⱼ indicates whether the jth draw (in some fixed order) is a success. By the symmetry of sampling without replacement, before any draws are observed, each individual draw is equally likely to land on any of the N population members — so marginally, P(Iⱼ=1) = K/N for every j, exactly as if the draws were independent, even though they are not. By linearity of expectation (which requires no independence assumption at all):

E[X] = Σ_{j=1}^n E[Iⱼ] = Σ_{j=1}^n (K/N) = n(K/N)

This confirms E[X]=nK/N without any combinatorial pmf manipulation — a good illustration of how linearity of expectation succeeds in settings (like this one, with dependent indicators) where the variance-additivity shortcut of Chapter 3 does not apply, precisely because linearity never required independence in the first place, while variance-additivity always does.

Why the Finite Population Correction Factor Looks the Way It Does

The correction factor (N−n)/(N−1) can be understood through the same "sum of indicators" technique used for the binomial in Section 4.5. Write X = I₁+⋯+In, where Iⱼ indicates whether the jth draw is a success. Each individual Iⱼ is still Bernoulli(K/N) marginally (before any draws are known, each position in the sample is equally likely to be any one of the N population members), so Var(Iⱼ)=(K/N)(1−K/N) exactly as in the binomial case. The crucial difference is that, unlike the binomial, the indicators here are not independent — drawing a success on one draw makes success slightly less likely on the next draw, since one fewer success remains in the shrinking pool. This negative correlation between draws is exactly what the factor (N−n)/(N−1) captures and subtracts out relative to the (larger) binomial variance n(K/N)(1−K/N) that would apply if the draws were independent.

Contrast with the Binomial

Notice E[X] = nK/N = np where p = K/N is exactly the same mean formula as the binomial with success probability p = K/N. The variance, however, carries an extra factor (N−n)/(N−1), called the finite population correction factor. This factor is always ≤ 1, so sampling without replacement produces less variance than sampling with replacement (binomial) — intuitively, once you've drawn several successes, the remaining pool has relatively fewer successes left, which "self-corrects" the count and reduces spread. As N → ∞ with K/N fixed, the correction factor → 1 and the hypergeometric converges to the binomial — sampling without replacement from a huge population is essentially the same as sampling with replacement.

Exam tip: The giveaway phrase for hypergeometric is sampling "without replacement" from a fixed, finite, explicitly-sized population (a deck of cards, an urn with a stated number of balls, a fixed batch of parts to inspect). If the population is described as effectively unlimited, or the problem says "with replacement," use binomial instead.
Example 5.1

An auditor samples 5 policies without replacement from a batch of 30, of which 6 contain a filing error. Find the probability that exactly 2 sampled policies contain an error, and find the mean and variance of the number of errors found.

Show Solution

N=30, K=6, n=5. P(X=2) = C(6,2)C(24,3)/C(30,5) = 15(2024)/142506 = 30360/142506 ≈ 0.2131.

E[X] = 5(6/30) = 1. Var(X) = 5(6/30)(24/30)·(25/29) = 5(0.2)(0.8)(0.8621) ≈ 0.6897.

5.2 Poisson Distribution

The Poisson distribution models the number of rare, independent events occurring in a fixed interval of time, space, or exposure, at a constant average rate λ.

POISSON(λ) PMFP(X=k) = λke−λ/k!, k = 0, 1, 2, …
POISSON MEAN & VARIANCEE[X] = λ, Var(X) = λ

The defining feature of Poisson is that mean and variance are equal — a useful diagnostic and a common way exam problems disguise the distribution ("mean and variance are both 3" is a strong hint the count is Poisson).

Poisson as a Limit of Binomial

If X ~ Binomial(n,p) with n large and p small, such that np = λ stays moderate, then X is approximately Poisson(λ). This is the classical justification for using Poisson to model rare events: a huge number of "opportunities" (e.g., policyholders, seconds in a day, cells in a grid) each with a tiny individual probability of an event, but a moderate expected total count.

Rate Interpretation and Sums of Independent Poissons

If events occur at rate λ per unit of exposure (e.g., per year), then over t units of exposure the count is Poisson(λt) — this rescaling of the parameter by exposure is used constantly in insurance ratemaking contexts.

SUM OF INDEPENDENT POISSONSIf X~Poisson(λ₁) and Y~Poisson(λ₂) are independent, then X+Y ~ Poisson(λ₁+λ₂)

This closure property makes Poisson exceptionally convenient for combining independent sources of claims, defects, or arrivals — e.g., combining claim counts from several independent territories or lines of business.

Example 5.2

The number of hail claims filed with an insurer in a month is Poisson with mean 3. Find the probability of at least 2 claims in a given month.

Show Solution

P(X ≥ 2) = 1 − P(X=0) − P(X=1) = 1 − e−3 − 3e−3 = 1 − 4e−3 ≈ 1 − 4(0.049787) ≈ 1 − 0.19915 = 0.8009.

Example 5.3

The number of forklift breakdowns at a distribution warehouse in a year is Poisson. The probability the warehouse has at least one breakdown this year is 0.15. Find the probability it has at least two breakdowns.

Show Solution

P(X ≥ 1) = 1 − e−λ = 0.15 ⟹ e−λ = 0.85 ⟹ λ = −ln(0.85) ≈ 0.16252.

P(X ≥ 2) = 1 − P(X=0) − P(X=1) = 1 − e−λ − λe−λ = 1 − 0.85 − (0.16252)(0.85) = 1 − 0.85 − 0.13814 = 0.01186.

Boxed answer: ≈ 0.0119.

5.3 Discrete Uniform Distribution

The discrete uniform distribution puts equal probability on each of a finite, evenly-spaced set of integer values — e.g., the outcome of a fair die roll, or a randomly chosen year in a fixed range.

DISCRETE UNIFORM ON {a, a+1, …, b} PMFP(X=k) = 1/(b−a+1), k = a, a+1, …, b
DISCRETE UNIFORM MEAN & VARIANCEE[X] = (a+b)/2, Var(X) = [(b−a+1)² − 1]/12
Example 5.4

A random variable is uniformly distributed on the integers {1,2,…,8}. Compare its standard deviation to that of a continuous uniform distribution on [1,8].

Show Solution

Discrete uniform on {1,…,8}: here b−a+1 = 8, so Var = (8²−1)/12 = 63/12 = 5.25, giving SD = √5.25 ≈ 2.2913.

Continuous uniform on [1,8] (see Chapter 6): Var = (8−1)²/12 = 49/12 ≈ 4.0833, giving SD ≈ 2.0207.

Absolute difference: 2.2913 − 2.0207 ≈ 0.2706 ≈ 0.27.

Boxed answer: ≈ 0.27. Note that discrete uniform on a set of integers always has slightly larger variance than the continuous uniform over the same range, because the discrete version places positive mass at the two extreme endpoints while the continuous density "smooths" over the interior.

5.4 Poisson Approximation to the Binomial in Practice

Example 5.5

A large insurer has 5,000 independent auto policies, each with probability 0.0006 of a catastrophic claim in a given year. Approximate the probability of at least 2 catastrophic claims across the entire book.

Show Solution

Exact treatment requires Binomial(5000, 0.0006), which is unwieldy by hand. Since n is large and p is small, approximate with Poisson(λ=np=5000(0.0006)=3).

P(X≥2) = 1−P(X=0)−P(X=1) = 1−e⁻³−3e⁻³ = 1−4e⁻³ ≈ 1−0.19915=0.8009.

This large-n/small-p/moderate-λ pattern (many policies, each individually unlikely to produce a catastrophic claim, but a moderate expected total count) is exactly the classical justification for the Poisson approximation, and this exact substitution is tested often enough that recognizing the pattern instantly is worth memorizing.

5.5 Hypergeometric vs. Binomial: a Direct Numerical Comparison

Example 5.6

A batch contains 20 items, 4 of which are defective. Five items are sampled without replacement. Compare P(exactly 1 defective) under the exact hypergeometric model to the binomial approximation using p=4/20=0.20.

Show Solution

Exact (hypergeometric, N=20, K=4, n=5): P(X=1) = C(4,1)C(16,4)/C(20,5) = 4(1820)/15504 = 7280/15504 ≈ 0.4697.

Binomial approximation (n=5, p=0.20): P(X=1) = C(5,1)(0.2)¹(0.8)⁴ = 5(0.2)(0.4096) = 0.4096.

The two values (0.4697 exact vs. 0.4096 approximate) differ by roughly 6 percentage points — a meaningful gap. The reason: dropping 5 items out of only 20 noticeably thins the remaining pool, so successive draws are not independent the way a binomial model assumes. As N grows large with K/N and n held fixed, this gap shrinks toward zero and the binomial approximation becomes reliable. Lesson: always use the exact hypergeometric formula whenever a problem specifies sampling without replacement from an explicitly stated, finite population — do not substitute the binomial "for convenience" unless the population is explicitly described as very large relative to the sample.

5.6 Discrete Uniform in Context

Example 5.7

Riders on an amusement park roller coaster are seated in a car uniformly at random among m seats, numbered 1 through m. Find E[X] and Var(X) for the seat number X in terms of m.

Show Solution

Discrete Uniform on {1,…,m}: E[X] = (1+m)/2. Var(X) = (m²−1)/12 (using b−a+1=m in the general formula).

5.8 A Poisson Conditional-Probability Example

Example 5.8

The number of severe storms striking a city in a year follows a Poisson distribution with mean 4. Given that at least one storm occurs in a given year, find the probability that exactly one storm occurs.

Show Solution

P(N=1 | N≥1) = P(N=1)/P(N≥1) = [4e⁻⁴]/[1−e⁻⁴].

4e⁻⁴ ≈ 4(0.018316) = 0.073263. 1−e⁻⁴ ≈ 1−0.018316=0.981684.

P(N=1|N≥1) ≈ 0.073263/0.981684 ≈ 0.0746.

5.9 A Negative-Binomial-Flavored Hypergeometric Contrast

It is worth explicitly contrasting all four "counting" distributions covered in Chapters 4–5 side by side, since exam problems are often designed to test whether you can tell them apart from a word description alone rather than from an explicit formula:

  • Binomial: fixed number of trials n; count successes; trials independent (with replacement, or infinite population).
  • Negative Binomial / Geometric: trials continue until a fixed number of successes r is reached; count is the (random) number of trials or failures needed; trials independent.
  • Hypergeometric: fixed number of draws n from a finite population without replacement; count successes; draws are not independent.
  • Poisson: count of events over a continuous exposure (time/space), not built from a fixed number of discrete trials at all; arises as a limit of binomial or as the event-count view of an exponential inter-arrival process.
Exam tip: Ask yourself two questions for any counting word problem: (1) "Is the number of trials/draws fixed, or is it the number of successes that's fixed (with the number of trials random)?" and (2) "Is sampling with or without replacement (or is there no notion of a finite population at all)?" The answers immediately narrow the choice to one of the four families above.

5.11 Thinning a Poisson Process

A powerful and exam-relevant property: if events occur as a Poisson process with rate λ, and each event is independently classified into "type 1" with probability q and "type 2" with probability 1−q (independent of everything else), then the type-1 events alone form a Poisson process with rate λq, and the type-2 events alone form an independent Poisson process with rate λ(1−q). This is called thinning, and it explains why, for instance, splitting a Poisson claim count by cause, by severity tier, or by territory again yields Poisson counts for each piece — a fact used constantly when a problem describes claims arriving at an overall Poisson rate that are then categorized.

Example 5.9

Claims arrive at an insurer according to a Poisson process at rate 10 per day. Each claim is independently a "small" claim with probability 0.7 or a "large" claim with probability 0.3. Find the probability that exactly 2 large claims arrive in a given day.

Show Solution

By thinning, large claims alone form a Poisson process with rate 10(0.3)=3 per day. P(2 \text{ large claims}) = 3²e^{-3}/2! = 9e^{-3}/2 ≈ 9(0.049787)/2 ≈ 0.2240.

5.13 Poisson Counts Over Sub-Intervals of Different Lengths

Example 5.10

Customer arrivals at a claims-service call center follow a Poisson process at a rate of 12 per hour. Find the probability of exactly 2 arrivals in a specific 15-minute period.

Show Solution

Rescale the rate to the exposure period: 15 minutes is 1/4 hour, so the mean count in that period is λt = 12(1/4) = 3.

P(X=2) = 3²e^{-3}/2! = 9e^{-3}/2 ≈ 9(0.049787)/2 ≈ 0.2240.

The general lesson: always rescale the given rate to match the exposure period stated in the question before applying the Poisson pmf — a rate quoted "per hour" must become a mean count "per 15 minutes" (i.e., multiplied by 1/4) whenever the question asks about a 15-minute window.

5.13b A Combined Hypergeometric/Poisson Comparison

Example 5.11

A shipment contains 500 items, 15 of which are defective. An inspector samples 10 items without replacement. (a) Using the exact hypergeometric model, find the expected number of defectives in the sample. (b) An alternative simplified model treats the count of defectives in the sample as approximately Poisson with the same mean. Under this Poisson approximation, find the probability of at least 1 defective, and compare to the general shape of a rare-event count.

Show Solution

(a) Hypergeometric mean: E[X] = n(K/N) = 10(15/500) = 10(0.03) = 0.3.

(b) Approximating with Poisson(0.3): P(X≥1) = 1−e^{-0.3} ≈ 1−0.740818 = 0.259182.

Because N=500 is large relative to the sample size n=10, and the defective proportion K/N=0.03 is small, this is exactly the regime (Section 5.4) where a hypergeometric count is well approximated by a Poisson (via the intermediate binomial approximation) — the finite population correction factor (N−n)/(N−1) = 490/499 ≈ 0.982 is close enough to 1 that the without-replacement effect is nearly negligible here, unlike the small-population Example 5.6.

5.14b A Note on Overlapping Distribution Families

It is worth explicitly noting the boundary connections between this chapter's three families and those of Chapter 4, since exam problems sometimes exploit these connections directly: the hypergeometric distribution converges to the binomial as the population size grows large relative to the sample (Section 5.5), and the binomial itself converges to the Poisson as the number of trials grows large and the success probability shrinks (Section 5.4). Together, these two limiting relationships form a chain — hypergeometric → binomial → Poisson — each link replacing a more complex, exact dependency structure with a simpler approximate one, in exchange for requiring fewer parameters (the Poisson needs only a single rate parameter, versus two for the binomial and three for the hypergeometric). Recognizing where a given problem sits on this chain is often the fastest route to the correct simplification.

5.16 Comprehensive Discrete Distribution Summary Table

All Discrete Distributions on the Syllabus (Chapters 4 & 5)
Distribution PMF Parameters E[X] Var(X) Typical Use
Bernoulli(p) px(1−p)1−x, x∈{0,1} 0<p<1 p p(1−p) Single yes/no trial
Binomial(n,p) C(n,k)pk(1−p)n−k n∈ℤ⁺, 0<p<1 np np(1−p) Count of successes in n independent trials
Geometric (trials) (1−p)k−1p 0<p<1 1/p (1−p)/p² Trials to first success
Negative Binomial(r,p) C(k+r−1,r−1)(1−p)kpr r∈ℤ⁺, 0<p<1 r(1−p)/p r(1−p)/p² Failures before r-th success
Hypergeometric(N,K,n) C(K,k)C(N−K,n−k)/C(N,n) N,K,n∈ℤ⁺, n≤N nK/N n(K/N)(1−K/N)(N−n)/(N−1) Sampling without replacement
Poisson(λ) λke−λ/k! λ>0 λ λ Rare-event counts over an interval
Discrete Uniform{a,…,b} 1/(b−a+1) a≤b integers (a+b)/2 [(b−a+1)²−1]/12 Equally-likely integer outcomes
Exam tip: Memorize this table cold. A large share of Exam P points come from simply recognizing which named distribution a word problem describes and plugging into the right mean/variance formula — the "derivation" work is rarely required live on the exam once you know the setup.

6. Continuous Distributions I: Uniform, Exponential & Gamma

Learning Outcomes Covered: Understand continuous univariate distributions including uniform, exponential, and gamma, and their applications.

The exponential distribution introduced in this chapter is, without exaggeration, the single most important continuous distribution for the applied portion of this exam: it is the default severity model in the overwhelming majority of Chapter 8–9 insurance-payment problems, precisely because its memoryless property makes deductible and limited-expected-value calculations dramatically simpler than for any other severity model. The gamma distribution generalizes the exponential and reappears whenever a sum of independent exponential waiting times, or a more general skewed positive quantity, needs to be modeled. Mastering the exponential's memoryless shortcut in this chapter will make Chapters 8 and 9 substantially faster.

6.1 Continuous Uniform Distribution

The continuous uniform distribution on [a,b] spreads probability evenly across the interval — every sub-interval of a given length has the same probability, regardless of location.

UNIFORM(a,b) PDFf(x) = 1/(b−a), a ≤ x ≤ b
UNIFORM CDFF(x) = (x−a)/(b−a), a ≤ x ≤ b
UNIFORM MEAN & VARIANCEE[X] = (a+b)/2, Var(X) = (b−a)²/12

The continuous uniform is the natural "complete ignorance within a range" model — used, for instance, to model the time within an hour that an event occurs, or (in insurance) a loss amount that is equally likely anywhere within a known range.

Example 6.1

An adjuster's arrival time within a scheduled one-hour window is uniform on [0,60] minutes past the hour. Find the probability the adjuster arrives in the last 15 minutes of the window, and find SD of the arrival time.

Show Solution

P(45 ≤ X ≤ 60) = (60−45)/60 = 15/60 = 0.25.

Var(X) = (60−0)²/12 = 3600/12 = 300, so SD = √300 ≈ 17.32 minutes.

6.2 Exponential Distribution

The exponential distribution models waiting times / time-to-event data and is the continuous analogue of the geometric distribution. It's usually parameterized either by a rate λ (events per unit time) or, equivalently, by a mean θ = 1/λ — actuarial texts (including Wackerly and most SOA materials) often prefer the mean parameterization θ, so watch which one a problem uses.

EXPONENTIAL PDF (RATE λ)f(x) = λe−λx, x ≥ 0
EXPONENTIAL CDFF(x) = 1 − e−λx = 1 − e−x/θ, x ≥ 0
EXPONENTIAL MEAN & VARIANCEE[X] = 1/λ = θ, Var(X) = 1/λ² = θ²

Memoryless Property

The exponential is the unique continuous distribution with the memoryless property:

MEMORYLESS PROPERTYP(X > s+t | X > s) = P(X > t), for all s,t ≥ 0

In words: given that a component has already survived time s, its remaining lifetime has the exact same exponential distribution as a brand-new component — the component does not "age" or "wear out" in a probabilistic sense. This is a strong and somewhat unrealistic assumption (real components typically do wear out), but it makes the exponential mathematically tractable and it is an excellent model for purely random event timing (radioactive decay, arrivals in a Poisson process, claim inter-arrival times).

Common mistake: Do not apply the memoryless property to any distribution other than the exponential (continuous) or geometric (discrete) — the gamma, Weibull, and lognormal distributions, for instance, do NOT have this property, even though they can also model waiting times.

Relation to the Poisson Process

If events occur according to a Poisson process with rate λ (so the count of events in an interval of length t is Poisson(λt)), then the time between consecutive events — and the time until the first event — is exponentially distributed with rate λ. This Poisson–exponential duality (count vs. waiting time views of the same underlying random process) is tested frequently: a problem may give you a Poisson rate and ask for a probability involving waiting time, requiring you to switch to the exponential distribution mid-problem.

Example 6.2

Claims arrive at an insurer according to a Poisson process at a rate of 2 per day. Find the probability that more than 3 days pass before the next claim arrives.

Show Solution

Time between claims is exponential with rate λ=2 (mean θ = 0.5 days). P(X > 3) = e−λ(3) = e−6 ≈ 0.00248.

Example 6.3

For the exponential, normal, and continuous uniform distributions, determine for which of the three the mean is always equal to the median.

Show Solution

Normal: the density is symmetric about μ, so exactly half the probability lies on each side of μ; hence median = mean = μ always.

Uniform[a,b]: the density is symmetric about the midpoint (a+b)/2, which is also the mean, so median = mean = (a+b)/2 always.

Exponential(θ): the median m solves 1−e−m/θ = 0.5 ⟹ m = θ ln 2 ≈ 0.6931θ, which is strictly less than the mean θ for any θ>0 — so mean ≠ median.

Boxed answer: normal and uniform (not exponential). Symmetry is exactly the property that forces mean = median; the exponential's right skew is precisely why its median falls below its mean.

6.3 Gamma Distribution

The gamma distribution generalizes the exponential and is the standard model for the sum of independent exponential waiting times, or more generally for skewed, positive-only quantities like insurance losses.

GAMMA(α, θ) PDFf(x) = xα−1e−x/θ / (Γ(α)·θα), x > 0
GAMMA MEAN & VARIANCEE[X] = αθ, Var(X) = αθ²

Here α > 0 is the shape parameter and θ > 0 is the scale parameter; Γ(α) = ∫0∞ tα−1e−t dt is the gamma function, which satisfies Γ(n) = (n−1)! for positive integers n, and Γ(α) = (α−1)Γ(α−1) in general.

Gamma as a Sum of iid Exponentials (Integer Shape)

SUM OF EXPONENTIALS ⟹ GAMMAIf X₁,…,Xn are iid Exponential(θ), then X₁+⋯+Xn ~ Gamma(n, θ)

This is one of the most useful facts about the gamma distribution: when the shape parameter α = n is a positive integer, the gamma distribution has the direct interpretation of "the total waiting time until the nth event" in a Poisson process with rate 1/θ — i.e., the gamma is to the exponential what the negative binomial is to the geometric. This also confirms the mean and variance formulas by linearity/independence: E[Σ Xi] = nθ and Var(Σ Xi) = nθ² (variances of independent variables add), matching α = n in the general gamma formulas.

Exam tip: When α is a positive integer, the gamma distribution is sometimes called the Erlang distribution. When α = 1, gamma reduces exactly to the exponential — a useful sanity check: plug α=1 into the gamma pdf and mean/variance formulas and confirm you recover the exponential exactly.

The chi-square distribution with k degrees of freedom is itself a special case of the gamma distribution, with α = k/2 and θ = 2; chi-square is not separately tested on Exam P's current syllabus but recognizing this link can occasionally shortcut a gamma calculation phrased in chi-square terms.

Example 6.4

Repair times for a particular machine part are modeled by independent Exponential(mean 2 hours) random variables. A technician must complete 3 independent repairs. Find the mean and variance of the total time required, and identify the distribution family.

Show Solution

Total time T = X₁+X₂+X₃ ~ Gamma(α=3, θ=2).

E[T] = αθ = 3(2) = 6 hours. Var(T) = αθ² = 3(4) = 12 hours², so SD(T) = √12 ≈ 3.464 hours.

6.4 Proof of the Memoryless Property

It is worth seeing why the exponential is memoryless, since the derivation is short and builds confidence in using the property freely. Let X ~ Exponential(λ). By the definition of conditional probability,

P(X>s+t | X>s) = P(X>s+t, X>s)/P(X>s) = P(X>s+t)/P(X>s)

(since X>s+t automatically implies X>s for t≥0). Using the survival function S(x)=e−λx:

= e−λ(s+t)/e−λs = e−λs−λt+λs = e−λt = P(X>t)

The key algebraic fact making this work is that e−λ(s+t) factors exactly into e−λs·e−λt — a property of exponentials (the mathematical function) that no other continuous survival function on [0,∞) shares. This is in fact why the property is called "memoryless": it can be shown that the exponential distribution is the only continuous distribution on [0,∞) with this factorization property.

6.5 Additional Worked Examples

Example 6.5

A transmission tower experiences an average of 9 lightning strikes in any 3-year period, and the times between strikes (and until the next strike) are iid Exponential. Find the median number of years from now until the next strike.

Show Solution

Rate: λ = 9/3 = 3 strikes per year, so mean time between strikes is θ=1/λ=1/3 years.

Median of Exponential(θ=1/3): m = θ ln2 = (1/3)(0.6931) ≈ 0.2310 years.

Boxed answer: ≈ 0.23 years.

Example 6.6

A component's lifetime (in years) is modeled by an Exponential distribution with variance 7225. Find the median minus the mean.

Show Solution

For Exponential(θ), Var(X)=θ²=7225 ⟹ θ=85 years (taking the positive root). Mean =θ=85.

Median = θ ln2 = 85(0.693147) ≈ 58.92.

Median minus mean: 58.92 − 85 = −26.08 years — negative, confirming the exponential's right skew (median below mean).

6.5b Scaling an Exponential or Gamma Random Variable

A fact used repeatedly in Chapter 8's inflation problems: if X ~ Exponential(θ) and c > 0 is a constant, then cX ~ Exponential(cθ) — multiplying an exponential by a positive constant simply rescales its mean by that same constant, and it remains exponential. The same is true of the gamma family more generally: if X ~ Gamma(α,θ), then cX ~ Gamma(α, cθ) — the shape parameter is unaffected by rescaling, only the scale parameter changes. This is easiest to see from the CDF: P(cX≤x) = P(X≤x/c) = 1−e^{-x/(cθ)}, which is exactly the CDF of Exponential(cθ).

Example 6.7

Claim severities this year are Exponential with mean 800. Next year, severities are expected to trend upward by 12%. Find the probability that a trended claim next year exceeds 1000.

Show Solution

Next year's severity X′=1.12X ~ Exponential(mean = 1.12(800)=896).

P(X′>1000) = e^{-1000/896} ≈ e^{-1.1161} ≈ 0.3276.

6.7 Relating the Gamma Back to the Poisson Process

Section 6.3 noted that a Gamma(n,θ) variable (integer n) is the waiting time until the nth event of a Poisson process running at rate 1/θ. This gives a second, purely probabilistic route to the gamma CDF that avoids the incomplete gamma function entirely, by translating a statement about waiting time into a statement about a Poisson count:

GAMMA CDF VIA THE POISSON COUNT (integer shape n)If T ~ Gamma(n, θ) and N(t) ~ Poisson(t/θ) counts events by time t, then P(T ≤ t) = P(N(t) ≥ n)

The logic: the nth event has occurred by time t if and only if at least n events have occurred by time t. This lets you compute an "exact" gamma probability using only the Poisson pmf, without ever encountering the gamma density or its (annoying, factorial-and-integral-laden) CDF directly.

Example 6.8

A workshop's 3rd machine breakdown of the month occurs at a time T that is Gamma-distributed, being the sum of 3 iid Exponential(mean 5 days) inter-breakdown times. Find P(T ≤ 10) (the probability the 3rd breakdown happens within the first 10 days) using the Poisson-count method.

Show Solution

Breakdown rate: 1/θ = 1/5 = 0.2 per day, so over t=10 days, the count of breakdowns N(10) ~ Poisson(10 × 0.2) = Poisson(2).

P(T≤10) = P(N(10) ≥ 3) = 1 − P(N(10)=0) − P(N(10)=1) − P(N(10)=2)

= 1 − e^{-2} − 2e^{-2} − (4/2)e^{-2} = 1 − e^{-2}(1+2+2) = 1−5e^{-2} ≈ 1−5(0.135335) ≈ 1−0.676676 ≈ 0.3233.

6.7b Shape of the Gamma Distribution as α Grows

The gamma density's shape depends strongly on the shape parameter α. For α ≤ 1, the density is highest at (or decreasing from) x=0 — a strongly right-skewed shape (the exponential, α=1, is the boundary case, decreasing monotonically from its peak at 0). For α>1, the density rises from 0, peaks at an interior mode, and then decays — an increasingly symmetric, bell-like hump as α grows large. This is not a coincidence: because a Gamma(n,θ) variable (integer n) is a sum of n iid Exponential(θ) variables, the Central Limit Theorem (a Part III topic) implies that as n grows, the shape of the gamma distribution approaches that of a normal distribution with the same mean (nθ) and variance (nθ²) — even though the gamma is always technically right-skewed for any finite α, in the sense that its skewness coefficient 2/√α shrinks toward 0 as α grows without bound.

6.9 A Uniform/Exponential Comparison Example

Example 6.9

Two severity models are proposed for the same line of business: Model A is Uniform on [0, 2000]; Model B is Exponential with mean 1000 (chosen to match Model A's mean). Compare the two models' standard deviations and their probabilities of exceeding 1500.

Show Solution

Model A: E[X]=1000 (matches by construction). Var(X) = 2000²/12 ≈ 333,333, SD(X) ≈ 577.35. P(X>1500) = (2000−1500)/2000 = 0.25.

Model B: E[X]=1000. Var(X)=1000²=1,000,000, SD(X)=1000. P(X>1500) = e^{-1500/1000} = e^{-1.5} ≈ 0.2231.

Even with identical means, the two models disagree substantially on spread (SD 577 vs. 1000) and, less dramatically here, on the tail probability (0.25 vs. 0.223). This is a useful reminder that matching only the mean of two severity models says very little about how they will behave in the tail — a region of particular importance for insurance applications, since large, rare losses (the tail) are often what matters most for solvency and reinsurance decisions.

6.11 A Gamma-Distributed Aggregate Claims Example

Example 6.10

A regional office processes claims that require, on average, an Exponential(mean 45 minutes) amount of adjuster time each. An adjuster plans to work on exactly 6 claims back-to-back in a shift, and the claims' processing times are independent. (a) Identify the distribution of the total time T required, with its parameters. (b) Find the probability the adjuster finishes all 6 claims within a standard 5-hour (300-minute) shift, using the Poisson-count method of Section 6.7.

Show Solution

(a) T is the sum of 6 iid Exponential(45) times, so T ~ Gamma(α=6, θ=45), with E[T]=6(45)=270 minutes and Var(T)=6(45²)=12,150, SD(T)=√12,150≈110.2 minutes.

(b) By the Poisson-count correspondence, the adjuster's 6th claim is finished by time t=300 if and only if at least 6 "events" (claim completions) have occurred in a Poisson process with rate 1/45 per minute by that time. The mean count by t=300 is 300/45 ≈ 6.667.

P(T≤300) = P(\text{Poisson}(6.667) ≥ 6). Computing the complementary sum P(N≤5) for Poisson(6.667) term by term, using P(0)=e^{-6.667}≈0.001273 and the recursive relation P(k)=P(k−1)·(6.667/k), gives P(0)≈0.00127, P(1)≈0.00848, P(2)≈0.02828, P(3)≈0.06284, P(4)≈0.10473, P(5)≈0.13964, summing to P(N≤5)≈0.345. So P(T≤300) ≈ 1−0.345 = 0.655.

This example is a good reminder that even when a direct gamma-CDF calculation would be cumbersome by hand, the Poisson-count reformulation converts it into a manageable (if slightly tedious) sum of Poisson pmf terms — always available whenever the gamma's shape parameter is a positive integer.

6.11b A Closing Note on Parameterization Conventions

Before leaving this chapter, note explicitly that some texts and problems parameterize the exponential and gamma distributions using a rate λ instead of a mean/scale θ=1/λ. Every formula in this chapter has been given in terms of θ because it interfaces most directly with the limited-expected-value machinery of Chapter 9, but always check a problem's notation before substituting: if a problem states "exponential with parameter 0.02," determine from context whether 0.02 is the rate (giving mean 50) or the mean itself (giving rate 0.02) — the two conventions produce very different numeric answers, and this ambiguity is exactly as consequential as the geometric-parameterization trap of Chapter 4.

6.13 Chapter Summary (partial)

Continuous Distributions I
Distribution PDF E[X] Var(X)
Uniform(a,b) 1/(b−a) (a+b)/2 (b−a)²/12
Exponential(θ=1/λ) λe−λx θ θ²
Gamma(α,θ) xα−1e−x/θ/(Γ(α)θα) αθ αθ²

7. Continuous Distributions II: Normal & Beta

Learning Outcomes Covered: Understand continuous univariate distributions including normal and beta, and their applications; calculate probabilities, percentiles, and moments for these families.

The normal distribution is familiar from general statistics, but Exam P tests it in specific, mechanical ways: standardizing to the Z-scale and reading probabilities and percentiles off the standard normal table are skills that must become fully automatic, since they reappear throughout Part III (sums and averages of random variables, the Central Limit Theorem) in addition to Part II itself. The beta distribution appears less frequently but is the standard model whenever a problem involves a random proportion or fraction — recognize its role as a natural counterpart to the continuous uniform (which is itself the special case Beta(1,1)).

7.1 Normal Distribution

The normal (Gaussian) distribution is the familiar symmetric "bell curve," fully determined by its mean μ and variance σ².

NORMAL(μ, σ²) PDFf(x) = (1/(σ√(2π)))·e−(x−μ)²/(2σ²), −∞ < x < ∞
NORMAL MEAN & VARIANCEE[X] = μ, Var(X) = σ²

Because this pdf has no elementary antiderivative, probabilities are never computed by direct integration on the exam. Instead, every normal calculation is converted to the standard normal distribution, Z ~ N(0,1), via standardization.

Standardization

STANDARDIZATIONZ = (X − μ)/σ ~ N(0,1)

Standardizing converts any probability statement about X into an equivalent statement about Z, which can then be looked up in the standard normal table (typically giving Φ(z) = P(Z ≤ z)). Key facts about the standard normal table:

  • Φ(z) is the CDF of Z; Φ(0) = 0.5 by symmetry.
  • Symmetry: Φ(−z) = 1 − Φ(z).
  • To find a percentile of X, first find the corresponding z-percentile from the table, then un-standardize: x = μ + zσ.

The Empirical Rule

EMPIRICAL RULE (68-95-99.7)P(μ−σ < X < μ+σ) ≈ 0.68, P(μ−2σ < X < μ+2σ) ≈ 0.95, P(μ−3σ < X < μ+3σ) ≈ 0.997

This is an extremely useful sanity check and mental-math shortcut: about 68% of a normal distribution's probability lies within 1 standard deviation of the mean, about 95% within 2, and virtually all (99.7%) within 3.

Example 7.1

Claim sizes for a book of business are normally distributed with mean 800 and standard deviation 150. Find P(X > 1000) and the 90th percentile of claim size.

Show Solution

Z = (1000−800)/150 = 1.333. From the standard normal table, Φ(1.33) ≈ 0.9082, so P(X>1000) = 1 − 0.9082 = 0.0918.

90th percentile: from the table, Φ(z) = 0.90 at z ≈ 1.2816. Un-standardize: x = 800 + 1.2816(150) ≈ 800 + 192.2 = 992.2.

Example 7.2

An insurer's aggregate annual losses are normal with mean 12,000 and standard deviation 4,500. Using only the empirical rule (no table), estimate the probability that annual losses exceed 21,000.

Show Solution

21,000 = 12,000 + 2(4,500), i.e., exactly 2 standard deviations above the mean. By the empirical rule, P(|X−μ| < 2σ) ≈ 0.95, so P(X > μ+2σ) ≈ (1−0.95)/2 = 0.025 (splitting the remaining 5% evenly between the two tails, valid by symmetry).

Common mistake: Never forget to standardize before consulting the normal table — the table only gives probabilities for the standard normal (mean 0, variance 1). A raw x-value plugged directly into the table lookup is meaningless unless μ=0, σ=1 already.

7.2 Beta Distribution

The beta distribution is supported on the finite interval [0,1], making it the natural model for random proportions or probabilities — e.g., the fraction of a policy limit paid out, the proportion of a portfolio in default, or a random probability parameter itself.

BETA(a, b) PDFf(x) = [Γ(a+b)/(Γ(a)Γ(b))]·xa−1(1−x)b−1, 0 < x < 1
BETA MEAN & VARIANCEE[X] = a/(a+b), Var(X) = ab / [(a+b)²(a+b+1)]

Here a > 0 and b > 0 are shape parameters that control where the density concentrates: a > b skews mass toward 1, a < b skews mass toward 0, and a = b gives a distribution symmetric about 0.5. When a = b = 1, the beta distribution reduces exactly to the continuous Uniform(0,1) — another useful special-case sanity check.

Example 7.3

The proportion of a liability policy's limit that is ultimately paid out on a claim that reaches the limit review process follows a Beta distribution with a=2, b=3. Find the mean and variance of this proportion.

Show Solution

E[X] = 2/(2+3) = 2/5 = 0.4.

Var(X) = (2)(3) / [(5)²(6)] = 6/150 = 0.04, so SD(X) = 0.2.

Example 7.4

A random proportion R has Beta(a,b) density with a=1, b=2: f(r) = 2(1−r), for 0<r<1. Verify this is a valid Beta density and find P(R > 0.5).

Show Solution

General Beta(1,2) pdf: f(r) = [Γ(3)/(Γ(1)Γ(2))] r0(1−r)1 = [2!/(0!·1!)](1−r) = 2(1−r). ✓ matches.

P(R>0.5) = ∫0.51 2(1−r) dr = [2r − r²]0.51 = (2−1) − (1−0.25) = 1 − 0.75 = 0.25.

7.3 Normal Approximation and Sums of Independent Normals

A fact used constantly, both within this chapter and in Part III (sums of random variables): if X₁,…,Xₙ are independent, each Xᵢ ~ N(μᵢ, σᵢ²), then any linear combination is itself normal:

LINEAR COMBINATION OF INDEPENDENT NORMALSa₁X₁+⋯+aₙXₙ ~ N(Σaᵢμᵢ, Σaᵢ²σᵢ²)

This "closure under linear combinations" is special to the normal family (it is not true in general for, say, exponentials — recall from Chapter 6 that a sum of independent exponentials is gamma, not exponential). It means that once you know a total or an average of independent normal variables is itself normal, you only need its mean and variance (found via linearity of expectation and additivity of variance for independent terms) to answer any probability or percentile question about it — via standardization, exactly as for a single normal variable.

Example 7.5

Total annual claims in an insurer's auto division, X, are normal with mean 10 and standard deviation 3 (in millions). Total annual claims in the property division, Y, are normal with mean 12 and standard deviation 4, independent of X. Find P(X+Y > 25).

Show Solution

X+Y ~ N(10+12, 3²+4²) = N(22, 25), so SD(X+Y)=5.

Z = (25−22)/5 = 0.6. P(X+Y>25) = 1−Φ(0.6) = 1−0.7257 = 0.2743.

7.4 Additional Beta Distribution Example

Example 7.6

The fraction of a reinsurance layer exhausted by a large claim has a Beta distribution with mean 0.3 and it is known that a+b=10. Find a and b, then find Var(X).

Show Solution

E[X]=a/(a+b)=a/10=0.3 ⟹ a=3. Then b=10−3=7.

Var(X) = ab/[(a+b)²(a+b+1)] = (3)(7)/[(100)(11)] = 21/1100 ≈ 0.01909.

7.4b Two Additional Normal Examples

Example 7.7

Two measuring instruments each independently estimate a tower's true height h with error normally distributed with mean 0. Instrument A's error has standard deviation 0.006h; instrument B's error has standard deviation 0.004h. Find the probability that the average of the two measurements is within 0.005h of the true height.

Show Solution

Let E_A, E_B be the two independent errors, each mean 0. The average measurement's error is (E_A+E_B)/2, which is normal with mean 0 and variance [(0.006h)²+(0.004h)²]/4 = [0.000036h²+0.000016h²]/4 = 0.000052h²/4 = 0.000013h².

Standard deviation of the average error: √0.000013 · h ≈ 0.0036056h.

We want P(|(E_A+E_B)/2| < 0.005h). Standardizing: z = 0.005h/0.0036056h ≈ 1.387.

P(|Z|<1.387) = 2Φ(1.387)−1 ≈ 2(0.9173)−1 = 0.8346.

Example 7.8

Exam scores are normal with mode 56 (recall the normal distribution's mode equals its mean by symmetry) and the 40th percentile is 50. Find the standard deviation.

Show Solution

Mean μ=56 (mode = mean for a normal distribution). The 40th percentile corresponds to z ≈ −0.2533 (from the table, Φ(−0.25)≈0.4013, close enough to 0.40).

50 = 56 + (−0.2533)σ ⟹ −6 = −0.2533σ ⟹ σ ≈ 23.69.

7.4c Normal Approximation to Discrete Distributions

Because a Binomial(n,p) random variable is a sum of n iid Bernoulli variables, and (as will be developed fully in Part III's Central Limit Theorem material) sums of many independent, identically-distributed variables tend toward a normal shape, the binomial (and, similarly, the Poisson for large λ) can be approximated by a normal distribution matching its mean and variance when n is large:

NORMAL APPROXIMATION TO THE BINOMIALBinomial(n,p) ≈ Normal(np, np(1−p)) for large n, especially when np(1−p) ≥ 10 or so

Because the binomial is discrete and the normal is continuous, a continuity correction of ±0.5 is conventionally applied when approximating a discrete probability with a continuous one: e.g., P(X≤k) is approximated using P(\text{Normal} ≤ k+0.5), since the discrete value k "occupies" the continuous interval from k−0.5 to k+0.5 under the approximation.

Example 7.9

A block of 400 independent policies each have probability 0.3 of renewal. Approximate the probability that at least 130 policies renew.

Show Solution

Exact model: X ~ Binomial(400, 0.3), E[X]=120, Var(X)=400(0.3)(0.7)=84, SD(X)=√84≈9.165.

Approximate X by N(120,84), and apply the continuity correction: P(X≥130) ≈ P(\text{Normal} ≥ 129.5).

Z = (129.5−120)/9.165 ≈ 1.037. P(Z≥1.037) = 1−Φ(1.037) ≈ 1−0.8501 = 0.1499.

Common mistake: Forgetting the continuity correction is a small error for large n but can meaningfully shift an answer for moderate n; when a problem's answer choices are close together, apply it. Some SOA problems accept the answer either way, but defaulting to including it is the safer habit.

7.4d A Combined Normal/Beta Review Example

Example 7.10

An insurer models the proportion of a catastrophe reinsurance layer that will be exhausted in a bad year as R ~ Beta(a=2,b=2). Separately, the insurer's total annual surplus change (in millions) is modeled as Normal with mean 5 and standard deviation 8. (a) Find P(R > 0.75). (b) Find the probability the insurer's surplus decreases (i.e., is negative) in a given year.

Show Solution

(a) Beta(2,2) has pdf f(r) = 6r(1−r) for 0<r<1 (using Γ(4)/[Γ(2)Γ(2)] = 6/(1·1) = 6). P(R>0.75) = ∫_{0.75}^1 6r(1−r)dr = 6∫_{0.75}^1 (r−r²)dr = 6\big[r²/2−r³/3\big]_{0.75}^1.

At r=1: 1/2−1/3=1/6. At r=0.75: 0.28125−0.140625=0.140625. Difference: 1/6−0.140625 ≈ 0.166667−0.140625=0.026042. Times 6: P(R>0.75) ≈ 0.15625.

(b) Z = (0−5)/8 = −0.625. P(\text{surplus}<0) = Φ(−0.625) ≈ 1−Φ(0.625) ≈ 1−0.7340 = 0.2660.

7.4e Percentile of a Sum of Independent Normals

Example 7.11

A reinsurer's combined annual result across two independent treaties is W = T₁+T₂, where T₁ ~ N(3, 4) and T₂ ~ N(5, 9) (in millions, variances given directly). Find the 5th percentile of W.

Show Solution

W ~ N(3+5, 4+9) = N(8, 13), so SD(W)=√13≈3.6056.

The 5th percentile corresponds to z≈−1.645. w = 8 + (−1.645)(3.6056) ≈ 8−5.931 ≈ 2.069.

Key point: the variances of the two independent components add directly (13 = 4+9); only under independence can you skip straight to the sum's variance without any covariance term, exactly as emphasized in Chapter 3's variance-of-a-sum rule.

7.4f A Note on the Standard Normal Table's Two Common Layouts

Standard normal tables are typically presented in one of two layouts, and it is worth being comfortable with both since different sources (and different exam administrations historically) have used each: a table giving Φ(z)=P(Z≤z) directly for z ranging from roughly −3.49 to 3.49, or a table giving only Φ(z) for z≥0 together with the symmetry relationship Φ(−z)=1−Φ(z) needed to handle negative z-values. If your table only lists non-negative z, always apply the symmetry relationship explicitly (rather than trying to "read backward") whenever a computed z-score is negative — this is a frequent source of sign errors under time pressure.

7.8 Comprehensive Continuous Distribution Summary Table

All Continuous Distributions on the Syllabus (Chapters 6 & 7)
Distribution PDF Parameters E[X] Var(X) Typical Use
Uniform(a,b) 1/(b−a) a<b (a+b)/2 (b−a)²/12 Equally likely values in a range
Exponential(θ) λe−λx, λ=1/θ θ>0 θ θ² Waiting time / memoryless duration
Gamma(α,θ) xα−1e−x/θ/(Γ(α)θα) α,θ>0 αθ αθ² Sum of exponentials; skewed positive data
Normal(μ,σ²) (1/(σ√2π))e−(x−μ)²/2σ² μ∈ℝ, σ>0 μ σ² Symmetric, aggregate/averaged quantities
Beta(a,b) [Γ(a+b)/(Γ(a)Γ(b))]xa−1(1−x)b−1 a,b>0 a/(a+b) ab/[(a+b)²(a+b+1)] Random proportions on [0,1]
Exam tip: When a problem gives you a pdf you don't immediately recognize, check whether it matches (up to a constant) one of the "kernel" shapes above: xk alone → beta or uniform-related; e−cx → exponential; xke−cx → gamma; e−c(x−m)² → normal. Recognizing the kernel instantly gives you the normalizing constant and the mean/variance formulas for free, without any integration.

8. Insurance Applications: Deductibles, Coinsurance, Policy Limits & Inflation

Learning Outcomes Covered: (e) Calculate the amount that an insurance company pays to a policyholder for a claim given policy information, including deductibles, coinsurance percentages, and benefit limits, as well as other factors, such as inflation.

This chapter is where Part II becomes distinctly actuarial rather than purely probabilistic, and it is one of the most heavily and consistently tested applied topics on the entire exam. Every idea here is, mechanically, "just" a piecewise function applied to a random variable whose distribution you already know how to work with from Chapters 4–7 — but the challenge is almost entirely in correctly translating policy language (deductibles, coinsurance, limits, franchise provisions, inflation trending) into that piecewise function without error. Expect several problems per sitting to draw directly on this chapter, frequently in combination with the exponential distribution's memoryless property from Chapter 6.

8.1 Loss vs. Payment Random Variables

Throughout this chapter, X denotes the ground-up loss — the full amount of damage or claim incurred, before any policy provisions are applied — and Y denotes the payment — the amount the insurer actually disburses to the policyholder, after deductibles, coinsurance, and limits have been applied to X. Nearly every insurance-application problem on Exam P asks you to build Y as a function of X and then compute a probability, percentile, mean, or variance of Y (moments of Y are covered fully in Chapter 9; this chapter focuses on correctly constructing the payment function itself).

8.2 Ordinary Deductibles

An ordinary deductible of d means the policyholder absorbs the first d of any loss, and the insurer pays the excess, if any:

PAYMENT UNDER AN ORDINARY DEDUCTIBLEY = (X − d)+ = max(X − d, 0)

The notation (X−d)+ ("positive part") is standard actuarial shorthand meaning "X−d if positive, else 0." Under an ordinary deductible, if the loss doesn't exceed d, the insurer pays nothing at all — not even a partial payment.

8.3 Franchise Deductibles

A franchise deductible works differently: if the loss exceeds d, the insurer pays the entire loss (not just the excess over d); if the loss does not exceed d, the insurer still pays nothing.

PAYMENT UNDER A FRANCHISE DEDUCTIBLEY = 0 if X ≤ d; Y = X if X > d
Common mistake — ordinary vs. franchise deductible: This distinction is one of the most heavily tested traps in this chapter. Under an ordinary deductible, the insurer's payment given that a loss exceeds d is X − d. Under a franchise deductible, given that a loss exceeds d, the insurer's payment is the full X, with no subtraction. The franchise deductible payment is always at least as large as the ordinary deductible payment for the same loss (it equals the ordinary payment plus d, whenever a payment is triggered at all) — franchise deductibles reward the policyholder once the threshold is crossed, at the cost of a discontinuous jump in the payment function at x=d.

8.4 The Loss Elimination Ratio

The loss elimination ratio (LER) measures the fraction of expected losses "eliminated" (i.e., shifted onto the policyholder) by imposing an ordinary deductible of d:

LOSS ELIMINATION RATIOLER(d) = E[X ∧ d] / E[X] = 1 − E[Y]/E[X]

where E[X ∧ d] = E[min(X,d)] is the limited expected value at d (developed fully in Chapter 9). The LER answers questions like "if the insurer imposes a $500 deductible, what fraction of total expected losses will now be borne by policyholders instead of the insurer?" It is directly used to solve for an unknown deductible given a target reduction in expected claim cost — a very common problem type (see Example 8.4 and several practice problems).

8.5 Coinsurance

Coinsurance means the insurer pays only a fixed percentage α (0 < α ≤ 1) of the covered loss, with the policyholder responsible for the remaining 1−α. Coinsurance is typically applied after the deductible is subtracted:

PAYMENT UNDER DEDUCTIBLE + COINSURANCEY = α·max(X − d, 0) = α·(X−d)+

8.6 Policy Limits

A policy limit u caps the maximum amount the insurer will pay on a single claim, regardless of how large the loss is. Combined with a deductible, the limit is usually expressed as a cap on the amount paid (equivalently, losses above d + u are all paid the same maximum amount u):

PAYMENT UNDER DEDUCTIBLE + LIMIT (NO COINSURANCE)Y = min(max(X−d, 0), u) = (X∧(d+u)) − (X∧d)

8.7 Combining All Three: Deductible, Coinsurance & Limit

The fully general payment structure combining an ordinary deductible d, coinsurance rate α, and maximum covered loss u (so that the maximum possible payment is αu) is:

GENERAL PAYMENT FORMULAY = α · min( max(X−d, 0), u ) = α · [ (X∧(d+u)) − (X∧d) ]

Read this piece by piece: max(X−d,0) applies the deductible; wrapping that in min(·, u) applies the limit to the amount otherwise payable in excess of the deductible; multiplying by α applies coinsurance last. Different textbooks and problems sometimes state the limit u as a cap on the loss itself rather than on the excess-of-deductible amount — always re-derive the payment function directly from the words of the problem rather than pattern-matching to a formula, since the placement of the cap changes the answer.

Exam tip: Build the payment function as a clearly labeled piecewise function before computing anything: Y = 0 for X ≤ d; Y = α(X−d) for d < X ≤ d+u; Y = αu for X > d+u. Writing out these three pieces explicitly, with their boundaries, prevents almost every error in this chapter.

8.8 Inflation (Trend)

When losses are subject to inflation at rate r before policy terms are applied, the loss random variable used in the payment formula is the trended loss X′ = (1+r)X, not the original X. Deductibles, coinsurance, and limits stated in the policy are fixed dollar amounts that do not automatically inflate, so trending increases the loss but not the deductible/limit — this asymmetry is exactly what drives the classic "how does inflation affect expected payment" problem type.

PAYMENT WITH INFLATIONY = α · min( max((1+r)X − d, 0), u )
Common mistake: Do not inflate the deductible or the limit along with the loss — only the ground-up loss X gets trended by (1+r). Confusing this leads to systematically wrong expected payments, since a fixed deductible becomes relatively smaller (and hence "bites less") as losses inflate around it, increasing the insurer's expected payment share.

8.9 Worked Multi-Part Examples

Example 8.1 — Deductible only

Losses follow an Exponential distribution with mean 300. A policy has an ordinary deductible of 100. Given that a loss occurs and exceeds the deductible, find the 80th percentile of the insurer's payment.

Show Solution

Given X > 100, by the memoryless property of the exponential, X − 100 | X > 100 ~ Exponential(mean 300) — the same distribution as the original, unshifted loss! So we simply need the 80th percentile of an Exponential(300): solve 1 − e−m/300 = 0.80 ⟹ m = −300 ln(0.20) = 300(1.6094) ≈ 482.9.

Boxed answer: ≈ 482.9. This "memoryless shortcut" — the excess-of-deductible loss for an exponential is itself exponential with the same mean — is one of the most valuable shortcuts in this entire chapter.

Example 8.2 — Deductible + coinsurance + limit, multi-part

Losses under an auto policy are uniformly distributed on [0, 2000]. The policy has an ordinary deductible of 200, coinsurance of 80% (insurer pays 80% of the loss in excess of the deductible), and a maximum payment per claim of 1000. (a) Write the payment Y as a function of the loss X. (b) Find the loss level at which the insurer's payment first hits its maximum. (c) Find P(Y = 0). (d) Find P(Y = 1000) (the payment is "capped").

Show Solution

(a) The uncapped coinsured payment is 0.8(X−200) for X>200. This hits the cap of 1000 when 0.8(X−200) = 1000 ⟹ X−200 = 1250 ⟹ X = 1450. So:

Y = 0 for X ≤ 200; Y = 0.8(X−200) for 200 < X ≤ 1450; Y = 1000 for X > 1450.

(b) As found above, X = 1450.

(c) P(Y=0) = P(X ≤ 200) = 200/2000 = 0.10 (uniform CDF).

(d) P(Y=1000) = P(X > 1450) = (2000−1450)/2000 = 550/2000 = 0.275.

Example 8.3 — Inflation applied before policy terms

This year's losses X are Exponential with mean 1000. Next year, losses are expected to inflate by 10%, i.e. next year's loss is X′=1.1X. The policy (unchanged from this year to next) has an ordinary deductible of 200. Find next year's expected payment E[Y′] where Y′ = max(X′−200,0), and compare to this year's expected payment.

Show Solution

This year: E[Y] = E[max(X−200,0)]. For an exponential with mean θ, the standard result (derived in Chapter 9) is E[max(X−d,0)] = θe−d/θ. So E[Y] = 1000·e−200/1000 = 1000e−0.2 ≈ 818.73.

Next year, X′ = 1.1X is Exponential with mean 1.1(1000)=1100 (an exponential scaled by a positive constant is still exponential, with mean scaled by the same constant). So E[Y′] = 1100·e−200/1100 = 1100·e−0.1818 ≈ 1100(0.8337) ≈ 917.1.

Expected payment rose from about 818.73 to about 917.1 — an increase of roughly 12.0%, which exceeds the 10% loss inflation rate. This illustrates the general principle: with a fixed deductible, inflation in the underlying losses always increases the insurer's expected payment by a larger percentage than the loss inflation rate itself, because the fixed deductible "erodes" in relative terms.

Example 8.4 — Solving for an unknown deductible

Repair-cost claims under a home warranty plan are uniformly distributed on [0, 250]. A deductible d is chosen so that the expected claim payment (net of the deductible) equals 20. Find d.

Show Solution

For Uniform(0,250), E[(X−d)+] = ∫d250(x−d)(1/250)dx = (1/250)·(250−d)²/2 = (250−d)²/500.

Set this equal to 20: (250−d)² = 10,000 ⟹ 250−d = 100 ⟹ d = 150 (taking the positive root with d<250; the other algebraic root, d=350, is rejected since it exceeds the maximum possible loss of 250).

Boxed answer: d = 150.

8.10 Franchise Deductible Worked Example

Example 8.5

Losses are uniformly distributed on [0, 1500]. Compare the insurer's expected payment under (a) an ordinary deductible of 250, and (b) a franchise deductible of 250.

Show Solution

(a) Ordinary deductible: E[(X−250)_+] = ∫_{250}^{1500}(x−250)(1/1500)dx = (1/1500)·(1250)²/2 = (1/1500)(781,250) ≈ 520.83.

(b) Franchise deductible: payment is 0 for X≤250, and the full loss X for X>250. E[Y] = ∫_{250}^{1500} x(1/1500)dx = (1/1500)[x²/2]_{250}^{1500} = (1/1500)[(1,125,000)−(31,250)] = (1/1500)(1,093,750) ≈ 729.17.

The franchise deductible produces a materially larger expected payment (729.17 vs. 520.83) — exactly 250·P(X>250) larger, in fact, since the franchise payment always equals the ordinary-deductible payment plus d whenever any payment is triggered at all: 729.17 − 520.83 = 208.34 ≈ 250 × (1250/1500) = 250(0.8333) = 208.3 ✓. This algebraic relationship — E[Y_{franchise}] = E[Y_{ordinary}] + d·P(X>d) — is a fast cross-check whenever both types of deductible appear in the same problem.

8.11 A Second Multi-Feature Worked Example

Example 8.6

A commercial property policy has an ordinary deductible of 1,000, coinsurance of 90% above the deductible, and a policy limit such that the maximum possible payment on any one claim is 50,000. Losses X follow an Exponential distribution with mean 20,000. (a) Find the loss level at which the maximum payment is first reached. (b) Find the probability that a loss produces the maximum payment. (c) Find the probability that the insurer pays nothing at all.

Show Solution

(a) The uncapped coinsured payment is 0.9(X−1000). Setting this equal to the maximum payment of 50,000: 0.9(X−1000)=50,000 ⟹ X−1000 = 55,555.6 ⟹ X = 56,555.6.

(b) P(X ≥ 56,555.6) = e^{-56,555.6/20,000} = e^{-2.8278} ≈ 0.0592.

(c) P(\text{payment}=0) = P(X ≤ 1000) = 1−e^{-1000/20,000} = 1−e^{-0.05} ≈ 1−0.9512 = 0.0488.

8.12 A Note on How "Limit" Can Be Stated

Policy limits are a frequent source of ambiguity in word problems because they can be described in at least two ways, and the resulting payment formula differs depending on which is meant:

  • Maximum payment u: the insurer will never pay more than u on a single claim, regardless of the size of the loss in excess of the deductible. This gives Y = min(max(X−d,0), u), as used throughout this chapter.
  • Maximum covered loss L: the policy simply stops covering any loss amount beyond L, so the payment (before coinsurance) is min(X,L) − min(X,d) = (X∧L)−(X∧d), which caps the maximum payment at L−d, not at some separately stated u.

These coincide exactly when u = L − d, so many problems can be solved either way once you notice the equivalence — but you must set up whichever description the problem actually gives, rather than assuming a "maximum payment" figure is the same number as a "maximum covered loss" figure.

Common mistake: If a problem states "the policy limit is L" without further clarification, check the surrounding context — some sources use "policy limit" to mean the maximum loss covered (so the deductible is subtracted afterward, capping the payment at L−d) and others use it to mean the maximum payment directly. When genuinely ambiguous, state your assumption explicitly as part of your work.

8.13 A Third Worked Example: Solving Backwards From a Target Expected Payment

Example 8.7

Losses are Exponential with mean 1000. An insurer wants to set an ordinary deductible d so that the expected payment is exactly 60% of the expected payment with no deductible at all. Find d.

Show Solution

With no deductible, expected payment is simply E[X] = 1000. We want E[(X−d)_+] = 0.6(1000) = 600.

Using the exponential shortcut from Chapter 9, E[(X−d)_+] = θe^{-d/θ} = 1000e^{-d/1000}. Set this equal to 600: e^{-d/1000} = 0.6 ⟹ −d/1000 = \ln(0.6) ⟹ d = −1000\ln(0.6) ≈ −1000(−0.5108) ≈ 510.8.

Boxed answer: d ≈ 510.8. Equivalently, the loss elimination ratio here is 1−0.6=0.4, i.e. imposing this deductible shifts 40% of expected losses onto the policyholder.

8.15 Per-Occurrence vs. Aggregate Provisions

Everything in this chapter so far has applied deductibles, coinsurance, and limits per occurrence — that is, separately to each individual loss. Some policies instead (or additionally) apply provisions on an aggregate basis, across all of a policyholder's losses within a period (typically a year). For example, a policy might have a $500 per-occurrence deductible and a $5,000 annual aggregate deductible, meaning the insurer pays nothing at all in a year until the policyholder's cumulative losses (net of the per-occurrence deductible) exceed $5,000, and pays only the excess beyond that point thereafter.

Aggregate provisions require you to first form the total loss for the period (usually as a sum of the individual per-occurrence losses or payments — a topic that properly belongs to Part III's treatment of sums of random variables, but the individual-occurrence piece is exactly the univariate payment function of this chapter) before applying the aggregate deductible/limit to that total. On Exam P, aggregate-provision problems are typically simplified to a small, explicit number of occurrences (often exactly one or two) specifically so that the "sum of losses" step stays elementary.

Example 8.8

A policyholder experiences exactly two independent losses in a year, each Uniform on [0, 1000]. The policy has no per-occurrence deductible, but has an aggregate (annual) deductible of 800 applied to the sum of the two losses, with the insurer paying the excess over 800 (no upper limit). Find the expected insurer payment for the year.

Show Solution

Let S = X₁+X₂ be the aggregate loss; we need E[(S−800)_+] = E[S] − E[S∧800].

E[S] = E[X₁]+E[X₂] = 500+500=1000 (linearity, regardless of independence).

Finding E[S∧800] requires the distribution of S, the sum of two independent Uniform(0,1000) variables — this is a triangular distribution on [0,2000], peaking at 1000 (a Part III topic: the convolution of two uniforms). Its density is g(s) = s/1,000,000 for 0≤s≤1000, and g(s) = (2000−s)/1,000,000 for 1000<s≤2000.

Since 800 < 1000, we only need the rising piece: E[S∧800] = ∫₀^{800} s·g(s)\,ds + 800·P(S>800).

∫₀^{800} s²/1,000,000\,ds = (800³/3)/1,000,000 = (512,000,000/3)/1,000,000 ≈ 170.667.

P(S≤800) = ∫₀^{800}g(s)ds = 800²/(2×1,000,000) = 640,000/2,000,000=0.32, so P(S>800)=0.68.

E[S∧800] ≈ 170.667 + 800(0.68) = 170.667+544 = 714.667.

E[(S−800)_+] = 1000 − 714.667 = 285.33.

This example illustrates why aggregate provisions are intrinsically harder than per-occurrence ones: even with only two losses, you must first derive the sum's distribution — a full univariate calculation is no longer sufficient on its own, though it remains the essential building block.

8.17 Recognizing the Payment Structure From Word Cues

A large fraction of the difficulty in this chapter's problems is linguistic, not mathematical — once the payment function is correctly identified, the calculus is routine. The following word cues are worth memorizing:

Word Cues for Payment Structure
Phrase in the problem Structural implication
"deductible of d" (no other qualifier) ordinary deductible: Y=(X−d)_+
"franchise deductible" or "the insurer pays the full loss once it exceeds d" franchise deductible: 0 below d, full X above d
"insurer pays α% of the loss" / "coinsurance rate α" multiply the (already-deductible-adjusted) payment by α
"maximum payment of u" / "the policy will not pay more than u" cap the final payment at u
"policy limit of L" (loss-side, ambiguous) check context: often means max covered loss, giving max payment L−d
"losses are trended/inflated by r before the policy responds" replace X with (1+r)X throughout
"annual aggregate deductible/limit" apply the provision to the sum of losses in the period, not to each loss individually

Whenever more than one of these phrases appears in the same problem, build the payment function feature by feature, applying them in the natural real-world order: trend the loss first (if applicable), then subtract the deductible, then apply the limit, then apply coinsurance last — reflecting the fact that coinsurance is normally computed as a percentage of whatever is left after the deductible and limit have already acted.

8.19 Comprehensive Worked Example: All Four Features Together

Example 8.9

This year's losses under a commercial umbrella policy are Exponential with mean 4,000. Next year, losses are expected to trend upward by 8% before any policy terms are applied. Next year's policy has an ordinary deductible of 500, coinsurance of 75% above the deductible, and a maximum payment per claim of 6,000. (a) Write next year's payment Y as an explicit function of this year's (untrended) loss variable X. (b) Find the loss level x (in terms of this year's untrended scale) at which the maximum payment is first reached. (c) Find the expected payment, E[Y].

Show Solution

(a) Next year's trended loss is X′ = 1.08X, which (Section 6.5b) is again Exponential, with mean 1.08(4000)=4320. The payment is built by applying the deductible, then the limit, then coinsurance, to the trended loss:

Y = 0.75 · min( max(X′−500, 0), 6000 ) = 0.75 · min( max(1.08X−500, 0), 6000 )

(b) The uncapped coinsured payment on the trended scale is 0.75(x′−500); it reaches the maximum payment of 6000 when 0.75(x′−500)=6000 ⟹ x′−500=8000 ⟹ x′=8500. Converting back to this year's untrended loss scale: x = x′/1.08 = 8500/1.08 ≈ 7870.4.

(c) Work entirely on the trended scale, since X′ is itself a clean Exponential(4320) variable. The cap on the trended loss (before coinsurance) is 500+8000=8500:

E[Y] = 0.75·\big(E[X′∧8500] − E[X′∧500]\big)

E[X′∧8500] = 4320(1−e^{-8500/4320}) = 4320(1−e^{-1.9676}) ≈ 4320(1−0.13944) ≈ 4320(0.86056) ≈ 3717.6

E[X′∧500] = 4320(1−e^{-500/4320}) = 4320(1−e^{-0.11574}) ≈ 4320(1−0.89072) ≈ 4320(0.10928) ≈ 472.1

E[Y] = 0.75(3717.6−472.1) = 0.75(3245.5) ≈ 2434.1.

This problem strings together every idea from this chapter in the natural order they would arise in practice: identify that trending simply rescales an exponential's mean (Chapter 6/8), build the piecewise payment function feature by feature per the word cues of Section 8.17, locate the breakpoint where the cap engages, and finally compute the expectation using the limited-expected-value shortcut of Chapter 9 — entirely on the trended scale, since that is the scale on which the policy terms actually operate.

8.21 Edge Cases Worth Checking

Two boundary situations are easy to overlook under time pressure but appear regularly enough to be worth a deliberate mental checklist:

  • Deductible at or above the maximum possible loss. If the loss distribution has a bounded support (e.g., Uniform on [0,b]) and the deductible d ≥ b, then P(\text{payment}>0)=0 — the insurer never pays anything at all, and E[Y]=0 exactly. Always sanity-check a computed deductible against the maximum possible loss (this is exactly why, in Examples 8.4 and in practice problems 20 and 27, one algebraic root of a quadratic is always rejected for exceeding the loss distribution's upper bound).
  • Zero deductible. With d=0, the ordinary-deductible and franchise-deductible payment formulas coincide exactly (both reduce to Y=X, or Y=αX under coinsurance) — the distinction between the two deductible types only matters once d>0.
