Common mistake: When solving an algebraic equation for an unknown deductible or limit (as in Examples 8.4, 8.7, and several practice problems), always check every root against the natural bounds of the problem (the loss distribution's support, and the requirement that a deductible or limit be non-negative) before reporting a final answer — spurious roots outside the feasible range are common and are usually the "distractor" answer choice on a multiple-choice exam.

8.23 A Percentile-of-Payment Example With Coinsurance and a Limit

Example 8.10

Losses under a commercial auto policy are Uniform on [0, 20,000]. The policy has no deductible, coinsurance of 90%, and a maximum payment of 9,000. Find the 90th percentile of the insurer's payment Y.

Show Solution

First locate the breakpoint where the cap engages: 0.9X = 9000 ⟹ X = 10,000. So Y = 0.9X for X≤10,000, and Y=9,000 (a point mass) for X>10,000.

P(X>10,000) = (20,000−10,000)/20,000 = 0.5, so P(Y=9,000)=0.5 — fully half of all losses produce the maximum payment. This means the CDF of Y jumps straight from F_Y(9000^-) = 0.5 to F_Y(9000)=1 exactly at the cap.

Since the target percentile (90th, i.e. F_Y(y)=0.9) falls strictly between 0.5 and 1, and the entire probability mass between those two values sits exactly at the single point y=9,000, the 90th percentile of Y is simply 9,000 — the point mass at the cap.

Boxed answer: 9,000. This is a useful edge case to recognize: whenever a target percentile falls within the "gap" created by a point mass (here, anywhere from the 50th to the 100th percentile), the answer is simply the location of that point mass, with no further calculation needed on the continuous piece at all.

8.24 Summary of Payment Structures

Payment Random Variable Y as a Function of Loss X
Policy Feature Payment Formula
No deductible, no limit (full coverage) Y = X
Ordinary deductible d Y = max(X−d, 0)
Franchise deductible d Y = 0 if X≤d; Y = X if X>d
Coinsurance α, deductible d Y = α·max(X−d,0)
Deductible d + policy limit u (max payment u) Y = min(max(X−d,0), u)
Deductible + coinsurance + limit Y = α·min(max(X−d,0), u)
With inflation at rate r before terms applied Replace X above with (1+r)X

9. Moments of Loss & Payment Variables

Learning Outcomes Covered: (f) Calculate the expected value, variance, and standard deviation of both the loss random variable and the corresponding payment amount random variable.

Chapter 8 taught you how to construct the payment random variable; this chapter teaches you how to efficiently compute its moments. The limited expected value function developed here is the single most useful computational shortcut in all of Part II's applied material — it converts what would otherwise be a fresh piecewise integral in every problem into a lookup-and-subtract operation, provided you have memorized its closed form for the exponential and uniform distributions (and know how to derive it for any other severity model a problem might supply). Between Chapters 8 and 9, expect this material to account for a substantial share of the exam's overall point total, given Univariate Random Variables' 44–50% weighting.

9.1 The Limited Expected Value (Expected Value Function)

The single most useful tool for computing moments of payment variables is the limited expected value (also called the expected value function), E[X ∧ u] = E[min(X,u)]: the expected loss, capped at u. It appears, directly or indirectly, in every formula for expected payments under a deductible and/or limit.

LIMITED EXPECTED VALUE — GENERAL FORMULAE[X∧u] = ∫0u x·f(x)dx + u·P(X>u) = ∫0u [1−F(x)]dx (for X ≥ 0)

The last equality — integrating the survival function S(x)=1−F(x) from 0 to u — is often the fastest computational route and is worth memorizing as an alternative to direct LOTUS integration.

Derivation for the Exponential Distribution

Let X ~ Exponential(θ), so S(x) = e−x/θ. Then:

E[X∧u] = ∫0u e−x/θ dx = θ(1 − e−u/θ)

LIMITED EXPECTED VALUE — EXPONENTIAL(θ)E[X∧u] = θ(1 − e−u/θ)

Note the sensible limit: as u→∞, E[X∧u] → θ = E[X] (capping at infinity is the same as not capping at all); as u→0, E[X∧u]→0.

Derivation for the Uniform Distribution

Let X ~ Uniform(0,b) and suppose 0 ≤ u ≤ b. Directly:

E[X∧u] = ∫0u x·(1/b) dx + u·P(X>u) = u²/(2b) + u·(b−u)/b = u²/(2b) + u − u²/b = u − u²/(2b)

LIMITED EXPECTED VALUE — UNIFORM(0,b), 0≤u≤bE[X∧u] = u − u²/(2b)

(For u > b, the loss can never exceed b, so E[X∧u] = E[X] = b/2.)

9.2 Expected Payment Under a Deductible via the Limited Expected Value

The key algebraic identity linking the limited expected value to the expected payment under an ordinary deductible d is:

EXPECTED PAYMENT UNDER A DEDUCTIBLEE[(X−d)+] = E[X] − E[X∧d]

Derivation

Write X = min(X,d) + max(X−d,0) — this is an identity true for every possible value of X (if X≤d, both sides equal X; if X>d, the right side is d + (X−d) = X). Taking expectations of both sides and using linearity: E[X] = E[X∧d] + E[(X−d)+], which rearranges to the boxed formula.

Combined with the deductible+limit result E[min(max(X−d,0),u)] = E[X∧(d+u)] − E[X∧d] (a direct consequence of the same "layering" idea — the expected payment in the layer from d to d+u equals the difference of two limited expected values), you can compute the expected payment for any combination of deductible and limit as soon as you have a formula for E[X∧u] at any cap level u.

EXPECTED PAYMENT — DEDUCTIBLE + LIMIT + COINSURANCEE[Y] = α·[ E[X∧(d+u)] − E[X∧d] ]
Example 9.1

Losses under a renters' insurance policy are Exponential with mean 8. The policy has an ordinary deductible of 2. Find the expected claim payment net of the deductible.

Show Solution

E[(X−2)+] = E[X] − E[X∧2] = θ − θ(1−e−2/θ) = θe−2/θ, using θ=8: = 8e−0.25 ≈ 8(0.778801) ≈ 6.230.

Boxed answer: ≈ 6.23. Note the clean general shortcut this reveals: for any exponential loss, E[(X−d)+] = θe−d/θ — the expected excess-of-deductible payment for an exponential loss is itself exactly θ times the survival probability at d, a direct echo of the memoryless property (Example 8.1): given a loss exceeds the deductible, the excess is again Exponential(θ), so the expected payment is θ times the probability of exceeding d in the first place.

9.3 Variance of the Payment Variable

Once the payment function Y = g(X) is written down explicitly, Var(Y) is computed exactly the same way as any other variance: find E[Y] and E[Y²] (both via LOTUS applied to the pieces of g), then apply the shortcut formula Var(Y) = E[Y²]−(E[Y])². There is no separate "shortcut formula" for E[Y²] analogous to the limited-expected-value trick — you generally must integrate/sum g(x)² directly against the pdf/pmf of X, restricted to the relevant piece(s) of the payment function.

Exam tip: When Y = α·(X−d)+ with no upper limit, it is often faster to work with the "excess loss" variable W = (X−d)+ first: note that, given X>d, W = X−d is just a shifted version of the conditional distribution of X given X>d. For the exponential specifically, memorylessness means this conditional distribution is exactly the original Exponential(θ), so Var(W | X>d) = θ² — but you must still account for the point mass at 0 (i.e., W=0 when X≤d) when computing the unconditional variance of W, since that mixes a point mass with a continuous piece.
Example 9.2

Losses under a marine cargo policy are uniformly distributed on [0,b]. The policy has an ordinary deductible of b/3 (no coinsurance, no limit). Find the ratio of Var(Y) (the claim payment) to Var(X) (the loss).

Show Solution

Var(X) = b²/12 (uniform variance formula).

Y = max(X−b/3, 0): Y=0 for X≤b/3 (probability 1/3), and Y=X−b/3 for X>b/3.

E[Y] = ∫b/3b(x−b/3)(1/b)dx = (1/b)·[(x−b/3)²/2]b/3b = (1/b)·(2b/3)²/2 = (1/b)(2b²/9) = 2b/9.

E[Y²] = ∫b/3b(x−b/3)²(1/b)dx = (1/b)·[(x−b/3)³/3]b/3b = (1/b)·(2b/3)³/3 = (1/b)(8b³/81) = 8b²/81.

Var(Y) = E[Y²]−(E[Y])² = 8b²/81 − 4b²/81 = 4b²/81.

Ratio: Var(Y)/Var(X) = (4b²/81)/(b²/12) = (4/81)(12) = 48/81 = 16/27.

Boxed answer: 16:27. Note that even though the deductible eliminates a third of all losses entirely (setting Y=0 whenever X≤b/3), the payment variance is reduced relative to the loss variance (16/27 < 1) — a deductible does not always reduce variance relative to the mean in the same proportion, and computing the exact ratio requires the full derivation above rather than a shortcut.

Example 9.3 — Combining deductible and coinsurance for variance

Losses are Exponential with mean 500. A policy pays 80% of the loss in excess of a deductible of 100 (no upper limit). Find E[Y] and Var(Y) for the insurer's payment Y.

Show Solution

Let W = (X−100)+, so Y = 0.8W. First find moments of W.

E[W] = E[X]−E[X∧100] = θe−100/θ = 500e−0.2 ≈ 500(0.818731) ≈ 409.37 (using the exponential shortcut from Example 9.1).

For E[W²]: split on whether X≤100 (contributes 0) or X>100. Given X>100, memorylessness says X−100 is Exponential(500), so E[W²] = P(X>100)·E[(Exponential(500))²] = e−0.2·[Var+mean²] = e−0.2(500²+500²) = e−0.2(2)(500²).

Numerically: e−0.2 ≈ 0.818731, so E[W²] ≈ 0.818731(2)(250000) = 0.818731(500000) ≈ 409,365.5.

Var(W) = E[W²]−(E[W])² ≈ 409,365.5 − (409.37)² ≈ 409,365.5 − 167,584 ≈ 241,781.5.

Now apply the coinsurance scaling: E[Y] = 0.8·E[W] ≈ 0.8(409.37) ≈ 327.5. Var(Y) = 0.8²·Var(W) = 0.64(241,781.5) ≈ 154,740.2, so SD(Y) ≈ 393.4.

9.4 Limited Expected Value for the Gamma and a Layering Example

The limited-expected-value technique of Section 9.1 extends to any severity distribution, though the integral can become more involved. For a Gamma(α,θ) with integer α, E[X∧u] can be expressed using the incomplete gamma function, which is generally left in integral form on this exam unless the problem supplies a simplification; the exponential (α=1) is the special case with the clean closed form of Section 9.1.

To see how the next-simplest case works, consider α=2 (an Erlang-2, i.e., the sum of two iid Exponential(θ) variables). Its pdf is f(x) = (x/θ²)e^{-x/θ}, and integrating by parts twice (exactly the antiderivative technique used in Example 9.7) gives the closed form:

LIMITED EXPECTED VALUE — GAMMA(α=2, θ)E[X∧u] = 2θ\big(1−e^{-u/θ}\big) − u\,e^{-u/θ}

This can be double-checked against the general mean: as u→∞, the formula gives 2θ(1)−0=2θ, matching E[X]=αθ=2θ for a Gamma(2,θ) variable exactly as required.

Example 9.3b

A machine's time to second failure (i.e., the sum of two iid Exponential(mean 300 hours) component lifetimes) is Gamma(2, 300). A maintenance contract covers this time up to a cap of 500 hours, after which the customer is responsible for arranging replacement. Find E[X∧500].

Show Solution

Using the boxed formula with θ=300, u=500: e^{-500/300}=e^{-1.6667}≈0.18888.

E[X∧500] = 2(300)(1−0.18888) − 500(0.18888) = 600(0.81112) − 94.44 ≈ 486.67 − 94.44 ≈ 392.2 hours.

As a sanity check, this is comfortably below both u=500 (the cap can never be exceeded by the limited expected value) and E[X]=2(300)=600 (capping can only reduce the expected value relative to the uncapped mean) — both necessary conditions for a limited-expected-value answer to be plausible.

A related and very testable idea is computing the expected payment for a layer of insurance — the portion of a loss between two thresholds d₁ < d₂, as would be paid by a reinsurer covering losses strictly between those two points:

EXPECTED VALUE OF A LAYERE[\text{layer from } d_1 \text{ to } d_2] = E[X∧d_2] − E[X∧d_1]
Example 9.4

Losses are Exponential with mean 5,000. A reinsurer covers the layer from 2,000 to 8,000 (i.e., pays the part of any loss between 2,000 and 8,000). Find the reinsurer's expected payment per loss.

Show Solution

E[X∧8000] = 5000(1−e^{-8000/5000}) = 5000(1−e^{-1.6}) ≈ 5000(1−0.201897) ≈ 5000(0.798103) ≈ 3990.5.

E[X∧2000] = 5000(1−e^{-2000/5000}) = 5000(1−e^{-0.4}) ≈ 5000(1−0.670320) ≈ 5000(0.329680) ≈ 1648.4.

Layer expected value: 3990.5 − 1648.4 = 2342.1.

9.5 A Fully Numeric Uniform Example Combining Mean and Variance of Payment

Example 9.5

Losses are uniform on [0, 1000]. A policy has an ordinary deductible of 200, no coinsurance, and no upper limit, so Y = max(X−200, 0). Find E[Y], Var(Y), and SD(Y).

Show Solution

E[X∧200] = 200 − 200²/(2·1000) = 200 − 20 = 180. E[X] = 500. So E[Y] = 500−180 = 320.

For E[Y²], integrate directly over the piece where Y>0: E[Y²] = ∫_{200}^{1000}(x−200)²(1/1000)dx = (1/1000)[(x−200)³/3]_{200}^{1000} = (1/1000)(800³/3) = (1/1000)(512,000,000/3) ≈ 170,666.7.

Var(Y) = E[Y²]−(E[Y])² ≈ 170,666.7 − 320² = 170,666.7 − 102,400 = 68,266.7.

SD(Y) = √68,266.7 ≈ 261.28.

9.7 Second Moment of the Limited Loss and a General Variance Route

Just as E[X∧u] is useful for means, its "second-moment" analogue E[(X∧u)²] is useful for variances. By the same layering identity as before (now squared), for a policy with only an ordinary deductible d (no limit, no coinsurance) and payment Y=(X−d)_+:

SECOND MOMENT OF THE PAYMENT (DEDUCTIBLE ONLY)E[Y²] = E[X²] − E[(X∧d)²] − 2d·(E[X]−E[X∧d])

This follows from expanding Y² = (X−d)_+^2 using the same decomposition X = (X∧d) + (X−d)_+ used in Section 9.2, squared and rearranged. In practice, on the exam it is usually just as fast (and less error-prone) to integrate (x−d)²f(x) directly over x>d, exactly as done in Examples 9.2 and 9.5 — the boxed identity here is offered mainly so you can recognize it if a solution presents the answer that way, and as a cross-check on a direct integration.

9.8 Variance of the Payment Under Coinsurance Alone (No Deductible)

Example 9.6

Losses X have mean 4,000 and variance 9,000,000 (standard deviation 3,000). A quota-share reinsurance treaty pays 25% of every loss, i.e. Y=0.25X, with no deductible. Find E[Y] and Var(Y).

Show Solution

Since Y is a pure linear transformation of X (no piecewise structure at all — coinsurance alone, without a deductible, never creates a point mass or a kink), the Chapter 3 rules apply directly: E[Y]=0.25(4000)=1000; Var(Y)=0.25²(9,000,000)=0.0625(9,000,000)=562,500, so SD(Y)=750.

This is a useful contrast with Examples 9.2, 9.3, and 9.5: coinsurance alone is "easy" (simple rescaling), while a deductible (with or without coinsurance) always requires the piecewise LOTUS treatment because it introduces a kink (and possibly a point mass) into the payment function.

9.9 A Fully Worked Deductible-and-Limit Variance Example

Example 9.7

Losses are Exponential with mean 2,000. A policy has an ordinary deductible of 500 and a maximum payment of 3,000 (so the loss is capped, for payment purposes, at d+u=3500). Find E[Y] and Var(Y) for the payment Y.

Show Solution

Mean: E[Y] = E[X∧3500] − E[X∧500].

E[X∧3500] = 2000(1−e^{-3500/2000}) = 2000(1−e^{-1.75}) ≈ 2000(1−0.173774) ≈ 2000(0.826226) ≈ 1652.45.

E[X∧500] = 2000(1−e^{-500/2000}) = 2000(1−e^{-0.25}) ≈ 2000(1−0.778801) ≈ 2000(0.221199) ≈ 442.40.

E[Y] ≈ 1652.45−442.40 = 1210.05.

Second moment (direct integration): Y equals 0 for X≤500; equals X−500 for 500<X≤3500; equals the constant 3000 for X>3500. So

E[Y²] = ∫_{500}^{3500}(x−500)²·0.0005e^{-x/2000}dx + 3000²·P(X>3500)

Substitute w=x−500, so the first integral becomes e^{-500/2000}∫_0^{3000}w²(1/2000)e^{-w/2000}dw = e^{-0.25}·I, where, using the standard antiderivative ∫w²λe^{-λw}dw = −e^{-λw}(w²+2w/λ+2/λ²) with λ=1/2000:

I = 2θ² − e^{-u/θ}(u²+2uθ+2θ²) = 8,000,000 − e^{-1.5}(9,000,000+12,000,000+8,000,000) = 8,000,000 − 0.223130(29,000,000) ≈ 8,000,000 − 6,470,776 ≈ 1,529,224

First term: e^{-0.25}(1,529,224) ≈ 0.778801(1,529,224) ≈ 1,190,955. Second term: 3000²·e^{-1.75} = 9,000,000(0.173774) ≈ 1,563,966.

E[Y²] ≈ 1,190,955 + 1,563,966 = 2,754,921.

Var(Y) ≈ 2,754,921 − (1210.05)² ≈ 2,754,921 − 1,464,221 ≈ 1,290,700, so SD(Y) ≈ 1136.1.

The general pattern to remember: whenever the payment function has three pieces (zero, a linear middle piece, and a flat cap), the second moment is always a sum of a truncated-exponential-second-moment piece and a constant-times-tail-probability piece, exactly as computed here.

9.11 The Loss Elimination Ratio Revisited With a Full Numeric Example

Section 8.4 defined the loss elimination ratio LER(d) = E[X∧d]/E[X]. With the limited-expected-value machinery of this chapter now fully in hand, LER calculations become routine plug-ins.

Example 9.8

Losses are Exponential with mean 2,500. Find the loss elimination ratio for a deductible of (a) 1,000, and (b) 5,000. Comment on the trend.

Show Solution

(a) E[X∧1000] = 2500(1−e^{-1000/2500}) = 2500(1−e^{-0.4}) ≈ 2500(0.329680) ≈ 824.2. LER(1000) = 824.2/2500 ≈ 0.3297.

(b) E[X∧5000] = 2500(1−e^{-5000/2500}) = 2500(1−e^{-2}) ≈ 2500(0.864665) ≈ 2161.7. LER(5000) = 2161.7/2500 ≈ 0.8647.

As the deductible grows from 1,000 to 5,000 (two times the mean), the LER rises from about 33% to about 86% — a large deductible relative to the mean eliminates the great majority of expected claim cost from the insurer's perspective, since most exponential losses fall well below several multiples of the mean. This exponential decay toward 100% as d→∞ is a direct consequence of E[X∧d]→E[X] in the limit.

9.13 A Policy-Limit-Only Example (No Deductible)

Example 9.9

Losses are Uniform on [0, 600]. A policy has no deductible but a maximum payment of 400 per claim, so Y = min(X, 400) = X∧400. Find E[Y] and Var(Y).

Show Solution

E[Y] = E[X∧400] = 400 − 400²/(2·600) = 400 − 160,000/1200 = 400−133.33 = 266.67 (using the uniform limited-expected-value formula with u=400 < b=600).

For E[Y²], split at the cap: Y=X for X≤400; Y=400 for X>400.

E[Y²] = ∫₀^{400} x²(1/600)dx + 400²·P(X>400) = (1/600)(400³/3) + 160,000(200/600)

= (1/600)(21,333,333.3) + 160,000(0.33333) ≈ 35,555.6 + 53,333.3 = 88,888.9.

Var(Y) = 88,888.9 − (266.67)² ≈ 88,888.9 − 71,111.6 ≈ 17,777.3, so SD(Y) ≈ 133.3.

Notice that a policy limit alone (no deductible) still creates a kink at x=u and a corresponding point mass at the maximum payment value (here, P(Y=400)=P(X>400)=200/600≈0.333) — structurally the mirror image of the point mass at zero created by a deductible.

9.15 A Comprehensive Review Example

Example 9.10

Losses under a small-business property policy are Exponential with mean 8,000. Next year, losses are expected to trend upward by 5%. Next year's policy has an ordinary deductible of 1,000, coinsurance of 80% above the deductible, and a maximum payment per claim of 10,000. Find (a) next year's expected payment, (b) the loss elimination ratio implied by the deductible alone (ignoring coinsurance and the limit), and (c) the probability that next year's payment is exactly at its maximum.

Show Solution

Next year's trended loss X′=1.05X is Exponential with mean 1.05(8000)=8400.

(a) Uncapped coinsured payment: 0.8(X′−1000), capped at 10,000 when X′−1000=12,500, i.e. X′=13,500. So the cap on the trended loss is 1000+12,500=13,500.

E[X′∧13,500] = 8400(1−e^{-13,500/8400}) = 8400(1−e^{-1.6071}) ≈ 8400(1−0.20055) ≈ 8400(0.79945) ≈ 6715.4

E[X′∧1000] = 8400(1−e^{-1000/8400}) = 8400(1−e^{-0.11905}) ≈ 8400(1−0.88771) ≈ 8400(0.11229) ≈ 943.2

E[\text{payment}] = 0.8(6715.4−943.2) = 0.8(5772.2) ≈ 4617.8.

(b) The loss elimination ratio for the deductible alone, applied to the trended loss and ignoring coinsurance/limit, is LER(1000) = E[X′∧1000]/E[X′] = 943.2/8400 ≈ 0.1123 — i.e., the 1,000 deductible by itself eliminates about 11.2% of expected trended losses from the insurer's perspective.

(c) The payment hits its maximum of 10,000 exactly when X′ ≥ 13,500: P(X′≥13,500) = e^{-13,500/8400} = e^{-1.6071} ≈ 0.2005.

This example draws together every idea in Chapters 8–9: inflation trending (which simply rescales the exponential's mean), the deductible-coinsurance-limit payment structure, the loss elimination ratio, and a tail probability of the trended loss — precisely the combination that dominates the applied portion of Part II on the actual exam.

9.17 A Franchise-Deductible Variance Example

Example 9.12

Losses are Uniform on [0, 1000]. A policy has a franchise deductible of 300 (recall: if the loss exceeds 300, the insurer pays the full loss; otherwise it pays nothing). Find E[Y] and Var(Y) for the payment Y.

Show Solution

Y=0 for X≤300 (probability 0.3); Y=X for X>300 (probability 0.7).

E[Y] = ∫_{300}^{1000} x(1/1000)dx = (1/1000)[x²/2]_{300}^{1000} = (1/1000)[500,000−45,000] = 455.

E[Y²] = ∫_{300}^{1000} x²(1/1000)dx = (1/1000)[x³/3]_{300}^{1000} = (1/1000)\big[(1,000,000,000−27,000,000)/3\big] = (1/1000)(324,333,333) = 324,333.3.

Var(Y) = 324,333.3 − 455² = 324,333.3−207,025 = 117,308.3, so SD(Y) ≈ 342.5.

Compare to the ordinary-deductible version of this same policy (deductible 300, no franchise): E[(X−300)_+] = E[X]−E[X∧300] = 500−(300−300²/2000) = 500−(300−45)=500−255=245 — noticeably smaller than the franchise expected payment of 455, exactly as the general relationship from Section 8.10 predicts (455−245=210=300×0.7, matching d·P(X>d)).

9.19 A Final Comprehensive Review Example

Example 9.13

A homeowners insurer models fire-damage losses as Exponential with mean 15,000. The policy has an ordinary deductible of 2,000 and a policy limit such that the maximum payment is 25,000. Find (a) E[Y], (b) the loss elimination ratio contributed by the deductible alone, and (c) the probability the insurer pays the maximum amount.

Show Solution

The cap on the loss (for payment purposes) is d+u = 2000+25,000 = 27,000.

(a) E[X∧27,000] = 15,000(1−e^{-27,000/15,000}) = 15,000(1−e^{-1.8}) ≈ 15,000(1−0.165299) ≈ 15,000(0.834701) ≈ 12,520.5.

E[X∧2,000] = 15,000(1−e^{-2,000/15,000}) = 15,000(1−e^{-0.13333}) ≈ 15,000(1−0.875173) ≈ 15,000(0.124827) ≈ 1872.4.

E[Y] = 12,520.5 − 1872.4 = 10,648.1.

(b) LER(2000) = E[X∧2000]/E[X] = 1872.4/15,000 ≈ 0.1248 — the deductible alone eliminates about 12.5% of expected losses from the insurer's perspective.

(c) The payment reaches its maximum exactly when X ≥ 27,000: P(X≥27,000) = e^{-27,000/15,000} = e^{-1.8} ≈ 0.1653.

As a final sanity check across all three parts: the expected payment (10,648.1) is comfortably below both the maximum possible payment (25,000) and the unlimited-deductible-only expected payment E[X]−E[X∧2000] = 15,000−1872.4 = 13,127.6 — confirming that adding the policy limit further reduced the insurer's expected payment, exactly as it should.

9.20 Chapter Summary

Key Formulas: Chapter 9
Quantity Formula
Limited expected value (general) E[X∧u] = ∫₀ᵘ [1−F(x)]dx
Limited expected value, Exponential(θ) θ(1−e−u/θ)
Limited expected value, Uniform(0,b), u≤b u − u²/(2b)
Expected payment, ordinary deductible d E[X] − E[X∧d]
Expected payment, deductible d + limit u E[X∧(d+u)] − E[X∧d]
Expected payment, add coinsurance α multiply the above by α
Variance of payment E[Y²]−(E[Y])², computed piecewise from g(X)
Exam tip: Chapters 8 and 9 together form the applied core of Part II. Nearly every sitting includes several problems requiring you to (1) correctly construct the payment function from a word description, and (2) compute a probability, percentile, mean, or variance of that payment. Practice writing the piecewise payment function first, every time, before doing any calculus or algebra — it is the step most likely to go wrong under time pressure.

Part II Key Takeaways & Practice Problems

Before working the practice set, review this checklist of the ideas from Chapters 1–9 that generate the most exam points relative to the effort required to master them.

The Non-Negotiables

  • The mean/variance table (Chapters 4–7). You must be able to recite the pmf/pdf, mean, and variance of all eleven named distributions without hesitation. Time spent memorizing this table is the highest-return use of study time in this entire manual.
  • Distribution recognition. Given only a word description (no formula), correctly identify which of the eleven distributions applies. Practice this explicitly — it is a distinct skill from applying a formula once you already know which one to use.
  • The geometric/negative binomial parameterization trap (Chapter 4). Always determine whether a problem counts trials or failures before applying a formula.
  • The memoryless property (Chapters 4 and 6). Know exactly what it does and does not imply, and use it as a computational shortcut whenever a problem conditions an exponential (or geometric) variable on exceeding some threshold.
  • Standardization (Chapter 7). Every normal-distribution question funnels through Z=(X−μ)/σ and the standard normal table — this must be fully automatic.
  • The payment function (Chapter 8). Before computing anything, write Y as an explicit piecewise function of X, incorporating deductible, coinsurance, limit, and inflation in the correct order. This single habit prevents the majority of errors on applied problems.
  • The limited expected value E[X∧u] (Chapter 9). Memorize its closed form for the exponential and the uniform, and know how to use it (via the layering identity) to get the expected payment under any combination of deductible and limit.

Common Traps, Consolidated

Cross-Chapter Trap List
Trap Where it appears Fix
Confusing the two geometric parameterizations Chapter 4 Read the support: {1,2,…} is trials; {0,1,…} is failures
Using binomial when sampling is without replacement Chapters 4–5 Check for a finite, explicitly-sized population
Confusing ordinary and franchise deductibles Chapter 8 Franchise pays the full loss once triggered; ordinary pays only the excess
Inflating the deductible/limit along with the loss Chapter 8 Only the ground-up loss X gets trended by (1+r)
Scaling SD by a² instead of |a| Chapter 3 Var(aX+b)=a²Var(X), but SD(aX+b)=|a|SD(X)
Forgetting the point mass at Y=0 (or at the cap) in a payment variable Chapter 9 Always identify all discrete "spikes" before integrating
Using strict vs. non-strict inequalities inconsistently for continuous X Chapter 1 They're identical for continuous X — the distinction only matters for discrete X
Final exam-day tip for this topic: When a Part II problem feels difficult, it is almost always because of one of two things: (1) you haven't yet identified which named distribution applies, or (2) you haven't yet written out the explicit piecewise payment function. Solve those two identification problems first, on paper, before doing any algebra — the calculations themselves are rarely the hard part.

Practice Problems

The following 32 problems span all nine chapters of Part II, in mixed order and mixed difficulty, reflecting the proportion of exam points this topic carries. Work each problem before revealing the solution.

1. A discrete random variable X has pmf p(x) = c(5−x) for x=1,2,3,4. Find c and P(X≤2).
Show Answer

Sum: c(4+3+2+1)=10c=1 ⟹ c=0.1. P(X≤2) = p(1)+p(2) = 0.4+0.3 = 0.7.

Answer: c = 0.1, P(X≤2) = 0.7. Key concept: any pmf-normalization problem reduces to summing the given expression over the stated support and setting the total to 1 — always double check the support (here, exactly the four integers 1–4) before summing.

2. A continuous random variable has CDF F(x) = 1 − 1/(1+x)² for x≥0. Find the pdf and compute P(X>3).
Show Answer

f(x) = F′(x) = 2/(1+x)³. P(X>3) = 1−F(3) = 1/(1+3)² = 1/16 = 0.0625.

Answer: f(x)=2/(1+x)³, P(X>3)=0.0625. Key concept: whenever a survival probability is requested and you already have (or can quickly get) the CDF, use 1−F(x) directly rather than integrating the pdf from scratch — differentiating to find the pdf was only needed here because the problem explicitly asked for it.

3. Let X have pdf f(x)=2x on (0,1). Find E[X], E[X²], and Var(X).
Show Answer

E[X] = ∫₀¹ x(2x)dx = ∫₀¹2x²dx = 2/3.

E[X²] = ∫₀¹x²(2x)dx = ∫₀¹2x³dx = 1/2.

Var(X) = 1/2 − (2/3)² = 1/2 − 4/9 = 9/18−8/18 = 1/18 ≈ 0.0556.

Answer: E[X]=2/3, Var(X)=1/18. Key concept: the shortcut formula Var(X)=E[X²]−(E[X])² requires only two applications of LOTUS (for g(x)=x and g(x)=x²) — there is rarely any reason to use the definitional formula E[(X−μ)²] directly.

4. The number of complaints filed against a repair shop in a week is N with P(N=n) = (1/3)(2/3)n for n=0,1,2,…, and complaint counts in different weeks are independent. Find the probability that exactly 5 complaints are filed over a two-week period.
Show Answer

This is geometric (Version 2, "failures" form) with p=1/3. The sum of two independent copies is Negative Binomial(r=2, p=1/3): P(N₁+N₂=5) = C(5+2−1,2−1)(1/3)²(2/3)⁵ = C(6,1)(1/9)(32/243) = 192/2187 = 64/729 ≈ 0.0878.

Answer: 64/729 ≈ 0.0878. Key concept: recognizing that a geometric pmf of the form (1−p)ⁿ·p is exactly the negative-binomial "failures" pmf with r=1 is what makes the sum of two such variables an immediate application of the negative binomial with r=2, without any separate convolution calculation.

5. A fair coin is flipped repeatedly until the first head. Find E[number of flips] and P(more than 5 flips needed).
Show Answer

Geometric (trials), p=0.5: E[X]=1/0.5=2. P(X>5) = (0.5)⁵ = 1/32 = 0.03125.

Answer: E[X]=2, P(X>5)=1/32. Key concept: for the trials-form geometric, P(X>n)=(1−p)ⁿ directly (the probability the first n trials all fail) — no summation of the pmf tail is needed.

6. An insurer's book contains 25 independent policies, each with probability 0.08 of a claim this year. Find the mean, variance, and standard deviation of the number of policies with a claim.
Show Answer

Binomial(25, 0.08): E[X]=25(0.08)=2, Var(X)=25(0.08)(0.92)=1.84, SD(X)=√1.84≈1.3565.

Answer: mean 2, variance 1.84, SD ≈ 1.36. Key concept: "n independent policies, each with a fixed claim probability" is the textbook verbal signature of a binomial count — no further derivation is needed once this pattern is recognized.

7. An urn has 8 red and 12 blue balls. Five balls are drawn without replacement. Find the probability that exactly 3 are red.
Show Answer

Hypergeometric(N=20,K=8,n=5): P(X=3) = C(8,3)C(12,2)/C(20,5) = 56(66)/15504 = 3696/15504 ≈ 0.2384.

Answer: ≈ 0.2384. Key concept: "drawn without replacement" from an urn with a stated, finite composition is the standard verbal cue for hypergeometric — using binomial here (with p=8/20=0.4) would give a noticeably different, incorrect answer.

8. The number of tornadoes in a given year in a region follows Poisson(3). Find Var(number of tornadoes | at least one tornado occurs).
Show Answer

Let N~Poisson(3). Condition on N≥1, with P(N≥1)=1−e⁻³.

E[N|N≥1] = E[N]/P(N≥1) = 3/(1−e⁻³) ≈ 3/0.950213 ≈ 3.1571 (since P(N=0) contributes 0 to E[N]).

E[N²|N≥1] = E[N²]/P(N≥1); unconditionally E[N²]=Var(N)+(E[N])²=3+9=12, so E[N²|N≥1] = 12/0.950213 ≈ 12.6288.

Var(N|N≥1) = 12.6288 − (3.1571)² ≈ 12.6288 − 9.9673 ≈ 2.6615.

Answer: ≈ 2.66.

9. A number is chosen uniformly at random from the integers 1 through 6 (a fair die). Find the mean and variance directly from the discrete uniform formulas, and confirm against the well-known die results.
Show Answer

a=1,b=6: E[X]=(1+6)/2=3.5. Var(X) = (6²−1)/12 = 35/12 ≈ 2.9167. These match the standard fair-die results.

Answer: mean 3.5, variance 35/12 ≈ 2.917.

10. Waiting times for a bus are uniform on [0,20] minutes. Find the probability a rider waits more than 15 minutes, and find SD of the waiting time.
Show Answer

P(X>15) = (20−15)/20 = 0.25. Var(X) = 20²/12 ≈ 33.33, SD ≈ 5.7735.

Answer: P=0.25, SD ≈ 5.77 minutes.

11. The lifetime of a battery (in years) is Exponential with mean 4. Given the battery has already lasted 2 years, find the probability it lasts at least 3 more years.
Show Answer

By memorylessness, P(X>2+3|X>2) = P(X>3) = e−3/4 ≈ 0.4724.

Answer: ≈ 0.4724. Key concept: no subtraction or shifting of the "2 years already elapsed" is needed at all — memorylessness means the conditional survival probability equals the unconditional survival probability over the additional period.

12. An electronic system requires 4 independent components to each function for the system to work, and the components' lifetimes are iid Exponential with mean 5 years. If the components operate sequentially (a spare replaces a failed one immediately, and the system needs a total of 4 component-lifetimes' worth of operating time before overhaul), find the mean and variance of the total operating time before overhaul.
Show Answer

Total time is Gamma(α=4, θ=5). E[T]=4(5)=20 years. Var(T)=4(25)=100, so SD(T)=10 years.

Answer: mean 20 years, variance 100, SD 10 years. Key concept: "sum of n iid exponentials" should immediately trigger recognition of Gamma(n, θ) — there is no need to derive the sum's distribution from scratch each time.

13. The annual number of workplace safety incidents at a manufacturing plant is Poisson with mean 4.5. Find the mode of the annual number of incidents.
Show Answer

For Poisson(λ), the pmf increases while k<λ and decreases for k>λ (using the ratio p(k)/p(k−1)=λ/k). With λ=4.5, the mode is ⌊4.5⌋=4.

Answer: mode = 4. Key concept: for any non-integer Poisson mean, the mode is simply its floor; only when λ is a whole number do two adjacent modes (λ−1 and λ) tie.

14. Exam scores are normally distributed with mean 500 and standard deviation 100. Find the probability a randomly chosen score is between 450 and 650.
Show Answer

Z₁=(450−500)/100=−0.5, Z₂=(650−500)/100=1.5. P(−0.5<Z<1.5) = Φ(1.5)−Φ(−0.5) = 0.9332 − 0.3085 = 0.6247.

Answer: ≈ 0.6247. Key concept: for any interval probability under a normal distribution, standardize both endpoints separately and subtract the resulting table values — there is no shortcut that avoids computing both z-scores.

15. A random proportion R of a reinsurance treaty's limit is used, with R ~ Beta(a=3,b=2). Find E[R] and Var(R).
Show Answer

E[R] = 3/5 = 0.6. Var(R) = (3)(2)/[(5)²(6)] = 6/150 = 0.04.

Answer: E[R]=0.6, Var(R)=0.04. Key concept: both beta formulas depend only on a, b, and their sum — plug in directly with no integration required once the parameters are identified.

16. An insurance policy has a franchise deductible of 250. Losses are uniform on [0,1000]. Find the expected payment (unconditional, including the possibility of a zero payment).
Show Answer

Franchise: Y=0 for X≤250; Y=X for X>250.

E[Y] = ∫2501000 x(1/1000)dx = (1/1000)[x²/2]2501000 = (1/1000)[(500000)−(31250)] = (1/1000)(468750) = 468.75.

Answer: E[Y] = 468.75. Key concept: contrast this with the ordinary-deductible expected payment, E[(X−250)_+]=468.75−250(1−0.25)=468.75−187.5=281.25 — the franchise version is exactly 250×P(X>250)=250(0.75)=187.5 larger, confirming the general relationship from Section 8.10.

17. A one-year homeowners policy has a deductible of 750. There is a 0.75 chance of no claim and (necessarily) a 0.25 chance of exactly one claim (never more than one). If a claim occurs, the loss (before the deductible) is Exponential with mean 4000. Find the 90th percentile of the insurer's payout on this policy.
Show Answer

Since P(\text{payout}=0) = 0.75 exactly, the 90th percentile of the (unconditional) payout falls in the region where a claim occurs. We need the value x such that P(\text{payout} ≤ x) = 0.90, i.e. the point where, conditional on a claim, the payout is at its (0.90−0.75)/(1−0.75) = 0.60 quantile.

Let m be the 60th percentile of the Exponential(4000) loss: 1−e−m/4000=0.60 ⟹ m = −4000ln(0.40) ≈ 3665.16.

Subtract the deductible: payout = 3665.16 − 750 ≈ 2915.16.

Answer: ≈ 2915. Key concept: whenever a payment variable has a point mass at zero (here, probability 0.75 of no payment), a percentile above that point-mass probability must be rescaled — dividing the "excess" target probability by the probability of a positive payment converts it into the correct conditional percentile of the continuous piece.

18. Losses are exponentially distributed with mean 6. The deductible is 2 for each loss. Calculate the median amount that the insurer pays a policyholder for a loss under the policy.
Show Answer

First find the median loss (ignoring the deductible): 1−e−m/6=0.5 ⟹ m = 6ln2 ≈ 4.1589.

Since 4.1589 > 2 (the deductible), the loss exceeds its own median if and only if the claim payment exceeds 4.1589 − 2 = 2.1589. Because the mapping X ↦ X−2 preserves order (and the deductible is below the median loss, so median payment among triggered claims lines up with median loss shifted down by 2), the median claim payment is 2.1589.

Answer: ≈ 2.16. Key concept: because the deductible-adjusted payment is a strictly increasing (shift) function of the loss above the deductible, percentiles of the payment correspond directly to percentiles of the loss shifted down by d — solve for the loss's percentile first, then subtract d.

19. Under a product liability policy, losses are uniform on [0,b], with a deductible of b/4. Find the ratio of Var(claim payment) to Var(loss).
Show Answer

Var(X)=b²/12. Y=max(X−b/4,0): Y=0 for X≤b/4 (probability 1/4), and Y=X−b/4 for X>b/4.

E[Y] = ∫b/4b(x−b/4)(1/b)dx = (1/b)·(3b/4)²/2 = 9b/32. E[Y²] = ∫b/4b(x−b/4)²(1/b)dx = (1/b)·(3b/4)³/3 = 9b²/64.

Var(Y) = 9b²/64 − (9b/32)² = 144b²/1024 − 81b²/1024 = 63b²/1024.

Ratio: Var(Y)/Var(X) = (63b²/1024)/(b²/12) = 756/1024 = 189/256.

Answer: 189:256 ≈ 0.738.

20. An insurance policy has a deductible of 6. Losses under the policy are exponentially distributed with mean 20. Find the expected claim payment net of the deductible.
Show Answer

E[(X−6)_+] = θe−6/θ = 20e−0.3 ≈ 14.816.

Answer: ≈ 14.82.

21. An automobile policy sells with 70% coinsurance above a deductible of 800. Losses (in units of 1000) are Exponential with mean 5. Find the probability that the policy payment on a randomly selected loss exceeds 3000 (i.e., exceeds 3 in the same units).
Show Answer

Payment Y = 0.7(X−0.8)_+ (deductible 800 = 0.8 in units of 1000). We need P(Y>3).

0.7(X−0.8) > 3 ⟹ X−0.8 > 4.2857 ⟹ X > 5.0857.

P(X>5.0857) = e−5.0857/5 = e−1.0171 ≈ 0.3616.

Answer: ≈ 0.362. Key concept: translate a target payment threshold back to the corresponding loss threshold by inverting the payment function algebraically first, then apply the loss distribution's survival function — do not attempt to find the distribution of the payment variable itself.

22. The number of conveyor-belt jams at a distribution center in a month is Poisson, and the probability the center has at least one jam is 0.20. Find the probability it has at least two jams.
Show Answer

e^{-λ}=1−0.20=0.80 ⟹ λ = −ln(0.80) ≈ 0.22314. P(X≥2) = 1−e−λ−λe−λ = 1−0.80−(0.22314)(0.80) ≈ 1−0.80−0.17851 ≈ 0.02149.

Answer: ≈ 0.0215. Key concept: whenever a Poisson problem gives you P(X≥1) rather than λ directly, back out λ via e^{-λ}=1−P(X≥1) before proceeding — this two-step "recover the parameter, then use it" pattern is common throughout this chapter.

23. Two random number generators select numbers between 0 and 4: generator P uses continuous Uniform[0,4]; generator Q uses discrete uniform on the integers 0 through 4. Find the absolute difference between their standard deviations.
Show Answer

SD_Q = √[(5²−1)/12] = √2 ≈ 1.4142. SD_P = √(4²/12) = √(4/3) ≈ 1.1547. Difference ≈ 0.2595.

Answer: ≈ 0.26. Key concept: the discrete uniform's variance formula uses (b−a+1) (the count of integers), not (b−a) — dropping the "+1" is a common slip that would incorrectly make the two variances equal.

24. For the exponential, normal, and continuous uniform distributions, suppose a random variable X from the family is rescaled by a positive constant c (i.e., Y=cX). Determine for which of the three distributions this rescaling multiplies both the median and the standard deviation by that same factor c.
Show Answer

For any distribution, if Y=cX with c>0, then every quantile of Y (including the median) is c times the corresponding quantile of X, since F_Y(y)=P(X≤y/c)=F_X(y/c). Likewise Var(Y)=c²Var(X), so SD(Y)=c·SD(X). Checking each family confirms this: Normal(cμ,c²σ²) has median cμ and SD cσ; Uniform(ca,cb) has median c(a+b)/2 and SD c(b−a)/√12; Exponential(cθ) has median cθln2 and SD cθ — in every case both quantities scale by exactly c.

Answer: all three. This holds regardless of the specific distribution's shape, because it follows purely from the algebra of a positive linear rescaling, not from any special property of the exponential, normal, or uniform families individually.

25. A company's annual profit (in billions) is normally distributed, with variance equal to the cube of the mean. The probability of an annual loss (profit < 0) is 5%. Find the company's expected annual profit.
Show Answer

Let μ = mean, σ² = μ³. P(X<0) = Φ((0−μ)/σ) = 0.05 ⟹ −μ/σ = −1.645 ⟹ μ/σ = 1.645.

So μ = 1.645σ = 1.645√(μ³) ⟹ μ = 1.645 μ^{1.5} ⟹ 1 = 1.645 μ^{0.5} ⟹ μ^{0.5} = 1/1.645 ≈ 0.6079 ⟹ μ ≈ 0.3696 billion.

Answer: ≈ 370 million. Key concept: whenever a normal distribution's variance is given as a function of its own mean, standardizing P(X<0) produces an equation purely in μ — solve that equation for μ directly rather than trying to find σ first.

26. A health insurer's medical reimbursement has density proportional to 100−x for 0<x<100. Find the mode of the medical reimbursements, and find the normalizing constant.
Show Answer

The mode is the value of x maximizing f(x) ∝ (100−x) on (0,100), which is decreasing throughout, so the maximum is at the left endpoint x=0.

Normalizing: ∫₀¹⁰⁰ c(100−x)dx = c[100x−x²/2]₀¹⁰⁰ = c(10000−5000)=5000c=1 ⟹ c=1/5000.

Answer: mode = 0; c = 1/5000. Key concept: a mode does not require calculus if the density is monotonic on its entire support — simply inspect whether the density is increasing or decreasing throughout, and the mode is the corresponding endpoint.

27. Losses X are uniform on [0,320]. A policy has a deductible d, and the expected value of the unreimbursed portion of a loss (i.e., E[X∧d]) is 70. Find d.
Show Answer

For Uniform(0,320), E[X∧d] = d − d²/(2·320) = d − d²/640 for 0≤d≤320.

Set d − d²/640 = 70 ⟹ 640d − d² = 44,800 ⟹ d² − 640d + 44,800 = 0.

Quadratic formula: d = [640 ± √(409,600−179,200)]/2 = [640±√230,400]/2 = [640±480]/2.

Two roots: d=560 (rejected, exceeds max loss 320) or d=80.

Answer: d = 80. Key concept: solving-backwards-for-a-deductible problems routinely produce a spurious algebraic root above the maximum possible loss (see Section 8.21) — always check both roots of the quadratic against the support of X before selecting the final answer.

28. An automobile insurance policy has a deductible of 1 and a maximum claim payment of 5 (i.e., a limit of 5 on the payment). Auto losses X follow a distribution with limited expected value function E[X∧u] = 3(1−e−u/3) for u≥0 (an Exponential(mean 3) loss). Find the expected claim payment.
Show Answer

Deductible d=1, limit u=5 on the payment, so the cap on the loss is d+u=6.

E[Y] = E[X∧6] − E[X∧1] = 3(1−e−6/3) − 3(1−e−1/3) = 3(1−e−2) − 3(1−e−1/3)

= 3[e−1/3 − e−2] ≈ 3[0.716531 − 0.135335] ≈ 3(0.581196) ≈ 1.7436.

Answer: ≈ 1.74.

29. A discrete random variable N represents the number of independent claims-adjuster site visits not resulting in a fraud finding, before the 3rd visit that does uncover fraud; each visit independently uncovers fraud with probability 0.25. Find E[N] and Var(N), and find P(N=2).
Show Answer

This is Negative Binomial with r=3, p=0.25 (failures form). E[N] = r(1−p)/p = 3(0.75)/0.25 = 9. Var(N) = r(1−p)/p² = 3(0.75)/0.0625 = 36.

P(N=2) = C(2+3−1, 3−1)(0.75)²(0.25)³ = C(4,2)(0.5625)(0.015625) = 6(0.5625)(0.015625) ≈ 0.05273.

Answer: E[N]=9, Var(N)=36, P(N=2)≈0.0527. Key concept: with r=3 successes needed, both the mean and variance are exactly 3 times the corresponding single-geometric (r=1) values — a quick cross-check on any negative binomial computation.

30. A workers' compensation claim severity X (in thousands of dollars) has pdf f(x) = 0.5e−0.5x for x>0 (Exponential, mean 2). The policy has an ordinary deductible of 1 (thousand) and coinsurance of 90% above the deductible, with no upper limit. Find E[Y] and Var(Y) for the insurer's payment Y, in thousands.
Show Answer

Let W = (X−1)_+. By the exponential memoryless shortcut, E[W] = θe^{-d/θ} = 2e^{-0.5} ≈ 2(0.606531) ≈ 1.21306.

For E[W²]: given X>1 (probability e^{-0.5}), memorylessness says X−1 is again Exponential(2), so E[(X−1)²|X>1] = Var+mean² = 4+4=8. Thus E[W²] = e^{-0.5}(8) ≈ 0.606531(8) ≈ 4.85225.

Var(W) = 4.85225 − (1.21306)² ≈ 4.85225 − 1.47151 ≈ 3.38074.

Now apply coinsurance: Y=0.9W. E[Y] = 0.9(1.21306) ≈ 1.0918 (about $1,091.80). Var(Y) = 0.81(3.38074) ≈ 2.7384 (in squared thousands), so SD(Y) ≈ 1.6548 thousand ≈ $1,654.80.

Answer: E[Y] ≈ 1.092 thousand, Var(Y) ≈ 2.738, SD(Y) ≈ 1.655 thousand. Key concept: this problem combines the memoryless shortcut for the exponential's excess-of-deductible moments (Section 9.3) with the coinsurance scaling rule from Chapter 3 — compute the moments of the uncoinsured excess W first, then scale by α² for the variance and α for the mean at the very last step.

31. A commercial property policy pays a fraction c of the amount by which a loss exceeds a threshold t; a claim is filed if and only if the loss exceeds the threshold. If the standard deviation of a filed loss (i.e., SD(X | X>t)) is s, express the standard deviation of a filed payment in terms of c and s.
Show Answer

A "filed loss" is, by definition, already conditioned on X>t. Given that a loss is filed, the payment is c(X−t) — a linear transformation (subtract the constant t, then multiply by the constant c) of the filed loss X. By the Chapter 3 rule SD(aX+b)=|a|·SD(X) (subtracting the constant t doesn't change spread; multiplying by c>0 scales SD by c):

Answer: SD(filed payment) = cs. This problem is a good conceptual check that once you condition away the "will a claim be filed at all" question, the deductible-and-coinsurance payment transformation on the remaining (conditional) loss is purely linear — all of the piecewise/point-mass complexity from Chapter 9 lived entirely in the unconditional (not-yet-conditioned-on-exceeding-t) distribution.

32. A tornado-loss model uses independent territories, each with annual tornado losses Exponential with mean 100 (thousand dollars). A regional pool aggregates 4 independent, identically-distributed territories' losses in a given year, T = X₁+X₂+X₃+X₄. (a) Identify the distribution of T and give its mean and variance. (b) A stop-loss reinsurance treaty on the pool pays the excess of T over 500 (thousand). Set up the calculation for the reinsurer's expected payment.
Show Answer

(a) Since the four territory losses are iid Exponential(θ=100), their sum is T ~ Gamma(α=4, θ=100). E[T] = 4(100)=400. Var(T) = 4(100²)=40,000, so SD(T)=200.

(b) The reinsurer's expected payment is E[(T−500)_+] = E[T] − E[T∧500], where E[T∧500] = ∫_0^{500}[1−F_T(t)]dt and F_T is the Gamma(4,100) (Erlang) CDF, F_T(t) = 1 − e^{-t/100}[1+(t/100)+(t/100)²/2+(t/100)³/6] — the standard closed form for an integer-shape gamma CDF, derivable by repeated integration by parts or by relating it to a sum of Poisson probabilities. Evaluating the integral numerically gives E[T∧500], and subtracting from 400 gives the reinsurer's expected payment.

Answer: T ~ Gamma(4,100), mean 400, variance 40,000; expected reinsurer payment = 400 − E[T∧500], with E[T∧500] computed from the Erlang CDF above.

Notation Reference for Part II

Symbols Used Throughout Part II
Symbol Meaning
p(x) Probability mass function of a discrete random variable
f(x) Probability density function of a continuous random variable
F(x) Cumulative distribution function, P(X≤x)
S(x) Survival function, 1−F(x) = P(X>x)
E[X], μ Expected value (mean) of X
Var(X), σ² Variance of X
SD(X), σ Standard deviation of X
CV(X) Coefficient of variation, σ/μ
X∧u, min(X,u) X capped at u
(X−d)+, max(X−d,0) Excess of X over d (zero if X≤d)
E[X∧u] Limited expected value of X at u
d Ordinary or franchise deductible
α Coinsurance rate (fraction of loss paid by insurer)
u Policy limit (maximum payment above the deductible)
r Inflation / trend rate
X, Y Ground-up loss (X) and insurer payment (Y), respectively

This completes Part II. The distribution mean/variance table (Sections 5.16 and 7.8) and the payment-structure table (Section 8.24) are the two single most valuable quick-reference tables in this part — revisit them frequently as you work through the practice problems above and any additional problem sets.

Part III — Multivariate Random Variables (23–30%)

Every topic so far has treated one random variable at a time. In practice, actuaries almost never work with a single quantity in isolation — a policy might generate both an auto claim and a homeowners claim in the same year; a portfolio's aggregate loss depends on both a random claim count and random claim severities; a reinsurance treaty responds to the largest of several ceded losses. Part III builds the machinery for handling two or more random variables simultaneously, and at 23–30% of the exam it is the single heaviest-weighted part of the syllabus. The seven chapters below move through the material in the order you'll need it: joint probability functions and joint CDFs (Chapter 1), marginal and conditional distributions obtained from a joint distribution (Chapter 2), moments — expectations, conditional expectations, and conditional variances — computed from joint distributions (Chapter 3), covariance and correlation as measures of association between two random variables (Chapter 4), the distribution theory of order statistics for independent samples (Chapter 5), the mean, variance, and distribution of linear combinations of independent random variables, including the special "reproductive" families and normal combinations (Chapter 6), and the Central Limit Theorem, which lets you approximate probabilities for sums and averages of almost any collection of independent, identically distributed random variables (Chapter 7). Per the syllabus, the discrete case is developed in full depth throughout; continuous joint distributions are mentioned only briefly, as a conceptual extension, since they are not separately tested apart from the special case of linear combinations of independent normal random variables.

A word on how to use this part. Chapters 1–4 form a tightly connected sequence built around a single running example — a small joint pmf table for a policyholder's auto and homeowners claim counts — so that you see the same numbers reused as a joint pmf, then split into marginals and conditionals, then summarized by moments, and finally summarized again by covariance and correlation. Working through that example once, carefully, is worth more than skimming it four times. Chapters 5–7 shift focus from "two variables observed together" to "many independent copies of essentially the same variable" — order statistics ask about the extremes and middle values of an independent sample, while linear combinations and the Central Limit Theorem ask about sums and averages. Because this part carries close to a third of the exam's weight, expect a mix of question styles: some problems hand you a joint pmf table directly and ask for a probability, marginal, or moment; others describe a two-stage random mechanism in words (a random number of events, each with a random size) and expect you to recognize the law of total expectation or variance; and a large fraction — especially in the final third of the exam — are Central Limit Theorem applications dressed up in insurance language. Recognizing which tool a word problem is asking for is at least as important as executing the algebra correctly once you've identified it.

For reference, here is how the official learning outcomes for this topic map onto the chapters ahead: outcome (a), joint probability and joint cumulative distribution functions, is Chapter 1; outcome (b), marginal and conditional probability functions, is Chapter 2; outcomes (c) and (d), moments — including variance and standard deviation — of joint, marginal, and conditional distributions, are Chapter 3; outcome (e), covariance and correlation, is Chapter 4; outcome (f), the joint distribution of order statistics, is Chapter 5; outcomes (g) and (h), probabilities and moments for linear combinations of independent random variables (including the special case of continuous normal combinations), are Chapter 6; and outcome (i), Central Limit Theorem applications, is Chapter 7. Every chapter opens with a "Learning Outcomes Covered" box quoting the relevant outcome(s) verbatim, so you can always tell exactly which piece of the syllabus a given chapter is targeting.

1. Joint Probability & Joint CDFs (Discrete)

Learning Outcomes Covered: The Candidate will be able to determine joint probability functions and joint cumulative distribution functions for discrete random variables.

The joint probability mass function

When two discrete random variables X and Y are defined on the same experiment, their joint probability mass function (joint pmf) assigns a probability to every pair of possible values:

JOINT PMFf(x,y) = P(X = x, Y = y)

The comma inside the probability statement means "and" — f(x,y) is the probability that X takes the value x at the same time that Y takes the value y. Just like an ordinary pmf, a joint pmf must satisfy two properties, and every joint-distribution problem on the exam implicitly relies on both of them.

PROPERTIES OF A JOINT PMFf(x,y) ≥ 0 for every (x,y), and ΣΣ f(x,y) = 1 (sum taken over the entire joint support)

The joint support is the set of pairs (x,y) for which f(x,y) > 0. For two discrete variables with a modest number of possible values, the most natural way to display a joint pmf is a two-way table, with rows indexed by the values of X and columns indexed by the values of Y (or vice versa).

It is tempting to assume the support is always a full rectangular grid — every value of X paired with every value of Y — but this is frequently not the case. A common exam construction restricts the support to a triangular region, such as x + y ≤ n for some fixed n, which arises naturally whenever X and Y represent, say, two counts that cannot jointly exceed some total capacity (two types of claims out of at most n total claims, or the number of successes of two competing types out of a fixed number of trials). When the support is triangular rather than rectangular, every sum — for the normalizing constant, for a marginal, for a rectangular-region probability — must be taken only over the pairs that are actually in the support; including "phantom" pairs outside the support is a frequent source of error.

Reading the table. Every cell of a joint pmf table is a single number: the probability of that exact (x,y) combination. To get any other probability — a marginal probability, a conditional probability, the probability of a region — you always start by adding up some subset of the cells in the table. There is no shortcut that skips this step.

A running example

Suppose a randomly selected auto-and-home policyholder is described by X = the number of auto claims filed in a year (0 or 1) and Y = the number of homeowners claims filed in the same year (0, 1, or 2). The joint pmf is given by the following table, which we will return to throughout Chapters 1–4.

Joint pmf f(x,y) — auto claims (X) and homeowners claims (Y)
X \ Y 0 1 2
0 0.10 0.15 0.05
1 0.20 0.30 0.20

Before doing anything else with a joint pmf table, always verify the two defining properties: every entry is non-negative (true here), and the six entries sum to 1: 0.10+0.15+0.05+0.20+0.30+0.20 = 1.00. ✓

Example 1.1

Using the table above, find (a) P(X=1, Y=2), and (b) P(X=0, Y≠1).

Show Solution

(a) Read the cell directly from the table: P(X=1,Y=2) = f(1,2) = 0.20.

(b) "Y ≠ 1" with X = 0 means (X,Y) = (0,0) or (0,2). Add the two cells: P(X=0,Y≠1) = f(0,0)+f(0,2) = 0.10+0.05 = 0.15.

The joint cumulative distribution function

Just as a univariate CDF accumulates probability up to a point, the joint CDF accumulates probability over the entire lower-left rectangular region of the plane:

JOINT CDFF(x,y) = P(X ≤ x, Y ≤ y) = Σ Σ f(s,t) (sum over all s ≤ x and t ≤ y in the support)

For discrete random variables, F(x,y) is a step function of both arguments — it jumps at every point of positive joint probability and is flat everywhere else. It is non-decreasing in each argument separately, satisfies F(x,y) → 0 as x → −∞ or y → −∞, and F(x,y) → 1 as both x,y → ∞.

Example 1.2

Using the auto/home claims table, compute F(0,1) and F(1,1).

Show Solution

F(0,1) = P(X≤0, Y≤1) sums every cell with x=0 and y ∈ {0,1}: f(0,0)+f(0,1) = 0.10+0.15 = 0.25.

F(1,1) = P(X≤1, Y≤1) sums every cell with x ∈ {0,1} and y ∈ {0,1}: f(0,0)+f(0,1)+f(1,0)+f(1,1) = 0.10+0.15+0.20+0.30 = 0.75.

Probabilities of rectangular regions

Once you have a joint pmf table, the probability of any rectangular event — of the form a ≤ X ≤ b, c ≤ Y ≤ d — is found by summing every table cell that falls inside the rectangle. This is almost always faster and safer than trying to force the inclusion–exclusion CDF identity

RECTANGLE PROBABILITY VIA THE CDFP(a < X ≤ b, c < Y ≤ d) = F(b,d) − F(a,d) − F(b,c) + F(a,c)

which is more commonly useful when you are handed a continuous joint CDF formula directly. For a discrete pmf table, just circle the relevant cells and add.

Example 1.3

Using the auto/home claims table, find P(0 ≤ X ≤ 1, 1 ≤ Y ≤ 2).

Show Solution

The region 0 ≤ X ≤ 1 covers both rows; 1 ≤ Y ≤ 2 covers the last two columns. Sum those four cells:

f(0,1)+f(0,2)+f(1,1)+f(1,2) = 0.15+0.05+0.30+0.20 = 0.70.

You can sanity-check this: it should equal 1 − P(Y=0) = 1 − (0.10+0.20) = 1 − 0.30 = 0.70. ✓

Joint pmfs given by a formula

Exam problems frequently specify a joint pmf as a formula in x and y with an unknown normalizing constant, rather than as an explicit table. The technique is always the same: sum the formula over the entire joint support and set the total equal to 1.

Example 1.4

Suppose f(x,y) = c(x+y) for x = 1,2 and y = 1,2,3. Find c, and then find F(1,2).

Show Solution

List x+y for all six pairs in the support: (1,1)→2, (1,2)→3, (1,3)→4, (2,1)→3, (2,2)→4, (2,3)→5. The sum is 2+3+4+3+4+5 = 21, so 21c = 1 and c = 1/21.

For the CDF, F(1,2) = P(X≤1,Y≤2) restricts to x=1 (the only value of X at or below 1) and y=1,2: f(1,1)+f(1,2) = (2+3)/21 = 5/21 ≈ 0.238.

Example 1.5 — a triangular (non-rectangular) support

Suppose f(x,y) = c (a constant) for all non-negative integers x,y satisfying x+y ≤ 3. Find c, and then find P(X+Y ≤ 1).

Show Solution

The support is triangular, not rectangular: for x=0, y can be 0,1,2, or 3 (4 points); for x=1, y can be 0,1, or 2 (3 points, since y cannot reach 3 without violating x+y≤3); for x=2, y can be 0 or 1 (2 points); for x=3, only y=0 works (1 point). In total there are 4+3+2+1=10 lattice points in the support, each assigned the same probability c, so 10c=1 and c=1/10.

P(X+Y≤1) counts only the points with x+y≤1: (0,0), (0,1), (1,0) — three points, each with probability 1/10, so P(X+Y≤1) = 3/10 = 0.30.

Notice how different this is from naively multiplying "4 values of X times 4 values of Y equals 16 points" — the triangular constraint cuts the support down to 10 points, and forgetting that constraint would give the wrong normalizing constant.

Recovering a joint pmf from a joint CDF

Occasionally a problem runs the machinery of this chapter in reverse: you're given values of the joint CDF and asked to recover the underlying joint pmf. The tool is exactly the rectangle-probability identity introduced above, applied at single points.

Example 1.6 — working backwards from a joint CDF

Discrete random variables X and Y each take the values 1 and 2. Their joint CDF is known at the four relevant points: F(1,1)=0.20, F(1,2)=0.50, F(2,1)=0.45, F(2,2)=1.00. Recover the joint pmf.

Show Solution

Since X and Y have no support below 1, f(1,1)=F(1,1)=0.20 directly (the CDF at the smallest point in the support equals the pmf there).

f(1,2) = F(1,2)−F(1,1) = 0.50−0.20 = 0.30 (moving up one step in y while holding x=1 fixed just adds the new cell's probability).

f(2,1) = F(2,1)−F(1,1) = 0.45−0.20 = 0.25 (the same idea, moving one step in x).

For the last cell, apply the rectangle-probability identity from earlier in this chapter: f(2,2) = F(2,2)−F(1,2)−F(2,1)+F(1,1) = 1.00−0.50−0.45+0.20 = 0.25.

Check: 0.20+0.30+0.25+0.25 = 1.00. ✓

Joint pmfs with more than two categories: the multinomial distribution

Everything above extends naturally when a single trial can fall into more than two categories. Suppose each of n independent trials lands in exactly one of k categories, with category i having probability pi on each trial (p1+⋯+pk=1). Let Xi count how many of the n trials land in category i. The joint pmf of (X1,…,Xk) is the multinomial distribution:

MULTINOMIAL JOINT PMFP(X1=x1,…,Xk=xk) = [n!/(x1!⋯xk!)]·p1x1⋯pkxk, where x1+⋯+xk=n

This is the natural multivariate generalization of the (two-category) Binomial pmf, and it is exactly the joint pmf of several counts that are forced to add up to a fixed total n — precisely the kind of triangular/constrained support discussed above, since specifying any k−1 of the counts automatically determines the last one.

Example 1.7 — a trinomial joint pmf

A call center classifies each incoming call as a sales inquiry (probability 0.5), a support request (probability 0.3), or a billing question (probability 0.2), independently from call to call. Out of 5 calls, let X = number of sales inquiries and Y = number of support requests (so 5−X−Y is the number of billing questions). Find P(X=3,Y=1).

Show Solution

Here n=5, and the implied third count is 5−3−1=1 billing question. Applying the multinomial formula with x1=3 (sales), x2=1 (support), x3=1 (billing):

P(X=3,Y=1) = [5!/(3!1!1!)]·(0.5)3(0.3)1(0.2)1 = 20·(0.125)(0.3)(0.2) = 20(0.0075) = 0.15.

Building a joint pmf from a two-stage experiment

Not every joint pmf is handed to you as a table or a formula — often you must construct it yourself from a description of a two-stage random experiment, using the multiplication rule for conditional probability one stage at a time.

Example 1.8 — sampling without replacement

An urn contains 3 red balls and 2 blue balls. Two balls are drawn without replacement. Let X=1 if the first ball drawn is red (0 otherwise), and let Y=1 if the second ball drawn is red (0 otherwise). Construct the joint pmf of X and Y.

Show Solution

Build each cell with the multiplication rule, P(X=x,Y=y) = P(X=x)·P(Y=y|X=x), using the fact that after the first draw, only 4 balls remain:

f(1,1) = P(1st red)·P(2nd red | 1st red) = (3/5)(2/4) = 6/20 = 0.30

f(1,0) = (3/5)(2/4) = 6/20 = 0.30 (first red, second blue: 2 blue remain out of 4)

f(0,1) = (2/5)(3/4) = 6/20 = 0.30 (first blue, second red: 3 red remain out of 4)

f(0,0) = (2/5)(1/4) = 2/20 = 0.10 (first blue, second blue: only 1 blue remains out of 4)

These four cells sum to 0.30+0.30+0.30+0.10 = 1.00. ✓ Notice the joint pmf is not symmetric in a product sense — fX(1)=fY(1)=0.6 here (each individual draw is marginally just as likely to be red as the overall urn proportion, a classical and slightly surprising fact about sampling without replacement), yet fX(1)fY(1) = 0.36 ≠ f(1,1)=0.30, confirming X and Y are dependent, exactly as you'd expect: removing a red ball on the first draw genuinely changes the odds for the second draw.

Working with a joint pmf given by a two-variable formula in general. Whenever a joint pmf formula involves both x and y in the same expression — a sum c(x+y), a product cxy, a power like c·rx+y — always determine the support first (the explicit list, or range, of valid (x,y) pairs) before doing any algebra with the formula itself. The single most efficient way to avoid errors on this type of problem is to physically write out (or sketch, for a larger support) every pair in the support before summing, rather than trying to reason about the sum abstractly.
Common mistake. When solving for a normalizing constant, students sometimes sum over only one row or one column of the support by accident, especially when the ranges of x and y are stated in a single dense sentence. Before summing, write out (or at least count) every pair in the support explicitly. A missed row or column silently changes every downstream answer.
Exam shortcut. If a problem only asks for a probability that is a ratio of two quantities built from the same joint pmf formula (for example, a conditional probability — covered next chapter), the normalizing constant c often cancels out and you don't need to solve for it at all. Still, it's safest to find c first unless you're confident it will cancel.
Chapter 1 takeaways. A joint pmf must be non-negative and sum to 1 over its entire support — always check what the support actually is before summing anything, since it need not be a full rectangular grid. The joint CDF F(x,y)=P(X≤x,Y≤y) accumulates probability over a lower-left region and is a step function for discrete variables. For a joint pmf given as a table, the fastest and safest way to find any probability — including the probability of a rectangular region — is to identify and sum the relevant cells directly, rather than forcing an inclusion–exclusion CDF formula meant for continuous settings.

2. Marginal & Conditional Distributions (Discrete)

Learning Outcomes Covered: The Candidate will be able to determine conditional and marginal probability functions for discrete random variables.

Marginal probability functions

If you only care about the behavior of X by itself — ignoring Y entirely — you recover its ordinary (univariate) pmf, called the marginal pmf of X, by summing the joint pmf over all possible values of Y. Symmetrically for Y.

MARGINAL PMFSfX(x) = Σy f(x,y) fY(y) = Σx f(x,y)

In a joint pmf table, the marginal of X is literally the sum across each row, and the marginal of Y is the sum down each column — this is exactly why the term "marginal" is used: the row and column totals are traditionally written in the margins of the table.

Example 2.1

Find the marginal pmfs of X and Y for the auto/home claims table of Chapter 1.

Show Solution

Row sums give the marginal of X: fX(0) = 0.10+0.15+0.05 = 0.30, fX(1) = 0.20+0.30+0.20 = 0.70. These sum to 1. ✓

Column sums give the marginal of Y: fY(0) = 0.10+0.20 = 0.30, fY(1) = 0.15+0.30 = 0.45, fY(2) = 0.05+0.20 = 0.25. These also sum to 1. ✓

Example 2.2

For the formula-based joint pmf of Example 1.4, f(x,y) = (x+y)/21 for x=1,2, y=1,2,3, find the marginal pmf of X.

Show Solution

Sum over y = 1,2,3 for fixed x: fX(x) = Σy=13 (x+y)/21 = [3x + (1+2+3)]/21 = (3x+6)/21.

So fX(1) = 9/21 = 3/7 and fX(2) = 12/21 = 4/7. Check: 3/7+4/7 = 1. ✓

Example 2.3

Recall the triangular-support pmf of Chapter 1, Example 1.5: f(x,y) = 1/10 for non-negative integers with x+y ≤ 3. Find the marginal pmf of X.

Show Solution

For each value of x, count how many values of y remain in the support (namely, y = 0,1,…,3−x — that's 4−x values), and multiply by the constant probability 1/10:

fX(0) = 4/10, fX(1) = 3/10, fX(2) = 2/10, fX(3) = 1/10. These sum to 10/10=1. ✓

Notice that even though the joint pmf is constant over the support, the marginal pmf of X is not constant — it decreases as x increases, simply because larger values of x leave fewer companion values of y available in a triangular support. This is a useful sanity check: a uniform joint distribution over a non-rectangular region essentially never has uniform marginals.

A shortcut for multinomial marginals. If (X1,…,Xk) follows the multinomial pmf of Example 1.7, you never need to sum out the other variables by brute force to find a single marginal: each individual Xi, on its own, is simply Binomial(n, pi) — "category i versus everything else." In the call-center example, the marginal of X (sales inquiries out of 5 calls) is Binomial(5, 0.5) without any further computation, since from the point of view of X alone, every call is either "sales" (probability 0.5) or "not sales" (probability 0.5).

Conditional probability functions

The conditional pmf of X given Y=y answers: "if I already know Y came out to be y, how is X now distributed?" It follows immediately from the definition of conditional probability:

CONDITIONAL PMFf(x|y) = f(x,y) / fY(y), provided fY(y) > 0

For each fixed y, f(x|y) viewed as a function of x is itself a legitimate pmf: it is non-negative, and summing it over all x gives exactly 1 (because the numerator, summed over x, gives fY(y), which cancels the denominator).

This is the same idea as Bayes' Theorem from the probability rules covered earlier in the syllabus, just written in pmf notation instead of event notation: reversing a conditional pmf, f(y|x) in terms of f(x|y), follows immediately by writing both as f(x,y)/fX(x) and f(x,y)/fY(y) respectively, and solving for one in terms of the other: f(y|x) = f(x|y)fY(y)/fX(x). Whenever a problem gives you conditional distributions "in one direction" (say, the distribution of claim severity given a risk class) and asks you to reverse them (the distribution of risk class given an observed severity), this is exactly the calculation being requested — a joint-pmf-flavored restatement of Bayes' Theorem.

Example 2.4

Find the conditional pmf of X given Y=2, and given Y=0, using the auto/home claims table.

Show Solution

Given Y=2: fY(2) = 0.25, so f(0|2) = 0.05/0.25 = 0.20 and f(1|2) = 0.20/0.25 = 0.80. These sum to 1. ✓

Given Y=0: fY(0) = 0.30, so f(0|0) = 0.10/0.30 = 1/3 and f(1|0) = 0.20/0.30 = 2/3.

Notice that the conditional distribution of X changes depending on the given value of Y (0.20/0.80 versus 1/3, 2/3) — a signal that X and Y are not independent, which we check formally next.

Independence of discrete random variables

INDEPENDENCEX and Y are independent ⟺ f(x,y) = fX(x)·fY(y) for every (x,y) in the joint support

This is an "for every pair" condition — a single pair where the equality fails is enough to prove dependence. Equivalently, X and Y are independent exactly when the conditional pmf f(x|y) does not depend on which value of y was given — i.e., every row (or column) of the table is a constant multiple of every other row (column).

Example 2.5

Are X and Y independent in the auto/home claims table?

Show Solution

Check one cell against the product of its marginals: fX(0)·fY(0) = 0.30 × 0.30 = 0.09, but the table gives f(0,0) = 0.10 ≠ 0.09. The equality fails for this single pair, so X and Y are not independent — no further checking is needed once one pair fails.

Example 2.6

Two components operate independently. Let X be an indicator (1 = failed, 0 = working) for component A, with P(X=1)=0.3, and let Y be the same type of indicator for component B, with P(Y=1)=0.6. Write down the joint pmf and verify the independence condition holds for all four cells.

Show Solution

Because the components operate independently, the joint pmf is simply the product of the marginals: f(x,y) = fX(x)fY(y).

X \ Y 0 1
0 0.7×0.4=0.28 0.7×0.6=0.42
1 0.3×0.4=0.12 0.3×0.6=0.18

Each cell equals the product of its row and column marginal by construction, so the independence identity holds for every pair — as it must, since we built the table that way.

Example 2.7

Recall the formula-based joint pmf f(x,y)=(x+y)/21 for x=1,2, y=1,2,3 from Example 1.4. Are X and Y independent?

Show Solution

From Example 2.2, fX(1)=3/7. A similar calculation (summing over x=1,2 for fixed y) gives fY(y) = (3+2y)/21, so fY(1)=5/21.

Check the pair (1,1): fX(1)fY(1) = (3/7)(5/21) = 15/147 = 5/49 ≈ 0.1020, but the joint pmf gives f(1,1)=(1+1)/21=2/21≈0.0952. These are not equal, so X and Y are not independent. This is typical of "additive" joint pmf formulas like c(x+y): because the formula mixes x and y together in a sum rather than keeping them in separate multiplicative factors, the joint pmf essentially never factors into a product of marginals. By contrast, a joint pmf formula that is already written as a product, such as c·g(x)·h(y) over a rectangular support, automatically describes independent random variables.

Example 2.8 — conditional probabilities from the urn example

Recall Example 1.8 (drawing two balls without replacement from an urn with 3 red, 2 blue): find the marginal pmf of Y and the conditional pmf of Y given each value of X.

Show Solution

Column sums give the marginal of Y: fY(0) = f(0,0)+f(1,0) = 0.10+0.30 = 0.40, fY(1) = f(0,1)+f(1,1) = 0.30+0.30 = 0.60. Interestingly, this exactly matches the marginal of X — each individual draw, considered on its own before any information is revealed, is red with probability 3/5, regardless of whether it's the first or second draw.

But the conditional distributions differ sharply depending on what the first draw revealed: f(Y=1|X=1) = f(1,1)/fX(1) = 0.30/0.60 = 0.50, while f(Y=1|X=0) = f(0,1)/fX(0) = 0.30/0.40 = 0.75.

This is exactly what intuition predicts: if the first ball drawn was blue (X=0), the second draw is happening from an urn that still has all 3 red balls but only 1 blue ball left, making red more likely (0.75) than if a red ball had already been removed (0.50). The marginal distributions of X and Y can be identical even while the variables are strongly dependent — marginal behavior and conditional behavior answer two different questions.

Example 2.9 — a Poisson conditioned on its sum is Binomial

Let X~Poisson(2) and Y~Poisson(3) be independent. Find P(X=2 | X+Y=4), and identify the general pattern this fits.

Show Solution

Use the definition of conditional probability directly: P(X=2|X+Y=4) = P(X=2,Y=2)/P(X+Y=4) (since X=2 and X+Y=4 together force Y=2).

By independence, P(X=2,Y=2) = P(X=2)P(Y=2) = [e−22²/2!][e−33²/2!] = (0.27067)(0.22404) = 0.06063.

By the Poisson reproductive property (Chapter 6), X+Y~Poisson(5), so P(X+Y=4) = e−554/4! = 0.17547.

P(X=2|X+Y=4) = 0.06063/0.17547 = 0.3456.

This matches — and is a special case of — a general and testable fact: if X~Poisson(λ1) and Y~Poisson(λ2) are independent, then conditional on X+Y=n, X follows a Binomial(n, λ1/(λ1+λ2)) distribution. Here that would be Binomial(4, 2/5): P(X=2) = C(4,2)(0.4)²(0.6)² = 6(0.16)(0.36) = 0.3456, matching the direct calculation exactly (up to rounding). Intuitively, once the total number of events n=X+Y is fixed, each of those n events independently "belongs" to the first Poisson stream with probability proportional to its rate, λ1/(λ1+λ2) — exactly the Binomial thinning mechanism.

Spotting independence from a formula. If a joint pmf is given as a formula, a fast diagnostic (before doing any arithmetic) is to check whether the formula can be factored into a function of x alone times a function of y alone, and whether the support is rectangular (the set of allowed x values doesn't depend on y, and vice versa). Both conditions are required for independence. A formula like c·x·y on a rectangular support is a good independence candidate; a formula like c(x+y), or any formula on a triangular or otherwise x-and-y-entangled support, cannot describe independent variables.

A brief remark on the continuous case

The syllabus tests continuous multivariate distributions only indirectly (through linear combinations of independent normal variables in Chapter 6), but it's worth knowing that every idea in this chapter carries over, with sums replaced by integrals. If X and Y are jointly continuous with joint density f(x,y) ≥ 0 satisfying ∬ f(x,y) dx dy = 1, then the marginal density of X is fX(x) = ∫ f(x,y) dy (integrating out y), and the conditional density is f(x|y) = f(x,y)/fY(y) — exactly the discrete formulas with integrals in place of sums. You will not be asked to compute a continuous joint density or a double integral of one directly on this exam; the concept is included here only so the vocabulary is familiar if it appears in a remark or a derivation.

Common trap. Do not confuse "the marginal distributions look similar" or "the table looks symmetric" with independence. The only valid test is the multiplicative identity f(x,y) = fX(x)fY(y), checked pair by pair. A table can have identical row totals and identical column totals and still fail to factor.
Fast independence check. Instead of checking every cell against the product of marginals, compute just two conditional distributions (say, f(x|y) for two different values of y). If they differ, you're done — dependent. If a quick check of a couple of rows/columns all agree, you likely have independence, but for a proof you technically need every pair to match.
Chapter 2 takeaways. Marginal pmfs come from summing (or integrating, in the continuous case) the joint pmf over the other variable; conditional pmfs come from dividing a joint pmf entry by the corresponding marginal. Independence is a strong, all-pairs condition — f(x,y)=fX(x)fY(y) must hold everywhere in the support, and a single failing pair is enough to rule it out. Marginal distributions alone never establish independence or dependence; you must look at the joint pmf (or the conditional pmfs) directly.

3. Moments of Joint, Marginal & Conditional Distributions

Learning Outcomes Covered: The Candidate will be able to calculate moments for joint, conditional, and marginal discrete distributions, and calculate variance and standard deviation for conditional and marginal probability distributions for discrete random variables.

Expectation of a function of two random variables

The single most useful formula in this chapter lets you compute the expected value of any function of X and Y directly from the joint pmf, without first finding the distribution of that function:

EXPECTATION OF g(X,Y)E[g(X,Y)] = Σ Σ g(x,y) f(x,y) (sum over the entire joint support)

Two special cases you'll use constantly: g(x,y)=x gives E[X]; g(x,y)=xy gives E[XY], a quantity central to Chapter 4's covariance formula.

Example 3.1

Using the auto/home claims table, compute E[XY] and E[X+Y].

Show Solution

E[XY] = Σ Σ xy·f(x,y). Every term with x=0 vanishes, so only the x=1 row contributes: 1(0)(0.20)+1(1)(0.30)+1(2)(0.20) = 0+0.30+0.40 = 0.70.

For E[X+Y], use linearity of expectation — it holds regardless of dependence: E[X+Y]=E[X]+E[Y]. From the marginals of Example 2.1, E[X]=0(0.30)+1(0.70)=0.70 and E[Y]=0(0.30)+1(0.45)+2(0.25)=0.95, so E[X+Y]=0.70+0.95=1.65.

Linearity always works. E[aX+bY] = aE[X]+bE[Y] is true whether or not X and Y are independent or correlated. Only variance calculations require you to track dependence (Chapter 4). Many students waste time trying to find the full distribution of X+Y just to get its mean — don't; linearity gets you there in one line.

Marginal moments

The mean and variance of X alone can be computed either directly from the joint pmf (summing x·f(x,y) over everything) or, more efficiently, from the marginal pmf fX(x) found in Chapter 2 — the two methods always agree.

MARGINAL MEAN & VARIANCEE[X] = Σx x·fX(x) Var(X) = E[X²] − (E[X])²

Conditional expectation and conditional variance

Given the conditional pmf f(x|y) from Chapter 2, the conditional mean and conditional variance of X, holding Y=y fixed, are computed exactly like an ordinary mean and variance — just using the conditional pmf in place of the marginal one.

CONDITIONAL MEAN & VARIANCEE[X|Y=y] = Σx x·f(x|y) Var(X|Y=y) = E[X²|Y=y] − (E[X|Y=y])²

Note that E[X|Y=y] and Var(X|Y=y) are ordinary numbers for each fixed y — but as y varies, they trace out functions of y. When we plug in the (random) Y itself, we get the random variables E[X|Y] and Var(X|Y), which is the key idea behind the two "total" laws below.

It helps to think of E[X|Y=y] as your best forecast of X once you've been told Y=y, and Var(X|Y=y) as how much uncertainty remains around that forecast. In an insurance setting, Y is often an observable risk classification (age band, territory, prior claim history) and X is an unobserved future outcome (claim count, claim severity) — the conditional mean and variance quantify exactly what the classification does and does not tell you about the future outcome.

Example 3.2

For the auto/home claims table, find E[X|Y=y] and Var(X|Y=y) for each value of y.

Show Solution

From Example 2.4 (and the analogous computation at y=1, which by symmetry of the first two columns gives the same conditional pmf as y=0): f(0|0)=f(0|1)=1/3, f(1|0)=f(1|1)=2/3, and f(0|2)=1/5, f(1|2)=4/5.
