E[X|Y=0] = E[X|Y=1] = 0(1/3)+1(2/3) = 2/3. Since X only takes values 0 and 1, X²=X, so E[X²|Y=0]=2/3 as well, giving Var(X|Y=0) = 2/3 − (2/3)² = 2/3 − 4/9 = 2/9 ≈ 0.222. Likewise Var(X|Y=1)=2/9.

E[X|Y=2] = 0(1/5)+1(4/5) = 4/5, and again E[X²|Y=2]=4/5, so Var(X|Y=2) = 4/5 − (4/5)² = 4/5 − 16/25 = 4/25 = 0.16.

The law of total expectation

Averaging the conditional means over the distribution of the conditioning variable always recovers the overall (marginal) mean — this is the single most useful identity for problems built around a two-stage random mechanism (a random count of items, each with a random size; a random risk class, with claims that depend on the class; and so on).

LAW OF TOTAL EXPECTATIONE[X] = E[ E[X|Y] ] = Σy E[X|Y=y]·fY(y)
Example 3.3

Verify the law of total expectation using the conditional means from Example 3.2.

Show Solution

E[X] = fY(0)E[X|Y=0] + fY(1)E[X|Y=1] + fY(2)E[X|Y=2]

= 0.30(2/3) + 0.45(2/3) + 0.25(4/5) = 0.20+0.30+0.20 = 0.70,

which matches the marginal computation E[X]=0.70 from Example 3.1 exactly, as it must. ✓

The law of total variance

Variance splits into two interpretable pieces: the average amount of variability within each conditional slice, plus the variability between the conditional means themselves.

LAW OF TOTAL VARIANCEVar(X) = E[ Var(X|Y) ] + Var( E[X|Y] )

The first term, E[Var(X|Y)], is the expected (weighted-average) conditional variance — it measures leftover randomness in X even after you learn Y. The second term, Var(E[X|Y]), is the variance of the conditional mean, treated as a random variable as Y varies — it measures how much knowing Y alone shifts your best guess for X. Both pieces are always non-negative, so Var(X) ≥ E[Var(X|Y)] always — conditioning can only reduce (or leave unchanged) the variance you'd otherwise have to explain.

One subtlety worth flagging explicitly: E[X|Y] is a function of Y alone (once Y's value is plugged in, all the randomness coming from X has been averaged away) — it is emphatically not a function of X. This is precisely why it makes sense to talk about the variance of E[X|Y] as a quantity in its own right: it is the variance of an ordinary (if derived) random variable that happens to depend only on Y. Among all functions of Y alone, E[X|Y] is also the one that best predicts X in a mean-squared-error sense — a fact you do not need to prove for this exam, but one that explains why conditional expectation is the natural notion of "best forecast given available information" referenced earlier in this chapter.

Example 3.4

Verify the law of total variance for X in the auto/home claims table, using the conditional variances from Example 3.2.

Show Solution

Term 1 — expected conditional variance: E[Var(X|Y)] = 0.30(2/9)+0.45(2/9)+0.25(4/25) = 0.75(2/9)+0.04 = 0.1667+0.04 = 0.2067.

Term 2 — variance of the conditional mean: E[X|Y] takes the value 2/3 with probability 0.30+0.45=0.75 and the value 4/5 with probability 0.25. Its mean is 0.75(2/3)+0.25(4/5)=0.50+0.20=0.70 (matching E[X], as required). Its second moment is 0.75(2/3)²+0.25(4/5)² = 0.75(4/9)+0.25(0.64) = 0.3333+0.16=0.4933, so Var(E[X|Y]) = 0.4933−0.70² = 0.4933−0.49=0.0033.

Total: 0.2067+0.0033 = 0.2100, matching the marginal variance Var(X)=E[X²]−(E[X])² = 0.70−0.49=0.21 computed directly. ✓

A special case worth memorizing: variance of a random sum

An extremely common actuarial structure is a random number of claims N, each with an independent, identically distributed severity Xi, giving a total loss S = X1+X2+⋯+XN. Applying the law of total variance by conditioning on N (using E[S|N=n]=nE[X] and Var(S|N=n)=nVar(X) for a fixed count n, from Chapter 6) yields a compact formula that appears often on this exam:

VARIANCE OF A COMPOUND (RANDOM) SUMVar(S) = E[N]·Var(X) + (E[X])²·Var(N)
Example 3.5

The number of storms hitting a region in a year, N, is Poisson with mean 3. Each storm produces a loss that is exponentially distributed with mean 500, independent of N and of the other losses. Find the variance of the total annual loss S.

Show Solution

For a Poisson variable, mean and variance are equal: E[N]=Var(N)=3. For an exponential variable with mean 500, E[X]=500 and Var(X)=500²=250{,}000.

Var(S) = E[N]Var(X) + (E[X])²Var(N) = 3(250{,}000) + 500²(3) = 750{,}000+750{,}000 = 1{,}500{,}000.

Example 3.6 — a mixed risk classification

A randomly selected driver is low-risk with probability 0.7 or high-risk with probability 0.3. Given a low-risk driver, the annual claim count is Poisson with mean 1; given a high-risk driver, the annual claim count is Poisson with mean 4. Let N be the claim count for a randomly selected driver (of unknown risk class). Find E[N] and Var(N).

Show Solution

Let C denote the (unobserved) risk class. By the law of total expectation, E[N] = P(C=low)E[N|low] + P(C=high)E[N|high] = 0.7(1)+0.3(4) = 0.7+1.2 = 1.9.

For the variance, apply the law of total variance. Since each conditional distribution is Poisson, the conditional variance equals the conditional mean: Var(N|low)=1, Var(N|high)=4. So E[Var(N|C)] = 0.7(1)+0.3(4) = 1.9 (numerically the same as E[N] here, purely because the Poisson conditional variances happen to equal the Poisson conditional means).

Next, E[N|C] takes the value 1 with probability 0.7 and the value 4 with probability 0.3; its mean is 1.9 (matching E[N], as required) and its second moment is 0.7(1)+0.3(16) = 0.7+4.8 = 5.5, so Var(E[N|C]) = 5.5 − 1.9² = 5.5−3.61 = 1.89.

Var(N) = E[Var(N|C)] + Var(E[N|C]) = 1.9+1.89 = 3.79.

Notice that Var(N)=3.79 is substantially larger than the mean, 1.9 — even though each individual risk class is Poisson (mean = variance), mixing two risk classes together always inflates the variance beyond what a single Poisson with the same overall mean would have. This "extra" variance, captured by the Var(E[N|C]) term, is exactly the effect of not knowing which risk class a given driver belongs to.

Common mistake. Students sometimes write Var(S) = Var(N)·Var(X) or forget the second term entirely and just use Var(S)=E[N]Var(X) (which would be correct only if N were non-random). Both terms are needed: uncertainty in the claim count contributes to the total variance even when every claim size is identically distributed.
Example 3.7

Recall Example 1.8 (drawing two balls without replacement from an urn with 3 red, 2 blue balls; X,Y are the red/blue indicators of the first and second draws). Compute E[XY], and compare it to the product E[X]E[Y].

Show Solution

Since X and Y only take the values 0 and 1, the product xy is 1 only when both equal 1, and 0 otherwise, so E[XY] = f(1,1) = 0.30 — no other cell of the joint pmf contributes.

From Example 2.8, E[X] = fX(1) = 0.60 and E[Y] = fY(1) = 0.60, so E[X]E[Y] = 0.36.

Since E[XY] = 0.30 < 0.36 = E[X]E[Y], we can already tell — before even computing it formally in Chapter 4 — that Cov(X,Y) = E[XY]−E[X]E[Y] will be negative. This matches intuition: sampling without replacement from a finite population always induces a slight negative association between successive draws of the same category, because removing a red ball on the first draw makes a red ball on the second draw somewhat less available.

Example 3.8 — a nonlinear function of (X,Y)

Using the auto/home claims table of Chapter 1, compute E[max(X,Y)] — the expected value of whichever claim count (auto or home) is larger for a given policyholder.

Show Solution

Apply E[g(X,Y)]=ΣΣg(x,y)f(x,y) with g(x,y)=max(x,y), evaluating max(x,y) at each of the six cells:

max(0,0)=0, max(0,1)=1, max(0,2)=2, max(1,0)=1, max(1,1)=1, max(1,2)=2.

E[max(X,Y)] = 0(0.10)+1(0.15)+2(0.05)+1(0.20)+1(0.30)+2(0.20)

= 0+0.15+0.10+0.20+0.30+0.40 = 1.15.

This is a useful illustration that the E[g(X,Y)]=ΣΣg(x,y)f(x,y) formula requires no special structure on g at all — linear, nonlinear, it makes no difference to the mechanics, only to the arithmetic.

Chapter 3 takeaways. E[g(X,Y)]=ΣΣg(x,y)f(x,y) lets you compute the expectation of any function of a joint pair directly, without first deriving its distribution; linearity of expectation, E[aX+bY]=aE[X]+bE[Y], needs no independence assumption at all. Conditional moments E[X|Y=y] and Var(X|Y=y) behave like ordinary moments but computed from the conditional pmf; the laws of total expectation and total variance let you assemble the marginal mean and variance from these conditional pieces, and are especially powerful for two-stage ("random parameter," "compound sum," "mixed risk class") models.

4. Covariance & Correlation

Learning Outcomes Covered: The Candidate will be able to calculate the covariance and the correlation coefficient for discrete random variables.

Covariance: measuring linear association

Covariance measures whether X and Y tend to move together (positive covariance), move oppositely (negative covariance), or show no linear tendency either way (zero covariance).

COVARIANCE — DEFINITIONCov(X,Y) = E[ (X−E[X])(Y−E[Y]) ]

Expanding the product inside the expectation and using linearity gives the computational shortcut used in essentially every exam problem:

COVARIANCE — COMPUTATIONAL SHORTCUTCov(X,Y) = E[XY] − E[X]·E[Y]

A handful of algebraic properties of covariance are used so routinely that they're worth collecting in one place:

PROPERTIES OF COVARIANCECov(X,Y)=Cov(Y,X) Cov(X,X)=Var(X) Cov(aX+b, cY+d)=ac·Cov(X,Y) Cov(X, Y+Z)=Cov(X,Y)+Cov(X,Z)

The first property (symmetry) is obvious from the definition. The second explains why variance can be thought of as "a variable's covariance with itself" — indeed, setting Y=X in the sum formula of the next section recovers the ordinary variance formula, Var(X+X)=Var(2X)=4Var(X), consistently. The third shows constants shift out of a covariance exactly as they shift out of a variance (as a product, not a sum). The fourth — bilinearity — is what allows the multi-variable variance formula later in this chapter to be built by expanding Var(ΣXi)=Cov(ΣXi, ΣXi) term by term.

Example 4.1

Compute Cov(X,Y) for the auto/home claims table.

Show Solution

From Example 3.1, E[XY]=0.70, E[X]=0.70, E[Y]=0.95.

Cov(X,Y) = 0.70 − (0.70)(0.95) = 0.70 − 0.665 = 0.035.

The small positive value indicates a slight tendency for auto and homeowners claims to occur together for the same policyholder — consistent with what we already saw in Example 2.5: the joint pmf is not the product of its marginals, and here we can now quantify the (mild) direction of that dependence.

Independence implies zero covariance — but not conversely

If X and Y are independent, then E[XY] = ΣΣ xy·fX(x)fY(y) = [Σx fX(x)][Σy fY(y)] = E[X]E[Y], so Cov(X,Y)=0 automatically.

The single most important trap in this chapter. The converse is false: Cov(X,Y)=0 does not imply that X and Y are independent. Covariance only detects linear association; two variables can be tightly, deterministically related in a non-linear (e.g., symmetric or quadratic) way and still have zero covariance. Exam questions exploit this constantly — never conclude independence from a covariance of zero alone.
Example 4.2 — the classic counterexample

Let X take the values −1, 0, 1, each with probability 1/3, and let Y = X². Show that Cov(X,Y)=0 even though Y is completely determined by X (and so they are about as far from independent as possible).

Show Solution

By symmetry, E[X] = (−1+0+1)/3 = 0. Since Y=X², E[XY]=E[X³]; but X³ takes the values −1, 0, 1 with the same probabilities as X itself, so E[X³]=0 as well. Then Cov(X,Y) = E[XY]−E[X]E[Y] = 0 − 0·E[Y] = 0.

Yet X and Y are obviously dependent — knowing X=1 tells you Y=1 with certainty. Indeed, P(X=1,Y=0)=0 while P(X=1)P(Y=0) = (1/3)(1/3) ≠ 0, so the independence identity fails outright, even though the covariance vanishes.

Variance of a sum: the bilinear formula

Expanding Var(X+Y)=E[(X+Y)²]−(E[X+Y])² and simplifying leads to the key generalization of the "variances add" rule you may already know for independent variables:

VARIANCE OF A SUMVar(X+Y) = Var(X) + Var(Y) + 2Cov(X,Y)
VARIANCE OF A GENERAL LINEAR COMBINATION (two variables)Var(aX+bY) = a²Var(X) + b²Var(Y) + 2ab·Cov(X,Y)

The extra 2ab·Cov(X,Y) cross term is exactly what independence eliminates (since Cov=0 then) — which is why the simple "variances add" rule from Chapter 6 only holds for independent random variables. When X and Y are dependent, you must include this cross term or the answer will be wrong.

This cross term is also the mathematical backbone of the intuitive idea of diversification. When you combine two positively correlated risks (Cov(X,Y)>0), the total variance is inflated beyond the sum of the individual variances — the risks tend to go wrong together, so nothing is "diversified away." When the two risks are negatively correlated (Cov(X,Y)<0, as in Example 4.8's urn illustration), the total variance is reduced below the sum of the individual variances — bad outcomes in one risk are partially offset by good outcomes in the other. Independent risks (Cov=0) sit exactly in between, with variances simply adding. This is precisely why insurers and reinsurers care about the correlation structure of a book of business, not just the variance of each policy in isolation.

The same idea extends to any number of random variables — every pair of distinct variables contributes its own covariance cross term:

VARIANCE OF A GENERAL LINEAR COMBINATION (n variables)Var(Σi aiXi) = Σi ai²Var(Xi) + 2 Σi<j aiajCov(Xi,Xj)

The double sum runs over every unordered pair i<j exactly once — for three variables that's 3 cross terms (pairs 1–2, 1–3, 2–3); for four variables it's 6 cross terms, and so on. If all the variables happen to be mutually independent, every one of those cross terms is zero and the formula collapses back down to Var(ΣaiXi) = Σai²Var(Xi), the special case used throughout Chapter 6.

Example 4.3

Using Var(X)=0.21, Var(Y)=0.5475, and Cov(X,Y)=0.035 from the auto/home claims table, compute Var(X+Y), and confirm it against the distribution of S=X+Y found directly from the joint pmf.

Show Solution

Via the formula: Var(X+Y) = 0.21+0.5475+2(0.035) = 0.21+0.5475+0.07 = 0.8275.

Direct check: S=X+Y takes the values 0,1,2,3 with probabilities P(S=0)=f(0,0)=0.10, P(S=1)=f(0,1)+f(1,0)=0.15+0.20=0.35, P(S=2)=f(0,2)+f(1,1)=0.05+0.30=0.35, P(S=3)=f(1,2)=0.20. Then E[S]=0(.10)+1(.35)+2(.35)+3(.20)=1.65 (matching E[X]+E[Y]), E[S²]=0+.35+4(.35)+9(.20)=.35+1.40+1.80=3.55, so Var(S)=3.55−1.65²=3.55−2.7225=0.8275. The two methods agree exactly. ✓

Example 4.4

Using the same values, compute Var(2X−Y).

Show Solution

Apply the bilinear formula with a=2, b=−1: Var(2X−Y) = 4Var(X) + Var(Y) + 2(2)(−1)Cov(X,Y) = 4(0.21)+0.5475−4(0.035)

= 0.84+0.5475−0.14 = 1.2475.

Example 4.5 — three variables

Suppose Var(X1)=2, Var(X2)=3, Var(X3)=4, Cov(X1,X2)=0.5, Cov(X1,X3)=−0.3, and Cov(X2,X3)=0.2. Find Var(X1+2X2−X3).

Show Solution

Here a1=1, a2=2, a3=−1. First the three squared-coefficient variance terms:

1²(2) + 2²(3) + (−1)²(4) = 2+12+4 = 18.

Now the three pairwise cross terms (each doubled):

2[a1a2Cov(X1,X2) + a1a3Cov(X1,X3) + a2a3Cov(X2,X3)]

= 2[(1)(2)(0.5) + (1)(−1)(−0.3) + (2)(−1)(0.2)] = 2[1.0+0.3−0.4] = 2(0.9) = 1.8.

Var(X1+2X2−X3) = 18+1.8 = 19.8.

Common mistake. When a coefficient is negative, only the cross term's sign flips relative to the sum case — the squared-coefficient variance terms are always added (never subtracted), since a square is never negative. In Var(X−Y), for instance, the result is Var(X)+Var(Y)−2Cov(X,Y) — variances still add, only the covariance term is subtracted.

The correlation coefficient

Covariance is unit-dependent (its units are the product of the units of X and Y), which makes its magnitude hard to interpret on its own. The correlation coefficient rescales covariance into a dimensionless quantity between −1 and 1:

CORRELATION COEFFICIENTρ(X,Y) = Cov(X,Y) / (σXσY)

Two properties are tested repeatedly:

  • −1 ≤ ρ ≤ 1 always (a consequence of the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality); ρ = ±1 exactly when Y is a perfect linear (increasing, for +1; decreasing, for −1) function of X.
  • ρ is invariant under positive linear rescaling of either variable, and flips sign under a sign change: ρ(aX+b, cY+d) = sign(ac)·ρ(X,Y) for constants a,c ≠ 0.

Why is ρ trapped between −1 and 1? Consider the variance of X/σX − Y/σY, which — being a variance — can never be negative. Expanding it with the bilinear formula gives Var(X/σX) + Var(Y/σY) − 2Cov(X/σX, Y/σY) = 1+1−2ρ(X,Y) ≥ 0, which forces ρ(X,Y) ≤ 1. Running the identical argument on X/σX + Y/σY forces ρ(X,Y) ≥ −1. Equality holds exactly when the corresponding variance is zero — i.e., when X/σX and Y/σY differ by at most a constant, which is precisely the statement that Y is a perfect linear function of X.

Example 4.6

Compute ρ(X,Y) for the auto/home claims table and interpret its size.

Show Solution

σX = √0.21 = 0.4583, σY = √0.5475 = 0.7399.

ρ = 0.035 / (0.4583 × 0.7399) = 0.035/0.3391 = 0.103.

A correlation of about 0.10 indicates a very weak positive linear association — consistent with the small departure from independence found earlier.

Example 4.7 — invariance under linear transformation

Show algebraically that ρ(aX+b, Y) = sign(a)·ρ(X,Y) for a constant a ≠ 0.

Show Solution

Cov(aX+b, Y) = E[(aX+b)Y] − E[aX+b]E[Y] = aE[XY]+bE[Y] − (aE[X]+b)E[Y] = a(E[XY]−E[X]E[Y]) = a·Cov(X,Y).

The standard deviation of aX+b is σaX+b = |a|·σX (a standard deviation is always non-negative, so the absolute value is required).

ρ(aX+b,Y) = [a·Cov(X,Y)] / (|a|σX·σY) = (a/|a|)·ρ(X,Y) = sign(a)·ρ(X,Y). So multiplying X by a positive constant (and shifting it by any amount) never changes the correlation; multiplying by a negative constant flips its sign but not its magnitude.

Example 4.8 — negative covariance from sampling without replacement

Finish the urn example (Examples 1.8, 2.8, 3.7): compute Cov(X,Y) and ρ(X,Y).

Show Solution

From Example 3.7, E[XY]=0.30, E[X]=E[Y]=0.60, so Cov(X,Y) = 0.30−0.36 = −0.06.

Each of X and Y is Bernoulli with p=0.6, so Var(X)=Var(Y)=0.6(0.4)=0.24, giving σX=σY=√0.24=0.4899.

ρ(X,Y) = −0.06/(0.4899×0.4899) = −0.06/0.24 = −0.25.

The negative correlation confirms the intuition from Example 3.7: drawing without replacement from a small, finite population creates a real (if modest) negative linear association between the outcomes of successive draws — knowing the first draw was red makes a red second draw less likely, and vice versa. This is a useful general fact: sampling without replacement always induces non-positive covariance between same-category indicators, whereas sampling with replacement (independent draws) would give exactly zero covariance.

Example 4.9 — portfolio variance from correlations

A portfolio holds three dependent risks with Var(X1)=100, Var(X2)=150, Var(X3)=200, and pairwise correlations ρ12=0.3, ρ13=−0.2, ρ23=0.1. Find Var(X1+X2+X3).

Show Solution

Since correlations, not covariances, are given, first convert each using Cov(Xi,Xj) = ρijσiσj. The standard deviations are σ1=10, σ2=√150=12.247, σ3=√200=14.142.

Cov(X1,X2) = 0.3(10)(12.247) = 36.74

Cov(X1,X3) = −0.2(10)(14.142) = −28.28

Cov(X2,X3) = 0.1(12.247)(14.142) = 17.32

Now apply the three-variable variance formula: sum the individual variances, then add twice the sum of the three covariances.

Var(X1+X2+X3) = (100+150+200) + 2(36.74−28.28+17.32) = 450 + 2(25.78) = 450+51.55 = 501.55.

Why this matters practically. Correlation is unaffected by a change of currency (e.g., dollars to thousands of dollars) or a change of origin (e.g., adding a flat surcharge). This is exactly why insurers can compare correlations of losses measured in different units, or before/after a deductible shift by a constant, without recomputing anything.
Example 4.10 — a full covariance-and-correlation calculation from a table

The joint pmf of X∈{1,2,3} and Y∈{1,2} is given below. Find Cov(X,Y) and ρ(X,Y).

X \ Y 1 2
1 0.10 0.15
2 0.20 0.15
3 0.25 0.15
Show Solution

Marginals: fX(1)=0.25, fX(2)=0.35, fX(3)=0.40; fY(1)=0.55, fY(2)=0.45.

E[X] = 1(.25)+2(.35)+3(.40) = 2.15, E[X²] = 1(.25)+4(.35)+9(.40) = 5.25, so Var(X)=5.25−2.15²=0.6275.

E[Y] = 1(.55)+2(.45) = 1.45, E[Y²] = 1(.55)+4(.45) = 2.35, so Var(Y)=2.35−1.45²=0.2475.

E[XY] = 1(1)(.10)+1(2)(.15)+2(1)(.20)+2(2)(.15)+3(1)(.25)+3(2)(.15) = 0.10+0.30+0.40+0.60+0.75+0.90 = 3.05.

Cov(X,Y) = 3.05 − (2.15)(1.45) = 3.05−3.1175 = −0.0675.

ρ(X,Y) = −0.0675/(√0.6275·√0.2475) = −0.0675/(0.7922×0.4975) = −0.0675/0.3941 = −0.171.

Chapter 4 takeaways. Cov(X,Y)=E[XY]−E[X]E[Y]; independence forces covariance to zero but zero covariance never proves independence. Var(aX+bY)=a²Var(X)+b²Var(Y)+2ab·Cov(X,Y) generalizes to any number of variables by adding one cross term per pair. Correlation ρ=Cov(X,Y)/(σXσY) rescales covariance to always lie in [−1,1] and is unaffected by positive linear rescaling of either variable.

5. Order Statistics

Learning Outcomes Covered: The Candidate will be able to determine the joint distribution of order statistics for a set of independent random variables.

Setup and notation

Let X1, X2, …, Xn be independent and identically distributed (iid) random variables with common CDF F(x) and density f(x). Sorting them from smallest to largest gives the order statistics:

ORDER STATISTICSX(1) ≤ X(2) ≤ ⋯ ≤ X(n)

X(1) is the sample minimum, X(n) is the sample maximum, and X(k) is the k-th smallest value. These arise constantly in actuarial applications: the largest of several ceded losses under a reinsurance layer, the time until the first of several components fails, the median claim size in a small sample.

It is worth being explicit about what is not assumed here: the individual Xi need not be normal, or even continuous in every result below — only independence and a shared distribution are required. This is what makes order statistics broadly useful in an actuarial context: the underlying claim severities, equipment lifetimes, or measurement errors can follow essentially any distribution you've encountered elsewhere in this manual (exponential, gamma, uniform, Pareto-like power laws), and the machinery of this chapter applies unchanged, since every formula below is built entirely from the parent CDF F(x) and density f(x), whatever they happen to be.

Order statistics also connect directly to percentile-based risk measures used elsewhere in actuarial practice: the sample median is X(k) with k at or near the middle of 1,…,n, and more generally, an empirical estimate of a percentile of an unknown distribution is built directly from an order statistic of an observed sample. While percentile estimation itself is outside this exam's scope, recognizing the vocabulary — "the largest of several observations," "the second-smallest," "the sample median" — as pointing straight at this chapter's formulas is a valuable pattern-matching skill.

The joint density of the full set of order statistics

Since the original sample is iid, all n! orderings (permutations) of any particular set of n distinct values are equally likely to have produced that set. This gives the joint density of the ordered sample directly:

JOINT DENSITY OF ALL ORDER STATISTICSf(x1,x2,…,xn) = n!·f(x1)f(x2)⋯f(xn), for x1 < x2 < ⋯ < xn

The factor n! arises because there are exactly n! ways to assign the labels X1,…,Xn to a given ordered set of values, and each labeling has the same joint density f(x1)⋯f(xn) by independence. In practice, the exam almost never asks for this full joint density directly — it asks about the distribution of a single order statistic (the min, the max, or the k-th one), derived below.

Distribution of the maximum

The maximum is below x exactly when every observation is below x — and by independence, that probability is a simple product:

CDF & PDF OF THE MAXIMUMF(n)(x) = P(X(n) ≤ x) = [F(x)]n f(n)(x) = n[F(x)]n−1f(x)

Distribution of the minimum

The minimum is above x exactly when every observation is above x:

CDF & PDF OF THE MINIMUMF(1)(x) = 1 − [1−F(x)]n f(1)(x) = n[1−F(x)]n−1f(x)

The general k-th order statistic

For X(k) to land in a small interval around x, exactly k−1 of the remaining n−1 observations must fall below x, and the other n−k must fall above x. Counting the number of ways to choose which observations play which role gives the general formula:

PDF OF THE k-TH ORDER STATISTICf(k)(x) = [n! / ((k−1)!(n−k)!)] · [F(x)]k−1 · [1−F(x)]n−k · f(x)

Setting k=n recovers the maximum formula (the exponent on 1−F(x) becomes 0); setting k=1 recovers the minimum formula (the exponent on F(x) becomes 0).

Common mistake. It is very easy to swap which exponent goes with F(x) and which goes with 1−F(x). Remember: k−1 observations must fall below x (that's the F(x) piece, since F measures probability below), and n−k must fall above (the 1−F(x) piece). If you find yourself with a formula where a small k (e.g., the minimum, k=1) has a large exponent on F(x), you have it backwards.
Example 5.1

Let X1,…,X5 be iid Uniform(0,1). Find the density of the minimum and its expected value.

Show Solution

Here F(x)=x and f(x)=1 on (0,1), with n=5. By the minimum formula, f(1)(x) = 5(1−x)4, 0<x<1.

E[X(1)] = ∫01 x·5(1−x)4 dx. Substituting u=1−x: = 5∫01(1−u)u4 du = 5[1/5 − 1/6] = 5(1/30) = 1/6 ≈ 0.167.

This matches the well-known general result that the minimum of n iid Uniform(0,1) variables has mean 1/(n+1).

Example 5.2 — the minimum of independent exponentials

Let X1,…,Xn be iid Exponential with rate λ (mean 1/λ). Show that the minimum is itself exponential, and identify its rate.

Show Solution

F(x)=1−e−λx, so 1−F(x)=e−λx. By the minimum-CDF formula,

F(1)(x) = 1 − [e−λx]n = 1 − e−nλx,

which is exactly the CDF of an Exponential random variable with rate nλ (mean 1/(nλ)). This is a classical result with an intuitive justification via the memoryless property: n independent exponential "alarm clocks," each ringing at rate λ, combine into a single process where some clock rings at the combined rate nλ — exactly the "first to fail" logic behind reliability of components in parallel/series systems.

Example 5.3 — the median of a small sample

Let X1,X2,X3 be iid Uniform(0,1). Find the density of the median X(2) and its expected value.

Show Solution

Here n=3, k=2, F(x)=x, f(x)=1. The general formula gives

f(2)(x) = [3!/(1!·1!)]·x1·(1−x)1·1 = 6x(1−x), 0<x<1.

By the symmetry of this density about x=1/2, E[X(2)]=1/2. Directly: ∫01 x·6x(1−x) dx = 6∫(x²−x³)dx = 6[1/3−1/4] = 6(1/12) = 1/2. ✓

Example 5.4 — an applied reliability problem

Four independent components, each with lifetime Exponential with mean 1000 hours, are arranged in parallel (the system keeps running until all four have failed). Find the probability the system is still running at t=3000 hours.

Show Solution

The system's lifetime is the maximum of the four component lifetimes, X(4). With F(x)=1−e−x/1000, we need P(X(4) > 3000) = 1 − [F(3000)]4.

F(3000) = 1−e−3 = 1−0.049787 = 0.950213.

[F(3000)]4 = 0.9502134 ≈ 0.81524.

P(X(4) > 3000) = 1 − 0.81524 = 0.1848.

Order statistics of a discrete distribution

Everything derived so far for the CDF of the minimum and maximum — F(n)(x)=[F(x)]n and F(1)(x)=1−[1−F(x)]n — used only independence, and holds equally well when the parent distribution is discrete. What changes in the discrete case is that you can no longer differentiate the CDF to get a density; instead, recover the pmf of the order statistic by differencing the CDF at consecutive integers, exactly as you would for any discrete CDF.

Example 5.5 — the maximum of several discrete rolls

Four fair six-sided dice are rolled independently. Let M be the largest value shown. Find P(M=6).

Show Solution

For a single die, F(x) = x/6 for integer x=1,…,6. By the maximum-CDF formula (which requires only independence, not continuity), FM(x) = [F(x)]4 = (x/6)4.

Since M is discrete, obtain the pmf at x=6 by differencing: P(M=6) = FM(6)−FM(5) = 1 − (5/6)4.

(5/6)4 = 625/1296 = 0.48225, so P(M=6) = 1−0.48225 = 0.51775.

Equivalently (and perhaps more intuitively): P(M=6) = 1 − P(all four dice show at most 5) = 1 − (5/6)4, the complement of "every die avoids 6" — the same calculation, arrived at directly rather than through the CDF-differencing machinery.

The joint density of two order statistics, and the sample range

Beyond the density of a single order statistic, it is sometimes useful to know the joint density of two order statistics at once — this is the piece of the theory that most literally matches the learning outcome's phrase "joint distribution of order statistics." For i < j, the same combinatorial reasoning used for a single order statistic (count how many observations fall in each of three zones: below x, between x and y, and above y) gives:

JOINT DENSITY OF X(i) AND X(j), i<jf(i,j)(x,y) = [n!/((i−1)!(j−i−1)!(n−j)!)]·[F(x)]i−1[F(y)−F(x)]j−i−1[1−F(y)]n−j·f(x)f(y), x<y

A particularly useful special case is the joint density of the minimum and maximum together (i=1, j=n), which simplifies because the exponents on F(x) and 1−F(y) both vanish:

JOINT DENSITY OF THE MIN AND MAXf(1,n)(x,y) = n(n−1)[F(y)−F(x)]n−2f(x)f(y), x<y

This joint density is exactly what's needed to study the sample range, R = X(n) − X(1), a quantity that shows up whenever a problem asks about the spread of a small independent sample.

Example 5.6 — the range of a small uniform sample

Let X1,X2,X3 be iid Uniform(0,1). Find the density of the range R = X(3)−X(1), and calculate P(R > 0.5).

Show Solution

With F(t)=t, f(t)=1, and n=3, the joint density of the min and max is f(1,3)(x,y) = 3(2)(y−x)1(1)(1) = 6(y−x) for 0<x<y<1.

To get the density of R=Y−X, fix r and integrate the joint density over all valid values of x (from 0 to 1−r), noting y=x+r makes y−x=r constant along the line of integration:

fR(r) = ∫01−r 6r dx = 6r(1−r), 0<r<1.

Then P(R>0.5) = ∫0.51 6r(1−r) dr = 6[r²/2 − r³/3]0.51 = 6[(0.5−0.3333)−(0.125−0.0417)] = 6(0.1667−0.0833) = 6(0.0833) = 0.5.

Example 5.7 — the maximum of n iid uniforms, in general

Let X1,…,Xn be iid Uniform(0,1). Find a general formula for E[X(n)] and compare it to the mean of the minimum found in Example 5.1.

Show Solution

With F(x)=x and f(x)=1, the maximum density is f(n)(x) = n[F(x)]n−1f(x) = nxn−1, 0<x<1.

E[X(n)] = ∫01 x·nxn−1 dx = n∫01 xn dx = n/(n+1).

Comparing to the general result E[X(1)]=1/(n+1) (Example 5.1 was the special case n=5): the minimum and maximum sit symmetrically around the center of the interval, at distances 1/(n+1) from each endpoint — a pleasing confirmation of the intuitive symmetry of the Uniform(0,1) distribution about x=1/2. As n grows, E[X(n)]→1 and E[X(1)]→0: with enough independent draws, the sample essentially fills up the whole interval.

Example 5.8 — a k-out-of-n system

A system has 5 independent identical components, each with lifetime Exponential with mean 1000 hours. The system continues to function as long as at least 3 of the 5 components are still working ("3-out-of-5" system). Find the probability the system is still functioning at t=1500 hours.

Show Solution

The system fails at the moment the 3rd component fails (since at that point only 2 remain, and 2 is not enough) — so the system's lifetime is exactly the order statistic X(3) (the 3rd-smallest of the 5 lifetimes, i.e., the time of the 3rd failure). The system survives past t exactly when fewer than 3 of the 5 components have failed by time t — that is, 0, 1, or 2 failures among the 5 independent Bernoulli-type "has this component failed by time t" events, each with probability F(t).

At t=1500: F(1500)=1−e−1.5=1−0.22313=0.77687, and 1−F(1500)=0.22313.

P(0 failures) = 0.223135 = 0.000553

P(1 failure) = 5(0.77687)(0.22313)4 = 5(0.77687)(0.0024788) = 0.009627

P(2 failures) = 10(0.77687)²(0.22313)3 = 10(0.603533)(0.011109) = 0.067047

P(X(3)>1500) = 0.000553+0.009627+0.067047 = 0.0772.

This "k-out-of-n system" framing — recognizing that the lifetime of a system requiring at least k of n components equals the (n−k+1)-th order statistic of the component lifetimes — is a standard and testable application of order statistics to reliability problems.

Chapter 5 takeaways. For an iid sample, F(n)(x)=[F(x)]n (maximum) and F(1)(x)=1−[1−F(x)]n (minimum) follow directly from independence; the general k-th order statistic density weights [F(x)]k−1[1−F(x)]n−kf(x) by the multinomial coefficient counting how many observations fall below versus above x. The same CDF formulas apply to discrete parent distributions too — just difference the CDF instead of differentiating it.

6. Linear Combinations of Independent Random Variables

Learning Outcomes Covered: The Candidate will be able to calculate probabilities for linear combinations of independent discrete random variables as well as for continuous normal random variables, and calculate moments for linear combinations of independent random variables.

Mean and variance of a linear combination

For independent random variables X1,…,Xn and constants a1,…,an, b, linearity of expectation gives the mean exactly as in Chapter 3 (no independence needed there); independence is what makes the variance formula this simple — every cross-covariance term vanishes.

MEAN & VARIANCE OF A LINEAR COMBINATION (INDEPENDENT CASE)E[ΣaiXi + b] = ΣaiE[Xi] + b Var(ΣaiXi + b) = Σai²Var(Xi)

Compare this to the general (possibly-dependent) formula from Chapter 4 — Var(aX+bY)=a²Var(X)+b²Var(Y)+2ab·Cov(X,Y) — and notice that independence is exactly what deletes the covariance cross-terms.

This chapter asks two closely related but distinct questions about a linear combination of independent random variables: what are its mean and variance (always answerable using the boxed formulas above, regardless of the underlying distributions), and — a much stronger question — what is its exact distribution? The second question only has a clean closed-form answer for a handful of special families, covered next; outside of those families, the exact distribution of a sum of independent random variables is generally intractable by hand, and the practical fallback is the Central Limit Theorem of Chapter 7.

It is also worth noting explicitly what independence buys you beyond the variance formula: it guarantees that no single term in the linear combination can be predicted from the others, so every unit of variance contributed by each Xi is "new" information rather than a repeat of variability already counted elsewhere. This is the deeper reason the variance of an independent sum grows only as fast as the sum of the individual variances (rather than being inflated by the covariance cross-terms of Chapter 4) — and it is exactly this additivity of "new" variance, term after term, that eventually produces the smooth, bell-shaped total described by the Central Limit Theorem in the next chapter.

Reproductive properties: sums that stay in the same family

For several important named families, a sum of independent members of the family is again a member of the same family, provided a key parameter matches across all the summands.

Reproductive (closure) properties under summation of independent variables
If independent… …then the sum is Condition required
X~Binomial(n₁,p), Y~Binomial(n₂,p) X+Y ~ Binomial(n₁+n₂, p) same p
X~Poisson(λ₁), Y~Poisson(λ₂) X+Y ~ Poisson(λ₁+λ₂) none — always holds
X~N(μ₁,σ₁²), Y~N(μ₂,σ₂²) aX+bY ~ N(aμ₁+bμ₂, a²σ₁²+b²σ₂²) none — always holds
X~Gamma(α₁,θ), Y~Gamma(α₂,θ) X+Y ~ Gamma(α₁+α₂, θ) same scale θ

The Exponential distribution is the special case α=1 of the Gamma, so a sum of n iid Exponential(mean θ) variables is Gamma(shape n, scale θ) — an Erlang distribution.

Common mistake. The reproductive property for Binomial and Gamma requires the matching parameter (p for Binomial, θ for Gamma) to be identical across the summands. A sum of two independent Binomial variables with different success probabilities is not Binomial at all — its distribution is more complicated (a convolution), even though its mean and variance can still be computed term-by-term using the boxed formula above. The Poisson and Normal properties, by contrast, hold no matter what the individual parameters are.
Example 6.1

Claims counts from two independent blocks of business are X ~ Binomial(10, 0.3) and Y ~ Binomial(15, 0.3). Let T=X+Y. Find P(T ≤ 2).

Show Solution

Since both blocks share the same success probability p=0.3, the reproductive property applies: T ~ Binomial(25, 0.3). Compute each term of P(T≤2) = P(T=0)+P(T=1)+P(T=2) using n=25, p=0.3, so q=0.7:

P(T=0) = 0.725 = 0.0001341

P(T=1) = 25(0.3)(0.7)24 = 0.0014368

P(T=2) = C(25,2)(0.3)²(0.7)23 = 0.0073900

P(T≤2) ≈ 0.0001341+0.0014368+0.0073900 = 0.00896.

Example 6.2

Daily claim counts from Region A and Region B are independent, A~Poisson(2) and B~Poisson(3). Find P(A+B=4).

Show Solution

By the Poisson reproductive property, T=A+B ~ Poisson(5).

P(T=4) = e−554/4! = 0.0067379 × 625/24 = 0.0067379×26.0417 = 0.1755.

Example 6.3 — a normal linear combination

Let X~N(500,40²) and Y~N(300,30²) be independent measurement errors (in some suitable units). Find P(X−Y > 150).

Show Solution

Let W=X−Y. By the normal reproductive property, W~N(500−300, 40²+30²) = N(200, 2500), so σW=50.

Standardize: Z = (150−200)/50 = −1.

P(W>150) = P(Z>−1) = Φ(1) = 0.8413.

Example 6.4 — Gamma sum via the Poisson-process connection

An insurer's losses arrive according to a stream of 4 independent Exponential(mean 200) severities, and total loss is S = the sum of these 4 severities. Find P(S > 1000).

Show Solution

By the reproductive property, S ~ Gamma(shape 4, scale 200) (an Erlang-4 distribution). Rather than integrating the Gamma density directly, use the well-known Poisson-process link: if N(t) counts Poisson(rate 1/200) events up to time t, then "the 4th event hasn't happened by time t" is the same event as "S > t." At t=1000, the Poisson mean is 1000/200=5, so

P(S>1000) = P(N(1000) ≤ 3) = Σk=03 e−55k/k!

= e−5(1+5+12.5+20.8333) = 0.0067379 × 39.3333 = 0.2650.

Example 6.5 — a three-variable normal portfolio

A reinsurer's retained loss is L = 0.5X + 0.3Y + 0.2Z, where X~N(1000,200²), Y~N(800,150²), and Z~N(1200,250²) are independent lines of business. Find P(L > 1000).

Show Solution

Since X,Y,Z are independent normals, any linear combination of them is also normal, with mean and variance obtained from the boxed formulas of this chapter:

E[L] = 0.5(1000)+0.3(800)+0.2(1200) = 500+240+240 = 980.

Var(L) = 0.5²(200²)+0.3²(150²)+0.2²(250²) = 0.25(40{,}000)+0.09(22{,}500)+0.04(62{,}500)

= 10{,}000+2025+2500 = 14{,}525, so σL = √14{,}525 = 120.52.

Z-score: (1000−980)/120.52 = 0.166.

P(L>1000) = 1−Φ(0.17) ≈ 1−0.5675 = 0.4325.

Example 6.6 — a difference is not Binomial

Let X~Binomial(2,0.5) and Y~Binomial(2,0.5) be independent (same n and same p). Find the pmf of D=X−Y, and confirm that it is not itself a Binomial distribution.

Show Solution

Each of X and Y has pmf P(0)=0.25, P(1)=0.5, P(2)=0.25. Since D can range from −2 to 2, condition on every combination that produces each value of D:

P(D=0) = P(X=0)P(Y=0)+P(X=1)P(Y=1)+P(X=2)P(Y=2) = 0.0625+0.25+0.0625 = 0.375

P(D=1) = P(X=1)P(Y=0)+P(X=2)P(Y=1) = 0.125+0.125 = 0.25, and by symmetry P(D=−1)=0.25.

P(D=2) = P(X=2)P(Y=0) = 0.0625, and by symmetry P(D=−2)=0.0625.

These five probabilities sum to 1. Notice that D takes negative values with positive probability — something a Binomial random variable can never do — so D is certainly not Binomial, even though X and Y share the same n and p. The reproductive property in the table above is stated for sums of independent like-parameter variables; it says nothing about differences, and indeed cannot, since a difference of two counts need not itself be a valid count.

Example 6.7 — when the reproductive shortcut doesn't apply

Two independent blocks of policies have claim counts X~Binomial(200, 0.3) and Y~Binomial(150, 0.5). Let T=X+Y. Approximate P(T > 140).

Show Solution

Since X and Y have different success probabilities, T is not Binomial — the reproductive shortcut of this chapter does not apply, and there is no simple closed-form pmf for T. However, the mean and variance of T are still easy to compute using independence alone: E[X]=200(0.3)=60, Var(X)=200(0.3)(0.7)=42; E[Y]=150(0.5)=75, Var(Y)=150(0.5)(0.5)=37.5.

E[T] = 60+75 = 135, Var(T) = 42+37.5 = 79.5, so σT = √79.5 = 8.916.

Because T is a sum of a large number (350) of independent Bernoulli trials, the Central Limit Theorem (the subject of Chapter 7) lets us treat T as approximately normal even without an exact reproductive form. Applying a continuity correction (since T is a discrete count):

Z = (140.5−135)/8.916 = 0.617.

P(T>140) = P(T≥141) ≈ 1−Φ(0.62) = 1−0.7324 = 0.2676.

This example is exactly the bridge between this chapter and the next: whenever the reproductive shortcut fails (mismatched parameters, or no reproductive property at all for the families involved), the Central Limit Theorem is almost always the fallback tool, provided the number of independent pieces being summed is reasonably large.

Sums versus differences. The reproductive properties in this chapter's table apply only to sums of independent random variables. A difference of two independent Binomial (or Poisson, or Gamma) variables is generally not a member of the same family — most obviously because differences can be negative while these families cannot. The mean and variance of a difference are still computed the same way as for a sum (using Var(X−Y)=Var(X)+Var(Y) for independent X,Y, since the covariance term is zero either way), but do not assume the difference's distribution stays in the family — only sums enjoy that closure property, and only the Normal family is simultaneously closed under both sums and differences (because negating a normal variable, −Y, is again normal).
Non-independent linear combinations. If the variables in a linear combination are not independent, you cannot use the simplified variance formula of this chapter — fall back to the general bilinear formula from Chapter 4, which includes the covariance cross-terms.
Why the reproductive properties work (optional derivation). Each of these families can be characterized by a moment generating function (MGF) that has a simple, recognizable form, and the MGF of a sum of independent variables is always the product of the individual MGFs. As a concrete illustration, the MGF of a Poisson(λ) variable is M(t)=exp(λ(et−1)). If X~Poisson(λ1) and Y~Poisson(λ2) are independent, the MGF of X+Y is the product MX(t)MY(t) = exp(λ1(et−1))·exp(λ2(et−1)) = exp((λ1+λ2)(et−1)), which is recognizable as the MGF of a Poisson(λ1+λ2) variable — and since an MGF uniquely determines its distribution, this proves the reproductive property outright rather than just motivating it. The Binomial, Normal, and Gamma families admit an analogous argument: multiplying their MGFs (with the matching-parameter condition in place) always collapses back into the MGF of the same family with parameters added. You do not need MGF machinery to use the results in the table above, but it explains why these four families are special rather than an arbitrary list to memorize.
Chapter 6 takeaways. For independent variables, Var(ΣaiXi)=Σai²Var(Xi) — no covariance terms. Binomial (same p), Poisson (any parameters), Normal (any parameters), and Gamma (same scale) are reproductive under summation; only Normal is also reproductive under subtraction. When in doubt about whether a named family's reproductive property applies, check the matching-parameter condition first.

7. The Central Limit Theorem

Learning Outcomes Covered: The Candidate will be able to apply the Central Limit Theorem to calculate approximations of probabilities for linear combinations of independent and identically distributed random variables.

Statement of the theorem

The Central Limit Theorem (CLT) is arguably the single most exam-relevant result in all of probability: it says that a sum (or average) of a large number of independent, identically distributed random variables is approximately normal — no matter what distribution the individual variables follow — as long as their mean and variance are finite.

CENTRAL LIMIT THEOREMIf X1,…,Xn are iid with mean μ and variance σ², then for large n: Sn=ΣXi is approximately N(nμ, nσ²); X̄n=Sn/n is approximately N(μ, σ²/n)
STANDARDIZED FORMZ = (Sn − nμ) / (σ√n) is approximately standard normal

The practical recipe is always the same three steps: (1) find the mean and variance of the total (or average), (2) standardize the boundary value(s) of interest, (3) look up the resulting probability in the standard normal table. A commonly used rule of thumb is that n ≥ 30 gives a reasonably good approximation for most underlying distributions, though highly skewed parent distributions may need a larger n for the approximation to be accurate.

What makes the CLT so powerful for exam purposes is that it applies uniformly across essentially every named distribution in the syllabus — discrete or continuous, symmetric or skewed — as long as you can compute the mean and variance of a single observation. You never need to know the exact shape of the parent distribution to apply it; you only need its first two moments and a large enough sample size. This is also precisely why the theorem shows up so often in word problems that never explicitly name a distribution at all ("the time to process a claim has mean 12 minutes and standard deviation 5 minutes" is a complete enough description to apply the CLT, even though no distributional family was ever specified).

The "large enough" qualifier deserves a brief comment. A symmetric parent distribution (like a Uniform or a Normal itself) produces an excellent normal approximation for the sum even at fairly small n; a heavily right-skewed parent distribution (like a Pareto with a thick right tail, or an Exponential with rate far from 1 relative to the scale of interest) needs a larger n before the sum's distribution looks convincingly normal, particularly out in the tails. On this exam, you are essentially never asked to judge whether a given n is "large enough" — the problem will either state or clearly imply that the normal approximation should be used — so the practical takeaway is simply to apply the CLT recipe whenever a problem describes a sum or average of many independent pieces, without second-guessing whether the approximation is technically valid.

Exact versus approximate normality — do not confuse the two. Chapter 6 established that a linear combination of independent normal random variables is exactly normal, no matter how small n is — even n=2 works perfectly. The Central Limit Theorem of this chapter is a completely different claim: it says a sum of independent random variables from any distribution (not assumed normal) becomes approximately normal, and only as n grows large. If a problem already tells you the summands are normal, you never need the CLT or its continuity correction — Chapter 6's exact reproductive result applies regardless of sample size. Reach for the CLT specifically when the parent distribution is not normal (or not fully specified at all — only its mean and variance are given) and the sample size is large.

A special case: the sample proportion

When each individual observation is itself a Bernoulli indicator (success/failure, claim/no claim, defective/not defective) with success probability p, the total Sn is exactly Binomial(n,p), and the CLT applied to the average of the indicators — i.e., the observed sample proportion p̂ = Sn/n — gives a formula worth recognizing on sight:

CLT FOR A SAMPLE PROPORTIONp̂ = Sn/n is approximately N( p, p(1−p)/n )

This is nothing more than the ordinary CLT applied with μ=p and σ²=p(1−p) (the mean and variance of a single Bernoulli trial), but it is common enough — especially in problems about sample surveys, defect rates, or the fraction of a portfolio exhibiting some behavior — that it is worth recognizing as its own named result rather than re-deriving every time.

The continuity correction

When the variable being summed is discrete and integer-valued (a count — Binomial, Poisson, or any other counting variable), replacing a probability mass at a single integer with an area under a smooth normal curve introduces a small systematic bias unless you adjust the boundary by ±0.5 first.

CONTINUITY CORRECTIONP(Sn ≤ k) ≈ Φ( (k+0.5−nμ) / (σ√n) ) P(Sn ≥ k) ≈ 1 − Φ( (k−0.5−nμ) / (σ√n) )
The most commonly tested CLT error. Forgetting the continuity correction when the underlying variable is a discrete count (claim counts, defect counts, number of successes) is one of the single most common point-losing mistakes on this exam. Conversely, the continuity correction should not be applied when the variable being summed is already continuous (e.g., summing continuous claim severities or continuous measurement errors) — there, there is no integer "gap" to correct for.
Example 7.1 — continuous parent, no correction needed

An insurer writes 50 independent policies, each with claim severity having mean 1200 and standard deviation 300. Approximate the probability that total claims exceed 63,000.

Show Solution

E[S] = 50(1200) = 60{,}000. Var(S) = 50(300²) = 4{,}500{,}000, so σS = 2121.32.

Since severities are continuous, no continuity correction is used. Standardize: Z = (63{,}000−60{,}000)/2121.32 = 1.4145.

P(S>63{,}000) = 1−Φ(1.41) = 1−0.9207 = 0.0793.

Example 7.2 — discrete parent, continuity correction required

A block of 200 independent policies each has probability 0.04 of filing a claim this year. Let S be the total number of claims. Approximate P(S ≤ 10).

Show Solution

S is (exactly) Binomial(200, 0.04), with E[S]=8 and Var(S)=200(0.04)(0.96)=7.68, so σS=2.7713.

Because S is a discrete count, apply the continuity correction, using boundary 10.5 in place of 10:

Z = (10.5−8)/2.7713 = 0.9021.

P(S≤10) ≈ Φ(0.90) ≈ 0.8166.

Example 7.3 — a two-sided continuity-corrected probability

Each of 1000 independent policies has probability 0.4 of filing a claim in a given year. Let S be the total number of policies filing a claim. Approximate P(360 ≤ S ≤ 420).

Show Solution

E[S]=1000(0.4)=400. Var(S)=1000(0.4)(0.6)=240, so σS=15.492.

Applying the continuity correction to both boundaries — extend the lower boundary down by 0.5 and the upper boundary up by 0.5:

Lower: Z = (359.5−400)/15.492 = −2.614. Upper: Z = (420.5−400)/15.492 = 1.323.

P(360≤S≤420) ≈ Φ(1.323) − Φ(−2.614) ≈ 0.9071 − 0.0045 = 0.9026.

Example 7.4 — CLT for a sample mean

Independent measurement rounding errors are uniformly distributed on [−0.5, 0.5] (mean 0, variance 1/12). For a sample of 40 independent measurements, approximate P(|average error| < 0.1).

Show Solution

The average has mean 0 and variance (1/12)/40 = 0.0020833, so its standard deviation is 0.045644. No continuity correction is needed — the underlying variable is continuous.

Z = 0.1/0.045644 = 2.1908.

P(|Z|<2.19) = 2Φ(2.19)−1 = 2(0.9857)−1 = 0.9714.

Example 7.5 — normal approximation to a large Poisson total

The total number of claims filed against a large book of business in a year is modeled as Poisson with mean 900. Using the normal approximation with a continuity correction, approximate P(S ≥ 950).

Show Solution

For a Poisson variable, E[S]=Var(S)=900, so σS=30. Since S is a large sum of many small independent Poisson contributions (or, equivalently, a discrete count in its own right), the CLT applies, and because S is discrete and integer-valued, a continuity correction is needed.

Z = (949.5−900)/30 = 49.5/30 = 1.65.

P(S≥950) ≈ 1−Φ(1.65) = 1−0.9505 = 0.0495.

Example 7.6 — comparing two independent sample means

Claim sizes in Region 1 have mean 500 and standard deviation 50; a sample of 100 independent claims is observed there. Claim sizes in Region 2 have mean 480 and standard deviation 60; an independent sample of 120 claims is observed there. Approximate P(X̄ − Ȳ > 30), where X̄ and Ȳ are the two sample means.

Show Solution

By the CLT, each sample mean is approximately normal: X̄ ≈ N(500, 50²/100) = N(500,25) and Ȳ ≈ N(480, 60²/120) = N(480,30), and the two samples are independent of each other.

The difference D = X̄−Ȳ is therefore approximately normal with mean 500−480=20 and variance 25+30=55 (variances add for independent variables — no continuity correction is needed here since sample means are effectively continuous), so σD = √55 = 7.416.

Z = (30−20)/7.416 = 1.348.

P(D>30) = 1−Φ(1.35) = 1−0.9115 = 0.0885.

A note on units. Keep the mean and variance of a linear combination in the same units throughout a calculation — a frequent source of arithmetic slips is mixing a mean expressed in one unit (say, thousands of dollars) with a variance whose square root was taken in a different unit. If a problem states severities "in thousands," it is usually cleanest to carry every mean, variance, and standard deviation through the whole calculation in thousands as well, and only convert back to raw dollars (if at all) in the very last step, once a probability — which is unitless — has already been produced.
Recognizing a CLT problem. Any time an exam problem describes a total or an average across "a large number" of independent, similar risks/measurements/claims — and does not give you an exact closed-form distribution for the total — the CLT is almost certainly the intended tool. Watch especially for phrases like "a large group of policies," "n independent observations," or "the total over the portfolio."
Example 7.7 — seeing why the continuity correction matters

Let X~Binomial(30, 0.5). Approximate P(X≤12) two ways — with and without the continuity correction — and compare both to the exact binomial probability, 0.1808 (obtainable from a binomial table or software).

Show Solution

E[X]=30(0.5)=15, Var(X)=30(0.5)(0.5)=7.5, so σX=2.7386.

Without the continuity correction (incorrectly treating 12 as the exact boundary): Z=(12−15)/2.7386=−1.095, giving Φ(−1.10)=0.1357 — noticeably below the true value.

With the continuity correction (using 12.5 as the boundary, since X≤12 and X≤12.5 describe the same discrete event): Z=(12.5−15)/2.7386=−0.913, giving Φ(−0.91)=0.1814 — within 0.0006 of the exact value.

The continuity-corrected approximation is dramatically more accurate here (error of about 0.0006 versus an error of about 0.045 without it), which is exactly why the correction is worth the extra half-step of arithmetic whenever the underlying variable is a discrete count.

Example 7.8 — CLT as an alternative to an exact Gamma calculation

An insurer's total loss is the sum of 50 independent Exponential(mean 200) severities. Approximate P(S > 11{,}000) using the Central Limit Theorem, and compare this approach to the exact Gamma/Poisson-process method of Chapter 6, Example 6.4.

Show Solution

E[S] = 50(200) = 10{,}000. Var(S) = 50(200²) = 2{,}000{,}000, so σS = 1414.2. Severities are continuous, so no continuity correction is needed.

Z = (11{,}000−10{,}000)/1414.2 = 0.707.

P(S>11{,}000) ≈ 1−Φ(0.71) = 1−0.7611 = 0.2389.

In Chapter 6, a sum of only 4 exponential severities was evaluated exactly using the Gamma distribution's link to a Poisson process, because 4 terms is few enough that an exact calculation was practical. Here, with 50 terms, the CLT offers a much faster route to an equally usable approximate answer — and as n grows, the exact Gamma/Erlang calculation and the CLT approximation converge to the same answer, illustrating concretely why the CLT is trusted as a stand-in for exact sums once n is reasonably large.

Beyond strictly identical distributions. The version of the CLT stated in this chapter assumes the summands are iid, and that is the version tested directly on this exam. It's worth knowing, however, that a more general form of the theorem also holds when a large number of independent but not necessarily identical random variables are added together (as long as no single term dominates the total variance) — the sum is still approximately normal. This is why, for example, Example 6.5's sum of three differently-distributed independent normal variables is exactly normal (a special, non-approximate case), and why an aggregate loss built from many independent policies with varying — but individually modest — means and variances is still routinely treated as approximately normal in practice, even though the policies are not identically distributed.
Chapter 7 wrap-up. Across the examples in this chapter, the same three-step recipe never changed: find the mean and variance of the total or average, decide whether a continuity correction is needed (discrete summand — yes; continuous summand — no), and standardize before consulting the normal table. What varies from problem to problem is only the source of the mean and variance — sometimes given directly, sometimes computed from a named distribution's formulas, sometimes assembled from Chapter 4's covariance machinery when the individual contributions are not mutually independent. Keeping the recipe fixed while treating "find the moments" as its own small sub-problem is the most reliable way to avoid errors under exam time pressure.

Part III Practice Problems

The following 24 problems span all seven chapters of this part, in increasing chapter order, with mixed difficulty. All problems are original to this manual. As a general strategy, before opening the solution, first identify which chapter's tool the problem is testing — a joint pmf table lookup, a conditional-moment calculation, a covariance/variance computation, an order-statistic density, a reproductive-family shortcut, or a CLT approximation — since correctly identifying the tool is often the harder half of the problem. Work each problem before expanding the solution.

Quick-reference formula summary for Part III
Concept Formula
Joint pmf properties (Ch. 1) f(x,y) ≥ 0; ΣΣ f(x,y) = 1 over the support
Joint CDF (Ch. 1) F(x,y) = P(X≤x, Y≤y)
Marginal pmfs (Ch. 2) fX(x) = Σy f(x,y); fY(y) = Σx f(x,y)
Conditional pmf (Ch. 2) f(x|y) = f(x,y) / fY(y)
Independence (Ch. 2) f(x,y) = fX(x)fY(y) for every (x,y)
E[g(X,Y)] (Ch. 3) ΣΣ g(x,y) f(x,y)
Conditional mean/variance (Ch. 3) E[X|Y=y] = Σx x f(x|y); Var(X|Y=y) = E[X²|Y=y] − (E[X|Y=y])²
Law of total expectation (Ch. 3) E[X] = E[ E[X|Y] ]
Law of total variance (Ch. 3) Var(X) = E[Var(X|Y)] + Var(E[X|Y])
Variance of a compound sum (Ch. 3) Var(S) = E[N]Var(X) + (E[X])²Var(N)
Covariance (Ch. 4) Cov(X,Y) = E[XY] − E[X]E[Y]
Variance of a sum (Ch. 4) Var(aX+bY) = a²Var(X)+b²Var(Y)+2ab·Cov(X,Y)
Correlation (Ch. 4) ρ(X,Y) = Cov(X,Y)/(σXσY), −1≤ρ≤1
Max/min CDF (Ch. 5) F(n)(x)=[F(x)]n; F(1)(x)=1−[1−F(x)]n
k-th order statistic pdf (Ch. 5) f(k)(x) = [n!/((k−1)!(n−k)!)][F(x)]k−1[1−F(x)]n−kf(x)
Linear combination, independent (Ch. 6) Var(ΣaiXi) = Σai²Var(Xi)
Reproductive families (Ch. 6) Binomial (same p), Poisson, Normal, Gamma (same scale) closed under independent sums
CLT, standardized (Ch. 7) Z = (Sn − nμ)/(σ√n) ≈ N(0,1)
Continuity correction (Ch. 7) P(Sn≤k) ≈ Φ((k+0.5−nμ)/(σ√n))
1. A joint pmf is given by f(x,y) = k(x+2y) for x = 0,1,2 and y = 0,1. Find k, and then compute P(X ≥ 1, Y = 1).
Show Answer

List x+2y over the six pairs: (0,0)→0, (0,1)→2, (1,0)→1, (1,1)→3, (2,0)→2, (2,1)→4. Sum = 0+2+1+3+2+4=12, so k=1/12.

P(X≥1,Y=1) = f(1,1)+f(2,1) = (3+4)/12 = 7/12 ≈ 0.583.

Answer: 7/12 ≈ 0.583

2. A partial joint pmf table for X (number of auto claims, 0/1/2) and Y (home claim indicator, 0/1) is given below, with one entry missing. You are also told P(X=1)=0.50.
X \ Y 0 1
0 0.15 0.10
1 0.20 ?
2 0.10 0.15
Find the missing entry, and then compute F(1,1) = P(X≤1, Y≤1).
Show Answer

Since fX(1) = f(1,0)+f(1,1) = 0.50, and f(1,0)=0.20, the missing entry is f(1,1) = 0.50−0.20 = 0.30. (Check: all six entries now sum to 1.00. ✓)

F(1,1) = f(0,0)+f(0,1)+f(1,0)+f(1,1) = 0.15+0.10+0.20+0.30 = 0.75.

Answer: missing entry = 0.30; F(1,1) = 0.75

3. The number of storms in a season, X (0,1, or 2), and a property-damage flag, Y (0 or 1), for a coastal town have the joint pmf below.
X \ Y 0 1
0 0.05 0.10
1 0.15 0.20
2 0.25 0.25
Calculate P(X ≥ 1, Y = 1).
Show Answer

P(X≥1,Y=1) = f(1,1)+f(2,1) = 0.20+0.25 = 0.45.

Answer: 0.45

4. Using the joint pmf table of Problem 3, find the marginal pmf of X, the conditional pmf of Y given X=1, and determine whether X and Y are independent.
Show Answer

Marginal: fX(0)=0.15, fX(1)=0.35, fX(2)=0.50.

Conditional on X=1: f(Y=0|X=1)=0.15/0.35=3/7, f(Y=1|X=1)=0.20/0.35=4/7.

Independence check: fY(1)=0.10+0.20+0.25=0.55, so fX(1)·fY(1) = 0.35(0.55)=0.1925, which does not equal f(1,1)=0.20. So X and Y are not independent.

Answer: fX = (0.15, 0.35, 0.50); f(Y|X=1) = (3/7, 4/7); dependent

5. Two independent underwriting decisions are made: X is an indicator that policy A is declined (P(X=1)=0.3), and Y is an indicator that policy B is declined (P(Y=1)=0.6), independent of X. Calculate the probability that exactly one of the two policies is declined.
Show Answer

"Exactly one declined" is (X=1,Y=0) or (X=0,Y=1). By independence, P = P(X=1)P(Y=0) + P(X=0)P(Y=1) = (0.3)(0.4)+(0.7)(0.6) = 0.12+0.42 = 0.54.

Answer: 0.54

6. Let X and Y be independent, each with pmf f(k) = (1/2)k+1 for k=0,1,2,…. Their joint pmf is therefore f(x,y) = (1/2)x+y+2. Calculate P(X=Y).
Show Answer

P(X=Y) = Σk=0∞ f(k,k) = Σk=0∞ (1/2)2k+2 = (1/4)Σk=0∞(1/4)k = (1/4)·[1/(1−1/4)] = (1/4)(4/3) = 1/3.

Answer: 1/3

7. Using the joint pmf table of Problem 3, find E[X|Y=1] and Var(X|Y=1).
Show Answer

fY(1)=0.55. Conditional pmf: f(0|1)=0.10/0.55=2/11, f(1|1)=0.20/0.55=4/11, f(2|1)=0.25/0.55=5/11.

E[X|Y=1] = 0(2/11)+1(4/11)+2(5/11) = 14/11 ≈ 1.2727.

E[X²|Y=1] = 0(2/11)+1(4/11)+4(5/11) = 24/11. Var(X|Y=1) = 24/11 − (14/11)² = (264−196)/121 = 68/121 ≈ 0.562.

Answer: E[X|Y=1] = 14/11 ≈ 1.273; Var(X|Y=1) = 68/121 ≈ 0.562

8. A weather app predicts rain (Y=1) or no rain (Y=0) for a given day; separately, rain actually occurs (X=1) or does not (X=0). The joint pmf is: P(X=0,Y=0)=0.60, P(X=1,Y=0)=0.05, P(X=0,Y=1)=0.10, P(X=1,Y=1)=0.25. Calculate Var(Y|X=1).
Show Answer

P(X=1) = 0.05+0.25 = 0.30. Given X=1: P(Y=1|X=1) = 0.25/0.30 = 5/6 ≈ 0.8333, P(Y=0|X=1)=0.05/0.30=1/6≈0.1667.

Since Y|X=1 is Bernoulli with p=5/6, Var(Y|X=1) = p(1−p) = (5/6)(1/6) = 5/36 ≈ 0.1389.

Answer: ≈ 0.139

9. The number of defective items found during the morning inspection (D) and the afternoon inspection (A) at a small parts factory on a given day are jointly distributed as shown.
D \ A 0 1 2 3 or more
0 0.10 0.08 0.06 0.02
1 0.08 0.15 0.05 0.02
2 0.09 0.05 0.03 0.02
3 or more 0.06 0.07 0.07 0.05
Calculate Var(D | D+A = 2).
Show Answer

Given D+A=2, the three possible outcomes are (D,A)=(0,2), (1,1), (2,0) with (unconditional) probabilities 0.06, 0.15, 0.09, totaling 0.30.

Conditional pmf of D: P(D=0|D+A=2)=0.06/0.30=0.20, P(D=1|·)=0.15/0.30=0.50, P(D=2|·)=0.09/0.30=0.30.

E[D|D+A=2] = 0(0.20)+1(0.50)+2(0.30)=1.10. E[D²|·]=0(0.20)+1(0.50)+4(0.30)=1.70.

Var(D|D+A=2) = 1.70−1.10² = 1.70−1.21 = 0.49.

Answer: 0.49

10. The number of escalated support tickets a call center receives in a day, N, is Poisson with mean 12. Each escalated ticket requires a resolution time (in minutes) that is Exponential with mean 45, independent of N and of every other resolution time. Calculate the variance of the total daily resolution time.
Show Answer

Use the compound-sum variance formula: Var(S) = E[N]Var(X) + (E[X])²Var(N). Here E[N]=Var(N)=12 (Poisson), E[X]=45, Var(X)=45²=2{,}025.

Var(S) = 12(2{,}025) + 45²(12) = 24{,}300+24{,}300 = 48{,}600.

Answer: 48,600

11. Given Var(X)=8, Var(Y)=12, Cov(X,Y)=−3, calculate (a) Var(X−Y), (b) ρ(X,Y), and (c) Var(2X+3Y).
Show Answer

(a) Var(X−Y) = Var(X)+Var(Y)−2Cov(X,Y) = 8+12−2(−3) = 8+12+6 = 26.

(b) ρ(X,Y) = −3/(√8·√12) = −3/√96 = −3/9.798 = −0.306.

(c) Var(2X+3Y) = 4(8)+9(12)+2(2)(3)(−3) = 32+108−36 = 104.

Answer: (a) 26 (b) −0.306 (c) 104

12. A claims adjuster tracks the repair cost, X, and towing cost, Y, for auto claims, with Var(X)=2500, Var(Y)=6000, and Var(X+Y)=10{,}700. The insurer plans to add a flat $100 processing fee to X and increase Y by 50%. Calculate the variance of the revised total, X+100+1.5Y.
Show Answer

First recover the covariance: Var(X+Y) = Var(X)+Var(Y)+2Cov(X,Y), so 10{,}700 = 2500+6000+2Cov(X,Y), giving Cov(X,Y) = (10{,}700−8500)/2 = 1100.

Adding a constant does not change variance, so Var(X+100+1.5Y) = Var(X+1.5Y) = Var(X)+1.5²Var(Y)+2(1)(1.5)Cov(X,Y)

= 2500+2.25(6000)+3(1100) = 2500+13{,}500+3300 = 19{,}300.

Answer: 19,300

13. A company's monthly profit (in $000s) is modeled as Z = 3X + 5Y − 10, where X (revenue from product line A) and Y (revenue from product line B) are independent with Var(X)=6 and Var(Y)=2. Calculate Var(Z).
Show Answer

Since X and Y are independent, the covariance term vanishes: Var(Z) = 3²Var(X)+5²Var(Y) = 9(6)+25(2) = 54+50 = 104.

Answer: 104

14. Independent iid observations X1,…,X6 are drawn from a Uniform(0,10) distribution. Calculate P(X(6) > 8), where X(6) is the sample maximum.
Show Answer

F(x)=x/10, so F(8)=0.8. P(X(6)≤8) = [F(8)]6 = 0.86 = 0.262144.

P(X(6)>8) = 1−0.262144 = 0.737856.

Answer: ≈ 0.738

15. Five identical components, each with lifetime Exponential with mean 100 hours, operate independently as a "hot standby" system — the system fails the instant a component's lifetime falls below a critical threshold, so the relevant quantity is the smallest of the five lifetimes. Calculate the probability the system survives beyond t=15 hours.
Show Answer

The relevant quantity is the minimum of 5 iid Exponential(mean 100) lifetimes, which (by the reproductive property of exponential minima) is Exponential with rate 5(1/100)=1/20, i.e., mean 20.

P(X(1) > 15) = e−15/20 = e−0.75 = 0.4724.

Answer: ≈ 0.472

16. Claim sizes (in thousands) for a certain peril have density f(x) = 3x−4 for x > 1. If 3 such claims occur independently, calculate the expected value of the largest of the three claims.
Show Answer

First find the CDF: F(x) = ∫1x 3t−4dt = 1−x−3 for x≥1.

The density of the maximum of n=3 such claims is g(y) = 3[F(y)]²f(y) = 3(1−y−3)²(3y−4) = 9y−4(1−y−3)².

Expand (1−y−3)² = 1−2y−3+y−6, so y·g(y) = 9[y−3−2y−6+y−9].

Integrating termwise from 1 to ∞ (using ∫1∞y−mdy = 1/(m−1) for m>1): ∫y−3=1/2, ∫y−6=1/5, ∫y−9=1/8.

E[max] = 9[1/2 − 2(1/5) + 1/8] = 9[0.5−0.4+0.125] = 9(0.225) = 2.025 (thousand).

Answer: 2.025 thousand (≈ $2,025)

17. Claims counts from two independent blocks of business are X~Binomial(20,0.25) and Y~Binomial(30,0.25). (a) Identify the distribution of T=X+Y and give its mean and variance. (b) Suppose instead Y~Binomial(30,0.35). Explain why T is no longer Binomial, and find Var(T) directly.
Show Answer

(a) Since both blocks share p=0.25, T~Binomial(50,0.25): E[T]=50(0.25)=12.5, Var(T)=50(0.25)(0.75)=9.375.

(b) With different success probabilities, the trials underlying X and Y are no longer "identical" Bernoulli trials, so their sum does not have the single-parameter Binomial form (the reproductive property requires a common p). However, independence alone still lets variances add termwise: Var(X)=20(0.25)(0.75)=3.75, Var(Y)=30(0.35)(0.65)=6.825, so Var(T)=3.75+6.825=10.575.

Answer: (a) Binomial(50,0.25), mean 12.5, variance 9.375 (b) not Binomial; Var(T) = 10.575

18. Claims counts from three independent territories are Poisson with means 1.5, 2.0, and 0.5. Calculate the probability that the combined claim count across all three territories equals 3.
Show Answer

By the Poisson reproductive property, the total T ~ Poisson(1.5+2.0+0.5) = Poisson(4.0).

P(T=3) = e−44³/3! = 0.0183156 × 64/6 = 0.0183156 × 10.6667 = 0.1954.

Answer: ≈ 0.195

19. Two independent business units have annual profit X~N(200,40²) and Y~N(150,25²) (in $000s). Calculate P(X+Y < 300).
Show Answer

T=X+Y ~ N(350, 40²+25²) = N(350, 2225), so σT = √2225 = 47.170.

Z = (300−350)/47.170 = −1.060.

P(T<300) = Φ(−1.06) = 1−Φ(1.06) = 1−0.8554 = 0.1446.

Answer: ≈ 0.145

20. A retail chain tracks 100 stores. For a given store on a particular day, revenue from in-store sales (G) and from online pickup orders (H), in $000s, has E(G)=50, E(H)=20, Var(G)=20, Var(H)=45, Cov(G,H)=8, with store-level totals mutually independent across stores. Let T be the combined revenue (in-store plus online pickup) across all 100 stores. Approximate P(T < 7135).
Show Answer

Per store, mean revenue = E(G)+E(H) = 50+20=70, and variance = Var(G)+Var(H)+2Cov(G,H) = 20+45+16=81.

Across 100 independent stores: E[T] = 100(70)=7000, Var(T)=100(81)=8100, so σT=90.

By the CLT, Z = (7135−7000)/90 = 1.5, so P(T<7135) ≈ Φ(1.5) = 0.9332.

Answer: ≈ 0.933

21. A regional airline operates 100 flights per day. Each flight independently has a 0.40 probability of experiencing a delay; given a delay occurs, there is a 0.50 probability it causes 1 downstream missed connection versus a 0.50 probability it causes 2 downstream missed connections. All flights behave independently. Using the normal approximation with a continuity correction, approximate the probability the airline has at most 65 total missed connections across all 100 flights on a given day.
Show Answer

For one flight, the number of missed connections generated is 0 with probability 1−0.40=0.60, 1 with probability 0.40(0.50)=0.20, and 2 with probability 0.40(0.50)=0.20.

Mean: 0(0.60)+1(0.20)+2(0.20) = 0.60. Second moment: 0(0.60)+1(0.20)+4(0.20) = 1.00. Variance: 1.00−0.60² = 1.00−0.36 = 0.64.

For 100 independent flights: E[Total]=100(0.60)=60, Var(Total)=100(0.64)=64, so σ=8.

Since the total is a discrete count, apply the continuity correction: Z = (65.5−60)/8 = 0.6875.

P(Total ≤ 65) ≈ Φ(0.69) ≈ 0.754 (interpolating between Φ(0.68)=0.7517 and Φ(0.69)=0.7549).

Answer: ≈ 0.754

22. Claim-processing times have mean 12 minutes and standard deviation 5 minutes (the shape of the underlying distribution is not specified). For a random sample of 64 independent claims, approximate the probability that the average processing time exceeds 13 minutes.
Show Answer

By the CLT, the sample mean is approximately normal with mean 12 and variance 5²/64 = 0.390625, so standard deviation 0.625.

Z = (13−12)/0.625 = 1.6.

P(X̄ > 13) = 1−Φ(1.6) = 1−0.9452 = 0.0548.

Answer: ≈ 0.055

23. An analyst classifies incoming calls into three categories with probabilities 0.40, 0.35, and 0.25, independently from call to call. Out of 6 calls, calculate the probability of exactly 2 calls of the first category, 3 of the second, and 1 of the third.
Show Answer

This is a multinomial joint pmf with n=6, x1=2, x2=3, x3=1:

P = [6!/(2!3!1!)]·(0.40)²(0.35)³(0.25)¹ = 60·(0.16)(0.042875)(0.25).

= 60 × 0.0017150 = 0.1029.

Answer: ≈ 0.103

24. Each of 200 independent policies is, individually, at fault in an accident with probability 0.15. Let p̂ be the observed proportion of the 200 policies at fault. Using the normal approximation (with a continuity correction applied on the count scale), approximate P(p̂ > 0.20).
Show Answer

Let X be the number of at-fault policies, so p̂=X/200 and p̂>0.20 is the same event as X>40, i.e. X≥41 (since X is an integer count).

X is (exactly) Binomial(200, 0.15): E[X]=30, Var(X)=200(0.15)(0.85)=25.5, so σX=5.0498.

Applying the continuity correction (boundary 40.5 in place of 40): Z = (40.5−30)/5.0498 = 2.079.

P(X≥41) ≈ 1−Φ(2.08) = 1−0.9812 = 0.0188.

Answer: ≈ 0.019

Before you move on — a Part III pre-exam checklist. Make sure you can, without hesitation: (1) read any probability off a joint pmf table by summing the correct cells, and find a normalizing constant over a possibly non-rectangular support; (2) produce a marginal pmf by summing out a variable, and a conditional pmf by dividing by the correct marginal; (3) state and apply the laws of total expectation and total variance, especially for a random-count/random-severity ("compound") structure; (4) compute a covariance from E[XY]−E[X]E[Y] and correctly apply the bilinear variance formula, remembering that zero covariance never implies independence; (5) write down the CDF or pdf of a minimum, maximum, or general k-th order statistic without needing to re-derive the combinatorial argument from scratch; (6) recognize which named families are reproductive under independent summation, and under what parameter-matching condition; and (7) apply the Central Limit Theorem with a continuity correction whenever the variable being summed is a discrete count. These seven skills, in combination, account for the large majority of Part III's 23–30% of exam weight.

Appendix A — Formula & Distribution Reference

A single cram sheet for the final days before the exam. Every formula here is derived and explained in full earlier in this manual — use this appendix for quick lookup and last-pass memorization, not as your first introduction to a topic.

A.1 General Probability

Complement RuleP(Ac) = 1 − P(A)
Addition Rule (General)P(A ∪ B) = P(A) + P(B) − P(A ∩ B)
Addition Rule (Mutually Exclusive)P(A ∪ B) = P(A) + P(B)
Inclusion–Exclusion (3 Events)P(A∪B∪C) = P(A)+P(B)+P(C) − P(A∩B) − P(A∩C) − P(B∩C) + P(A∩B∩C)
Conditional ProbabilityP(A | B) = P(A ∩ B) / P(B),   P(B) > 0
Multiplication RuleP(A ∩ B) = P(A | B)·P(B) = P(B | A)·P(A)
IndependenceP(A ∩ B) = P(A)·P(B)  ⟺  P(A|B) = P(A)
Law of Total ProbabilityP(A) = Σᵢ P(A | Bᵢ)·P(Bᵢ), for a partition {B₁,…,Bₙ} of the sample space
Bayes' TheoremP(Bⱼ | A) = [P(A | Bⱼ)·P(Bⱼ)] / Σᵢ P(A | Bᵢ)·P(Bᵢ)
PermutationsₙPᵣ = n! / (n − r)!
CombinationsₙCᵣ = (n choose r) = n! / [r!(n − r)!]
Multinomial Coefficientn! / (n₁! n₂! … nₖ!),   n₁+n₂+…+nₖ = n

Quick Decision Guide: Which Counting Tool?

Order matters? Repetition allowed? Tool
Yes No Permutations, ₙPᵣ
Yes Yes nr (multiplication principle)
No No Combinations, ₙCᵣ
No Yes Stars and bars, (n+r−1 choose r)

A.2 Univariate Random Variables

Expected ValueE[X] = Σₓ x·f(x)  (discrete)  or  E[X] = ∫ x·f(x) dx  (continuous)
Law of the Unconscious StatisticianE[g(X)] = Σₓ g(x)·f(x)  or  ∫ g(x)·f(x) dx
Variance (Shortcut)Var(X) = E[X²] − (E[X])²
Linear TransformationE[aX+b] = aE[X]+b,   Var(aX+b) = a²Var(X)
Coefficient of VariationCV(X) = σ / μ,   μ ≠ 0
Limited Expected Value ("E[X∧u]")E[X ∧ u] = ∫₀ᵘ x f(x)dx + u·P(X>u) = ∫₀ᵘ [1−F(x)] dx
Payment Random Variable (Deductible d, Coinsurance α, Limit u)Y = α · [min(X, u) − min(X, d)] = α · [ (X∧u) − (X∧d) ]

Discrete Distributions

Discrete Distribution Summary
Distribution pmf f(x) E[X] Var(X) Support / Notes
Bernoulli(p) px(1−p)1−x p p(1−p) x ∈ {0,1}
Binomial(n,p) (n choose x)px(1−p)n−x np np(1−p) x = 0,1,…,n; sum of n iid Bernoulli(p)
Geometric(p) — # trials to 1st success (1−p)x−1p 1/p (1−p)/p² x = 1,2,3,…; memoryless
Geometric(p) — # failures before 1st success (1−p)xp (1−p)/p (1−p)/p² x = 0,1,2,…; watch parameterization!
Negative Binomial(r,p) (x−1 choose r−1)pr(1−p)x−r r/p r(1−p)/p² x = r, r+1,…; sum of r iid Geometrics
Hypergeometric(N,K,n) (K choose x)(N−K choose n−x) / (N choose n) n·K/N n(K/N)(1−K/N)[(N−n)/(N−1)] sampling without replacement
Poisson(λ) e−λλx/x! λ λ x = 0,1,2,…; sum of independent Poissons adds λ's
Discrete Uniform{1,…,n} 1/n (n+1)/2 (n²−1)/12 equally likely integers

Continuous Distributions

Continuous Distribution Summary
Distribution pdf f(x) E[X] Var(X) Support / Notes
Uniform(a,b) 1/(b−a) (a+b)/2 (b−a)²/12 a ≤ x ≤ b
Exponential(λ) λe−λx 1/λ 1/λ² x > 0; memoryless; λ = rate
Gamma(α,λ) λαxα−1e−λx/Γ(α) α/λ α/λ² x > 0; sum of α iid Exponential(λ) when α ∈ ℤ⁺
Normal(μ,σ²) (1/√(2πσ²))e−(x−μ)²/2σ² μ σ² x ∈ ℝ; standardize Z=(X−μ)/σ
Beta(a,b) xa−1(1−x)b−1/B(a,b) a/(a+b) ab / [(a+b)²(a+b+1)] 0 ≤ x ≤ 1; models proportions

A.3 Multivariate Random Variables

Marginal pmffX(x) = Σy f(x,y)
Conditional pmff(x|y) = f(x,y) / fY(y)
Independence (Joint)f(x,y) = fX(x)·fY(y) for all x,y
CovarianceCov(X,Y) = E[XY] − E[X]E[Y]
Correlationρ = Cov(X,Y) / (σXσY),   −1 ≤ ρ ≤ 1
Variance of a SumVar(aX+bY) = a²Var(X) + b²Var(Y) + 2ab·Cov(X,Y)
Variance of a Sum (Independent)Var(X₁+X₂+…+Xₙ) = Var(X₁)+Var(X₂)+…+Var(Xₙ)
Law of Total ExpectationE[X] = E[ E[X|Y] ]
Law of Total VarianceVar(X) = E[Var(X|Y)] + Var(E[X|Y])
Max Order Statistic (iid sample, n obs)F(n)(x) = [F(x)]n,   f(n)(x) = n[F(x)]n−1f(x)
Min Order Statistic (iid sample, n obs)F(1)(x) = 1−[1−F(x)]n,   f(1)(x) = n[1−F(x)]n−1f(x)
General Order Statistic pdff(k)(x) = [n! / ((k−1)!(n−k)!)]·[F(x)]k−1[1−F(x)]n−kf(x)
Central Limit Theorem(X̄ − μ) / (σ/√n) → N(0,1) as n → ∞,   equivalently   (ΣXᵢ − nμ)/(σ√n) → N(0,1)
Continuity Correction (discrete → normal approx.)P(X ≤ k) ≈ Φ[(k+0.5−μ)/σ]

Reproductive ("Sum-Stable") Families

If X₁,…,Xₙ are independent… …then the sum is
Binomial(nᵢ, p) — common p Binomial(Σnᵢ, p)
Poisson(λᵢ) Poisson(Σλᵢ)
Normal(μᵢ,σᵢ²) Normal(Σμᵢ, Σσᵢ²)
Gamma(αᵢ, λ) — common λ Gamma(Σαᵢ, λ)
Exponential(λ) — n of them Gamma(n, λ)

A.4 Standard Normal Table (Φ(z))

Selected values of the standard normal CDF Φ(z) = P(Z ≤ z). The full table provided in the actual exam (under the Exhibit button in the CBT interface) covers z = 0.00 to 3.99 in increments of 0.01; the abbreviated table below covers the values most frequently needed for quick reference and sanity-checking your work.
